runs over the nonzero eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{W}
. Thus the covariance problem can be reformulated as a spectral problem for a sparse Hermitian operator on a bipartite graph. This reformulation is particularly natural for diluted Wishart ensembles, because the elementary disorder variables live on the bipartite graph rather than directly on the induced covariance matrix
[
56
,
79
,
70
,
72
]
.

Example 3.3 : The bipartite linearization of a rank-one covariance matrix

Take

\boldsymbol{X}=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptN=2\,,\hskip 22.99988ptP=1\,,\hskip 22.99988ptd=1\,.

(140)

Then

\boldsymbol{W}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{2}&x_{1}x_{2}\\
x_{1}x_{2}&x_{2}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(141)

Since
\boldsymbol{W}
has rank one, its eigenvalues are

\nu_{1}=x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\nu_{2}=0\,.

(142)

Now consider the bipartite linearization

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&x_{1}\\
0&0&x_{2}\\
x_{1}&x_{2}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(143)

A direct multiplication gives

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{2}&x_{1}x_{2}&0\\
x_{1}x_{2}&x_{2}^{2}&0\\
0&0&x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{W}&0\\
0&x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(144)

Hence the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}
are

+\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt-\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt0\,.

(145)

This simple calculation makes explicit the general statement in the text: the nonzero eigenvalues of the bipartite linearization come in opposite pairs, and their squares reproduce the nonzero eigenvalues of the covariance matrix.

Spectral counting observables, which are an integral part of these lecture notes in later sections, are also expressible in terms of the resolvent. For an interval
I=[a,b]\subset\mdmathbb{R}
, define

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(I)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda_{i}\in I}\,,

(146)

as the number of eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{A}
inside the interval
I
. Assume, for this formula, that no eigenvalue lies exactly at
a
or
b
, or adopt the usual boundary convention for endpoint eigenvalues. Using Eq. (
102
), we can express it as

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(I)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\int_{a}^{b}d\lambda\,{\rm Tr}[(\lambda-i\epsilon)\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}]^{-1}\,.

(147)

This observable is the starting point for index statistics and large-deviation theory. In invariant ensembles, the fluctuations of such counting functions can be studied using Coulomb-gas methods and the joint eigenvalue density
[
20
,
42
]
. For sparse and non-invariant matrices, one instead returns to determinant and resolvent representations, introducing biased replicated partition functions that count eigenvalues in a prescribed interval. This strategy leads to large-deviation functions for sparse random graphs and diluted Wishart matrices, and later to conditioned spectral densities
[
48
,
49
,
70
,
71
]
.

For non-Hermitian matrices the eigenvalues are complex, and the resolvent is not sufficient in the same way. Let
\boldsymbol{A}
be a general real or complex
N\times N
matrix with eigenvalues
z_{1},\ldots,z_{N}\in\mdmathbb{C}
. The empirical spectral density in the complex plane is

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta^{(2)}(z-z_{i})\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz=x+iy\,.

(148)

The non-Hermitian analogue of the logarithmic potential is

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})=\frac{1}{N}\log\det\left[(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})(z^{*}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger})+\eta^{2}\boldsymbol{I}\right]\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\eta>0\,.

(149)

Using the identity

\partial_{z^{*}}\frac{1}{z-z_{i}}=\pi\delta^{(2)}(z-z_{i}),

(150)

one obtains the expression

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\partial_{z^{*}}\partial_{z}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})\,,

(151)

or equivalently

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\Delta_{z}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\Delta_{z}=\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{y}^{2}\,.

(152)

This is Girko’s Hermitization principle in the language used in physics and random matrix theory
[
33
,
29
,
84
]
.

Example 3.4 : Hermitization for a
1\times 1
non-Hermitian matrix

Take the
1\times 1
matrix

\boldsymbol{A}=(a)\,,\hskip 22.99988pta\in\mdmathbb C\,.

(153)

Its spectral density should be

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\delta^{(2)}(z-a)\,.

(154)

Let us recover this from the Hermitized logarithmic potential. Since

(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})(z^{*}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger})+\eta^{2}\boldsymbol{I}=|z-a|^{2}+\eta^{2}\,,

(155)

we have

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})=\log\left(|z-a|^{2}+\eta^{2}\right)\,.

(156)

Now

\partial_{z}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})=\frac{z^{*}-a^{*}}{|z-a|^{2}+\eta^{2}}\,,

(157)

and differentiating once more,

\partial_{z^{*}}\partial_{z}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})=\frac{\eta^{2}}{\left(|z-a|^{2}+\eta^{2}\right)^{2}}\,.

(158)

Therefore

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\eta^{2}}{\left(|z-a|^{2}+\eta^{2}\right)^{2}}\,.

(159)

This is a normalized two-dimensional mollifier centered at
a
, and in the limit
\eta\downarrow 0
it converges in the sense of distributions to

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\delta^{(2)}(z-a)\,.

(160)

Thus Hermitization reproduces the correct point spectrum in the simplest non-Hermitian example.

The determinant in (
149
) can again be represented by a Gaussian integral, now after doubling the degrees of freedom. Introduce the
2N\times 2N
Hermitized block matrix

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)=\begin{pmatrix}\eta\boldsymbol{I}&i(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})\\
i(z^{*}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger})&\eta\boldsymbol{I}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(161)

A block-determinant identity gives

\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)=\det\left[(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})(z^{*}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger})+\eta^{2}\boldsymbol{I}\right]\,.

Thus the non-Hermitian spectral density is again obtained from the free energy of a Gaussian theory, but with two coupled fields per vertex. In sparse non-Hermitian ensembles the resulting cavity inverse variances are therefore matrix-valued rather than scalar. This is the basis of the cavity approach to non-Hermitian sparse matrices and of later developments on non-Hermitian number statistics
[
77
,
47
,
75
]
.

The observables introduced in this section form the technical backbone of the notes. The empirical density is recovered from the imaginary part of the trace resolvent; local densities are recovered from diagonal resolvent entries; covariance matrices can be linearized on bipartite graphs; counting observables are integrals of resolvents; and non-Hermitian spectral densities are obtained by Hermitization. The common feature is that all these objects can be written in terms of logarithms of determinants or Gaussian partition functions. Once this translation has been made, sparse random matrix theory becomes a problem in the statistical mechanics of disordered Gaussian models on sparse graphs.

Exercises for this section

Exercise 3.1 : Stieltjes inversion in the sign convention of the notes.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}
be an
N\times N
real symmetric or Hermitian matrix with real eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N}
. Starting from

g_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{z-\lambda_{i}}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz=\lambda-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon>0\,,

(162)

show that

{\rm Im}\frac{1}{\lambda-i\epsilon-\lambda_{i}}=\frac{\epsilon}{(\lambda-\lambda_{i})^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}\,,

(163)

and deduce

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}g_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda-i\epsilon)\,.

(164)

Exercise 3.2 : Normalization of the regularized density.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}
be an
N\times N
real symmetric or Hermitian matrix with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N}
, and define

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A},\epsilon}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\epsilon}{(\lambda-\lambda_{i})^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}\,.

(165)

Show that for every fixed
\epsilon>0
,

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\lambda\rho_{\boldsymbol{A},\epsilon}(\lambda)=1\,.

(166)

Why is this a useful numerical check in later sections?

Exercise 3.3 : Local density of states from eigenvectors.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}
be an
N\times N
real symmetric or Hermitian matrix with normalized eigenvectors
\boldsymbol{u}^{(j)}
and eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}
. Starting from the spectral decomposition of the resolvent,

G_{ii}(z)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{|u_{i}^{(j)}|^{2}}{z-\lambda_{j}}\,,

(167)

derive

\rho_{i}(\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}|u_{i}^{(j)}|^{2}\delta(\lambda-\lambda_{j})\,.

(168)

Then verify explicitly that

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho_{i}(\lambda)\,.

(169)

Exercise 3.4 : Derivative of the logarithmic determinant.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}
be an
N\times N
matrix, let
\boldsymbol{I}
be the
N\times N
identity matrix, and let
z
be such that
z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}
is invertible. Prove the matrix identity

\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\log\det(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})={\rm Tr}(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\,.

(170)

Then use it to recover equation (
118
) from the text.

Exercise 3.5 : The Edwards–Jones Gaussian integral.

Let
z\in\mdmathbb C
with
{\rm Im}\,z<0
. Diagonalize an
N\times N
real symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
and use the one-dimensional Gaussian integral

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dx}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{a}{2}x^{2}}=a^{-1/2}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Re}a>0\,,

(171)

to derive

Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\det\left[i(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})\right]^{-1/2}\,.

(172)

Why is the choice
{\rm Im}\,z<0
convenient for convergence?

Exercise 3.6 : Schur complement and the preview cavity recursion.

Consider a real symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
supported on a tree, and a vertex
j
connected to neighbors in the set
\partial j
. Let
G_{\ell\to j}(z)
denote the diagonal resolvent entry at vertex
\ell
in the branch obtained by removing the edge between
\ell
and
j
. Write the matrix
z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}
in block form with the row and column corresponding to vertex
j
separated from the remaining vertices, and use the Schur complement formula to derive

G_{jj}(z)=\frac{1}{z-A_{jj}-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial j}A_{j\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to j}(z)}\,.

(173)

This reproduces the cavity structure previewed in the section.

Exercise 3.7 : A small exact cavity check.

Take the three-site chain

\boldsymbol{A}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
1&0&1\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(174)

Compute the full resolvent entry
G_{22}(z)
directly from
(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}
. Then compute it again using the Schur-complement or cavity recursion of Exercise 3.6. Verify that the two answers agree exactly.

Exercise 3.8 : Bipartite linearization.

Let
\boldsymbol{X}
be a real
N\times P
matrix, let
d>0
, and define

\boldsymbol{W}=\frac{1}{d}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{0}_{N\times N}&\boldsymbol{X}\\
\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}&\boldsymbol{0}_{P\times P}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(175)

Show that

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}^{2}=\frac{1}{d}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}&0\\
0&\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{X}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(176)

Deduce that the nonzero eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}
are
\pm\sqrt{\nu}
, where
\nu
runs over the nonzero eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{W}
.

Exercise 3.9 : Interval counts from the resolvent.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}
be an
N\times N
real symmetric or Hermitian matrix with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N}
, and assume that no eigenvalue lies exactly at
a
or
b
. Starting from

{\rm Tr}(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{z-\lambda_{i}}\,,

(177)

show that

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}([a,b])=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\int_{a}^{b}d\lambda{\rm Tr}\left[(\lambda-i\epsilon)\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}\right]^{-1}\,.

(178)

Explain why this formula is the natural precursor of the index-number large-deviation theory.

Exercise 3.10 : A direct check of Hermitization.

For the scalar non-Hermitian matrix
\boldsymbol{A}=(a)
, with
a\in\mdmathbb C
, derive explicitly

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\eta^{2}}{\left(|z-a|^{2}+\eta^{2}\right)^{2}}\,,

(179)

starting from the logarithmic potential. Then verify directly that

\int_{\mdmathbb C}d^{2}z\rho_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z)=1\,.

(180)

Exercise 3.11 : Programming exercise: diagonalization versus resolvent.

Fix a mean degree
c=O(1)
, a list of system sizes
N
, a number
S
of independent samples for each
N
, a regulator
\epsilon>0
, and a grid of real values of
\lambda
. For each value of
N
and each sample, generate an
N\times N
real symmetric sparse Erdős–Rényi adjacency matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
with
A_{ii}=0
,
A_{ij}=A_{ji}
, and

{\rm Prob}(A_{ij}=1)=\frac{c}{N}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Prob}(A_{ij}=0)=1-\frac{c}{N}\,,\hskip 22.99988pti<j\,.

(181)

For that sample:

–

diagonalize the matrix and build the broadened density from the eigenvalues,

\rho_{\epsilon}^{\rm diag}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\epsilon}{(\lambda-\lambda_{i})^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}\,,

(182)

and

–

compute the trace of the resolvent on a grid of
\lambda
values and build

\rho_{\epsilon}^{\rm res}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}{\rm Im}\frac{1}{N}{\rm Tr}\left[(\lambda-i\epsilon)\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}\right]^{-1}\,.

(183)

Verify numerically, on the same
\lambda
grid and at the same value of
\epsilon
, that the two procedures agree. Report the values of
c
,
N
,
S
,
\epsilon
, the grid spacing, and a simple discrepancy measure such as the maximum absolute difference between
\rho_{\epsilon}^{\rm diag}
and
\rho_{\epsilon}^{\rm res}
on the grid.

4
Replica and cavity methods for disordered systems

The previous section showed that spectral observables of sparse matrices can be represented through logarithms of Gaussian partition functions. This places the spectral problem in the same formal class as the computation of quenched free energies in disordered statistical mechanics. The purpose of this section is to recall the two main tools that will be used throughout these notes: the replica method and the cavity method. For the locally tree-like finite-connectivity systems that will concern us, these two approaches are closely related and, at the replica-symmetric level, often lead to the same self-consistency equations. In that sense, choosing one or the other is sometimes a matter of taste. Their practical use, however, is not equally transparent in all problems: the cavity method is often more natural when the graph structure directly suggests recursive local equations and message-passing algorithms, whereas an equivalent replica derivation may be considerably more cumbersome. Conversely, replica methods can be more convenient when one wants direct access to disorder-averaged free energies or generating functions. We therefore introduce both viewpoints, first in the more familiar setting of spin systems on sparse random graphs, and then explain how the same logic applies to Gaussian models generated by resolvent and determinant representations.

The historical origin of these ideas lies in the statistical mechanics of disordered systems. In a spin glass, the interactions are random and remain fixed during the thermal equilibration of the spins. The disorder is therefore quenched. The Edwards–Anderson model introduced this viewpoint for finite-dimensional spin glasses
[
25
]
, while the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model provided a model with infinite-range random couplings for which mean field theory became exact
[
81
]
. The replica method became the central analytic tool for this class of problems, and a systematic and historical development can be reviewed in
[
51
]
. For finite-connectivity systems, such as diluted spin glasses and spin models on random graphs, the relevant mean-field geometry is not the complete graph but a locally tree-like sparse graph. The Viana–Bray model is the canonical diluted spin-glass example
[
85
]
, and the cavity method gives the natural description of such finite-connectivity systems
[
52
,
50
]
.

To introduce these ideas, we will use one of the most important models in physics, the Ising model, but now placed on a simple undirected graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
, where
V=\{1,\ldots,N\}
is the set of vertices and
E
is a set of unordered edges. The spin variables are
\sigma_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
, and the Hamiltonian is

H_{\mathcal{G},\boldsymbol{J},\boldsymbol{B}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})=-\sum_{(i,j)\in E}J_{ij}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}B_{i}\sigma_{i}\,.

(184)

Here the graph
\mathcal{G}
, the couplings
\boldsymbol{J}=\{J_{ij}\}
, and possibly the fields
\boldsymbol{B}=\{B_{i}\}
are all considered quenched random variables. For this fixed disorder, the partition function is

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{G},\boldsymbol{J},\boldsymbol{B}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{N}}\exp\left[-\beta H_{\mathcal{G},\boldsymbol{J},\boldsymbol{B}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right]\,,

(185)

and the corresponding free energy is

-\beta F_{\mathcal{G},\boldsymbol{J},\boldsymbol{B}}=\log\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{G},\boldsymbol{J},\boldsymbol{B}}\,.

(186)

The thermodynamic quantity of interest is usually not the free energy of a particular realization, but its typical value. In the thermodynamic limit this is encoded by the quenched free-energy density

-\beta f=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\overline{\log\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{G},\boldsymbol{J},\boldsymbol{B}}}\,,

(187)

where the overbar denotes the average over the graph and coupling disorder, and
f
is the limiting disorder-averaged free-energy density. The central difficulty is the logarithm. An annealed calculation would average
\mathcal{Z}
before taking the logarithm, but the quenched problem requires the average of
\log\mathcal{Z}
. This distinction is the statistical-mechanics analogue of the distinction, in sparse random matrix theory, between averaging a determinant and averaging the logarithm of a determinant.

Example 4.1 : Annealed versus quenched Edwards–Jones at small connectivity

It is useful to see explicitly why replacing a quenched average by an annealed one is not an innocent operation in sparse spectral problems. We illustrate this in the simplest possible setting: the adjacency matrix of an Erdős–Rényi graph in the low-connectivity expansion.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}
be the adjacency matrix of an Erdős–Rényi graph with edge probability
c/N
, and consider the Edwards–Jones partition function

Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\int\left[\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{du_{i}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm T}(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})\boldsymbol{u}\right]\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz=\lambda-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon>0\,.

(188)

For the empty graph,
\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{0}
, the partition function is

Z_{0}(z)=(iz)^{-N/2}\,.

(189)

Now add a single edge between two vertices. The only nontrivial block is

\boldsymbol{A}_{\rm edge}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(190)

and the ratio between the two-site edge partition function and two isolated one-site partition functions is

r(z)=\frac{\det[i(z\boldsymbol{I}_{2}-\boldsymbol{A}_{\rm edge})]^{-1/2}}{(iz)^{-1}}=\left(1-\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)^{-1/2}\,,

(191)

up to a
z
-independent phase, which does not affect the spectral density.

At small
c
, the graph is, to first order in
c
, a collection of isolated vertices and isolated edges. Components with two or more adjacent edges contribute only at order
c^{2}
. Hence the quenched free energy per site is

\frac{1}{N}\overline{\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)}=-\frac{1}{2}\log(iz)+\frac{c}{2}\log r(z)+O(c^{2})\,.

(192)

Using (
191
), this becomes

\frac{1}{N}\overline{\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)}=-\frac{1}{2}\log(iz)-\frac{c}{4}\log\left(1-\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)+O(c^{2})\,.

(193)

The corresponding quenched resolvent is

g_{\rm q}(z)=-2\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\left[\frac{1}{N}\overline{\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)}\right]\,,

(194)

and therefore

g_{\rm q}(z)=\frac{1}{z}+\frac{c}{z(z^{2}-1)}+O(c^{2})\,.

(195)

Equivalently,

g_{\rm q}(z)=\frac{1-c}{z}+\frac{c}{2}\left(\frac{1}{z-1}+\frac{1}{z+1}\right)+O(c^{2})\,.

(196)

This is exactly the physical low-connectivity picture: with probability
1-c+O(c^{2})
a uniformly chosen vertex is isolated and contributes an eigenvalue at zero, while with probability
c+O(c^{2})
it belongs to an isolated edge, whose two eigenvalues are
+1
and
-1
. Thus

\rho_{\rm q}(\lambda)=(1-c)\delta(\lambda)+\frac{c}{2}\delta(\lambda-1)+\frac{c}{2}\delta(\lambda+1)+O(c^{2})\,.

(197)

Now repeat the calculation in the annealed approximation. Instead of computing

\overline{\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)}
, one computes
\log\overline{Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)}
. To first order in
c
, each possible edge contributes the factor
r(z)-1
, and there are asymptotically
N^{2}/2
possible unordered pairs, each present with probability
c/N
. Hence

\overline{Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)}=Z_{0}(z)\exp\left[\frac{cN}{2}\big(r(z)-1\big)+O(c^{2}N)\right]\,,

(198)

so that

\frac{1}{N}\log\overline{Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)}=-\frac{1}{2}\log(iz)+\frac{c}{2}\left[\left(1-\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)^{-1/2}-1\right]+O(c^{2})\,.

(199)

The corresponding annealed resolvent is

g_{\rm a}(z)=-2\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\left[\frac{1}{N}\log\overline{Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)}\right]\,,

(200)

and therefore

g_{\rm a}(z)=\frac{1}{z}+\frac{c}{z^{3}}\left(1-\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)^{-3/2}+O(c^{2})\,.

(201)

This differs from (
195
). In particular, the quenched expression has simple poles at
z=0,\pm 1
, reflecting isolated vertices and isolated edges, whereas the annealed expression has a branch singularity inherited from the averaged partition function. Thus, already at order
c
, the annealed Edwards–Jones calculation does not reproduce the finite-connectivity spectral density.

The lesson is not that annealed calculations are never useful. In dense Gaussian ensembles, and in some high-temperature disordered systems, annealed and quenched free energies may agree at leading order. The lesson is more precise: in sparse spectral problems, annealing may wash out the local component structure that is visible to the quenched logarithm. The quenched average sees the logarithm of the component contribution; the annealed average sees the component contribution before taking the logarithm. These two operations are not equivalent.

The replica method addresses the logarithm through the identity

\log\mathcal{Z}=\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{\mathcal{Z}^{n}-1}{n}\,.

(202)

In thermodynamic calculations one usually implements this in the form

-\beta f=\lim_{n\to 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{nN}\log\overline{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{G},\boldsymbol{J},\boldsymbol{B}}^{n}}\,,

(203)

where the interchange of the replica and thermodynamic limits is a formal step of the method. In these notes we shall use this formula as an organizing device, without entering into the mathematical problem of justifying this interchange.

For positive integer
n
, the quantity
\mathcal{Z}^{n}
is the partition function of
n
identical copies, or replicas, of the original system. The replicated spins are

\underline{\sigma}_{i}=(\sigma_{i}^{1},\ldots,\sigma_{i}^{n})\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\sigma_{i}^{a}\in\{-1,1\}\,,\hskip 22.99988pta=1,\ldots,n\,.

(204)

The disorder average couples the replicas, even though the replicas were independent before the average is performed. For an Erdős–Rényi graph with mean degree
c
, independent couplings drawn from a distribution
p_{J}(J)
, and fixed external fields
B_{i}
, one obtains schematically

\overline{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{G},\boldsymbol{J},\boldsymbol{B}}^{n}}=\sum_{\{\sigma_{i}^{a}\}}\exp\left[\frac{c}{2N}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\left(\int dJ\,p_{J}(J)\exp\left[\beta J\sum_{a=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}^{a}\sigma_{j}^{a}\right]-1\right)+\beta\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{a=1}^{n}B_{i}\sigma_{i}^{a}\right]\,,

(205)

where terms subleading in
N
have been ignored as they are not relevant for the typical properties of the system in the thermodynamic limit. This expression illustrates the characteristic structure of finite-connectivity replica calculations. The effective interaction after disorder averaging depends on the whole replicated spin vector at each site, not only on a small number of scalar order parameters.

The natural order parameter is therefore the empirical distribution of replicated spin vectors,

P(\underline{\sigma})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\underline{\sigma},\underline{\sigma}_{i}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\sum_{\underline{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}P(\underline{\sigma})=1\,.

(206)

Example 4.2 : Why the finite-connectivity order parameter is a distribution

For
n=2
replicas, the replicated spin at one site can take four values,

(\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2})\in\{(++),(+-),(-+),(--)\}\,.

(207)

Let us write

P_{++}=P(+,+)\,,\hskip 22.99988ptP_{+-}=P(+,-)\,,\hskip 22.99988ptP_{-+}=P(-,+)\,,\hskip 22.99988ptP_{--}=P(-,-)\,,

(208)

with

P_{++}+P_{+-}+P_{-+}+P_{--}=1\,.

(209)

From this distribution one can reconstruct the replica magnetizations and the overlap:

\displaystyle m_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2}}P(\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2})\sigma^{1}=P_{++}+P_{+-}-P_{-+}-P_{--}\,,

(210)

\displaystyle m_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2}}P(\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2})\sigma^{2}=P_{++}-P_{+-}+P_{-+}-P_{--}\,,

(211)

\displaystyle q_{12}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2}}P(\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2})\sigma^{1}\sigma^{2}=P_{++}+P_{--}-P_{+-}-P_{-+}\,.

(212)

Thus the overlap is only one moment of the full distribution
P(\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2})
.

To see that
P
contains more information than
q_{12}
alone, consider the two distributions

P^{(A)}_{++}=\frac{1}{2}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptP^{(A)}_{--}=\frac{1}{2}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptP^{(A)}_{+-}=P^{(A)}_{-+}=0\,,

(213)

and

P^{(B)}_{++}=1\,,\hskip 22.99988ptP^{(B)}_{+-}=P^{(B)}_{-+}=P^{(B)}_{--}=0\,.

(214)

In both cases

q_{12}=1\,,

(215)

but the magnetizations are different:

m_{1}^{(A)}=m_{2}^{(A)}=0\,,\hskip 22.99988ptm_{1}^{(B)}=m_{2}^{(B)}=1\,.

(216)

This simple calculation shows why, in finite-connectivity systems, the natural order parameter is the whole empirical distribution of replicated local configurations rather than only the overlap matrix.

In fully connected spin glasses the standard replica order parameter is the overlap matrix
q_{ab}=N^{-1}\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}^{a}\sigma_{i}^{b}
. In finite-connectivity systems the correct order parameter is, however, richer: it is a probability distribution over replicated local configurations
P(\underline{\sigma})
. This is the replica signature of the fact that vertices have different local neighborhoods and therefore experience different effective fields. After introducing the order parameter function (
206
), the replicated partition function takes a large-deviation form

\overline{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{G},\boldsymbol{J},\boldsymbol{B}}^{n}}=\int\mathcal{D}P\exp\left[N{}_{n}[P]\right]\,,

(217)

and the thermodynamic limit is evaluated by the saddle-point method,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\overline{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{G},\boldsymbol{J},\boldsymbol{B}}^{n}}=\operatorname*{extr}_{P}{}_{n}[P]\,.

(218)

The analytic continuation
n\to 0
is the formal step of the replica method. It is powerful, but it is also the step at which care is needed: one makes an ansatz about how the system of replicas behaves under permutations of the replicas, computes
{}_{n}[P]
for integer
n
under this ansatz, rewrites the result in a form suitable for analytic continuation, and finally evaluates the
n\to 0
limit.

Within the replica-symmetric (RS) ansatz, the order parameter (
206
) is assumed to be invariant under permutations of replicas. For finite-connectivity Ising systems the order parameter function
P(\underline{\sigma})
then takes the form

P_{\rm RS}(\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{n})=\int dh\,W(h)\prod_{a=1}^{n}\frac{e^{\beta h\sigma^{a}}}{2\cosh(\beta h)}\,,

(219)

where
W(h)
is the mixing distribution of effective fields in the replica-symmetric order parameter. Thus the replica-symmetric order parameter is not a single magnetization or overlap, but a full distribution of fields. In the cavity interpretation below, this distribution is identified with the ensemble law of a typical directed-edge cavity field. This is the point where the replica and cavity descriptions meet. Appendix
D
gives an explicit replica-symmetric saddle-point derivation showing how the corresponding cavity-message distribution appears from the replicated disorder average.

The cavity method starts from the observation that sparse random graphs are locally tree-like. If the edge
(i,j)
is removed, the branches attached to
i
through the neighbors
k\in\partial i\setminus j
are disconnected from the rest of the graph through
j
; on a tree, these branches are independent once
\sigma_{i}
is fixed. One therefore introduces a cavity marginal
{}_{i\to j}(\sigma_{i})
which corresponds to the marginal distribution of
\sigma_{i}
in the graph where the edge between
i
and
j
has been deleted. On a tree these cavity marginals obey the exact recursion

{}_{i\to j}(\sigma_{i})=\frac{1}{Z_{i\to j}}e^{\beta B_{i}\sigma_{i}}\prod_{k\in\partial i\setminus j}\left[\sum_{\sigma_{k}=\pm 1}e^{\beta J_{ik}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{k}}{}_{k\to i}(\sigma_{k})\right]\,,

(220)

where
\partial i
is the set of neighbors of
i
, and
Z_{i\to j}
is a normalization factor. This is the belief-propagation equation for the Ising model on a graph. Belief propagation was developed in probability and information theory as a message-passing algorithm on graphical models
[
67
,
38
]
, while in statistical mechanics its fixed points are the stationary points of the Bethe free energy
[
10
,
92
]
. On a tree the equations are exact. On a sparse random graph they are asymptotically exact for local observables in regimes where the locally tree-like approximation and the assumed pure-state structure are valid.

It is often convenient to parametrize the binary cavity marginals in terms of a cavity field,

{}_{i\to j}(\sigma_{i})=\frac{e^{\beta h_{i\to j}\sigma_{i}}}{2\cosh(\beta h_{i\to j})}\,.

(221)

Substituting (
221
) into (
220
) gives the following set of self-consistency equations known as cavity equations

h_{i\to j}=B_{i}+\sum_{k\in\partial i\setminus j}u(J_{ik},h_{k\to i})\,,

(222)

with
u(J,h)
being the propagating field

u(J,h)=\frac{1}{\beta}\operatorname{arctanh}\left[\tanh(\beta J)\tanh(\beta h)\right]\,.

(223)

From the cavity marginals one can reconstruct the physical single-site marginal
P_{i}(\sigma_{i})
. It can be parametrized in the same way by a full effective, or physical, field
h_{i}
, given by

h_{i}=B_{i}+\sum_{k\in\partial i}u(J_{ik},h_{k\to i})\,.

(224)

Local observables then follow from the corresponding single-site marginalization. For example,

\langle\sigma_{i}\rangle=\tanh(\beta h_{i})\,.

(225)

Example 4.3 : Cavity fields on a three-spin chain

Consider the Ising chain

1\!-\!2\!-\!3

(226)

with Hamiltonian

H(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})=-J_{12}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}-J_{23}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}-B_{1}\sigma_{1}-B_{2}\sigma_{2}-B_{3}\sigma_{3}\,.

(227)

Since vertex
3
is a leaf, the cavity marginal sent from
3
to
2
is simply

{}_{3\to 2}(\sigma_{3})\propto e^{\beta B_{3}\sigma_{3}}\,.

(228)

Comparing with (
221
), we read off

h_{3\to 2}=B_{3}\,.

(229)

Now let us compute the cavity marginal sent from
2
to
1
. By definition,

{}_{2\to 1}(\sigma_{2})\propto e^{\beta B_{2}\sigma_{2}}\sum_{\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}e^{\beta J_{23}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}}{}_{3\to 2}(\sigma_{3})\,.

(230)

Using (
228
),

{}_{2\to 1}(\sigma_{2})\propto e^{\beta B_{2}\sigma_{2}}\sum_{\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}e^{\beta(J_{23}\sigma_{2}+B_{3})\sigma_{3}}=e^{\beta B_{2}\sigma_{2}}2\cosh\left[\beta(J_{23}\sigma_{2}+B_{3})\right]\,.

(231)

To identify the corresponding cavity field, take the ratio

\frac{{}_{2\to 1}(+1)}{{}_{2\to 1}(-1)}=\exp\left(2\beta h_{2\to 1}\right)\,.

(232)

From (
231
),

\exp\left(2\beta h_{2\to 1}\right)=e^{2\beta B_{2}}\frac{\cosh[\beta(B_{3}+J_{23})]}{\cosh[\beta(B_{3}-J_{23})]}\,.

(233)

Hence

h_{2\to 1}=B_{2}+\frac{1}{2\beta}\log\frac{\cosh[\beta(B_{3}+J_{23})]}{\cosh[\beta(B_{3}-J_{23})]}\,.

(234)

Using the identity

\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\cosh(x+y)}{\cosh(x-y)}=\operatorname{arctanh}\!\big(\tanh x\,\tanh y\big)\,,

(235)

we recover the cavity-field recursion

h_{2\to 1}=B_{2}+u(J_{23},B_{3})\,,\hskip 22.99988ptu(J,h)=\frac{1}{\beta}\operatorname{arctanh}\!\big[\tanh(\beta J)\tanh(\beta h)\big]\,.

(236)

The full effective field at the middle spin is obtained by including both neighbors:

h_{2}=B_{2}+u(J_{12},B_{1})+u(J_{23},B_{3})\,,

(237)

and therefore

\langle\sigma_{2}\rangle=\tanh(\beta h_{2})\,.

(238)

This is the simplest explicit derivation of the belief-propagation/cavity recursion on a tree.

At the ensemble level one describes the statistics of a typical cavity field. For a finite undirected graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
, let
\overrightarrow{E}=\{(i,j),(j,i):\{i,j\}\in E\}
be the set of oriented edges obtained by giving both orientations to every undirected edge. A cavity fixed point assigns one field
h_{i\to j}
to each
(i,j)\in\overrightarrow{E}
, namely the field sent from
i
to
j
in the graph where the edge between
i
and
j
has been removed. The empirical cavity-field law on this finite graph is therefore

W_{N}^{\rm cav}(h)=\frac{1}{|\overrightarrow{E}|}\sum_{(i,j)\in\overrightarrow{E}}\delta(h-h_{i\to j})\,.

When this empirical directed-edge law has a deterministic large-
N
limit, we denote it by
W(h)
. For an Erdős–Rényi graph with mean degree
c
, independent couplings with distribution
p_{J}(J)
, and a fixed external field
B
, this limiting cavity-field law obeys

W(h)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}e^{-c}\frac{c^{\ell}}{\ell!}\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}dh_{r}\,W(h_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\left(h-B-\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}u(J_{r},h_{r})\right)\,.

(239)

For a general degree distribution, the Poisson law that appears in Eq. (
239
) is replaced by the appropriate excess-degree law. This distinction is crucial in sparse systems: a cavity message is attached to an oriented edge, and therefore its incoming neighborhood is distributed according to the degree distribution seen from a randomly chosen edge. This cavity-field law is distinct from the law of full site fields
h_{i}
, which include all neighbors of a vertex and are used to compute physical single-site observables. In random regular graphs, the cavity recursion has a fixed number
c-1
of incoming messages; in heterogeneous networks, it involves the excess-degree distribution, equivalently the size-biased degree distribution shifted by one.

The Bethe free energy associated with a fixed point of (
220
) is

-\beta F_{\rm Bethe}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log Z_{i}-\sum_{(i,j)\in E}\log Z_{ij}\,,

(240)

where

Z_{i}=\sum_{\sigma_{i}=\pm 1}e^{\beta B_{i}\sigma_{i}}\left[\prod_{k\in\partial i}\sum_{\sigma_{k}=\pm 1}e^{\beta J_{ik}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{k}}{}_{k\to i}(\sigma_{k})\right]\,,

(241)

and

Z_{ij}=\sum_{\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j}=\pm 1}e^{\beta J_{ij}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}{}_{i\to j}(\sigma_{i}){}_{j\to i}(\sigma_{j})\,.

(242)

The first term in (
240
) sums the free-energy contributions of vertices, while the second subtracts the edge contributions that have been counted twice. On a tree this expression is exact. On a sparse random graph it is the finite-connectivity mean-field approximation associated with the locally tree-like structure.

The connection with random matrices is now direct. The Edwards–Jones representation rewrites the resolvent problem as a Gaussian partition function with quenched graph and weight disorder
[
26
]
. For a symmetric diluted matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
, the Gaussian measure associated with the spectral parameter
z=\lambda-i\epsilon
has the form

Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\int\left[\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{dx_{i}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}x_{i}(z\delta_{ij}-A_{ij})x_{j}\right]\,.

(243)

This is a continuous-spin model with complex quadratic interactions. The role played by the Ising free energy is now played by
\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)
, and the disorder average of the spectral density involves the quenched average of this logarithm. Thus the same formal obstruction appears:

\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)}=-\frac{2}{\pi N}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\overline{\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda-i\epsilon)}\,.

(244)

The replica method would compute this quantity by introducing
n
copies of the Gaussian variables, averaging
Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}^{n}
, and taking
n\to 0
. This was the strategy initiated in the random-matrix context by Edwards and Jones and later applied to sparse random matrices by Rodgers and Bray
[
26
,
76
]
. The finite-connectivity nature of the problem again forces the order parameter to be a distribution, now of Gaussian variances or inverse variances rather than of Ising cavity fields.

The cavity method gives the same equations more directly. Because the model (
243
) is Gaussian, all cavity marginals remain Gaussian. We may therefore write

P_{i\to j}(x_{i})\propto\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{i\to j}x_{i}^{2}\right]\,,

(245)

with complex inverse variance
\omega_{i\to j}
. The proportionality constant is fixed by normalization once a branch of the complex Gaussian integral is chosen. For the derivation of the cavity recursion, only the quadratic coefficient is needed. For a locally tree-like graph, integrating over the neighboring cavity variables gives

\omega_{i\to j}=i(z-A_{ii})+\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\frac{A_{i\ell}^{2}}{\omega_{\ell\to i}}\,.

(246)

The corresponding full inverse variance is

\omega_{i}=i(z-A_{ii})+\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}\frac{A_{i\ell}^{2}}{\omega_{\ell\to i}}\,,

(247)

and the diagonal resolvent is obtained as

G_{ii}(z)=\frac{i}{\omega_{i}}\,.

(248)

Consequently,

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi N}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{i}{\omega_{i}(\lambda-i\epsilon)}\,.

(249)

Equations (
246
)–(
249
) are the spectral analogue of the Ising cavity equations (
222
)–(
224
). In the Ising model the message is a cavity field; in the Gaussian spectral problem the message is a complex inverse variance. The simplification is substantial: instead of an arbitrary function on a continuous variable, the Gaussian ansatz closes the cavity equations on a single complex parameter per directed edge.

Example 4.4 : The Gaussian cavity recursion on a three-site chain

Consider the real symmetric matrix

\boldsymbol{A}=\begin{pmatrix}D_{1}&J_{12}&0\\
J_{12}&D_{2}&J_{23}\\
0&J_{23}&D_{3}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(250)

which corresponds to the three-site chain
1\!-\!2\!-\!3
. Let

z=\lambda-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon>0\,.

(251)

Since sites
1
and
3
are leaves, their cavity marginals are obtained without integrating over any neighbor:

P_{1\to 2}(x_{1})\propto\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}(z-D_{1})x_{1}^{2}\right]\,,\hskip 22.99988ptP_{3\to 2}(x_{3})\propto\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}(z-D_{3})x_{3}^{2}\right]\,.

(252)

Thus

\omega_{1\to 2}=i(z-D_{1})\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\omega_{3\to 2}=i(z-D_{3})\,.

(253)

Now compute the full inverse variance at the middle site. By (
247
),

\omega_{2}=i(z-D_{2})+\frac{J_{12}^{2}}{\omega_{1\to 2}}+\frac{J_{23}^{2}}{\omega_{3\to 2}}\,.

(254)

Hence the diagonal resolvent at site
2
is

G_{22}(z)=\frac{i}{\omega_{2}}=\frac{i}{i(z-D_{2})+\displaystyle\frac{J_{12}^{2}}{i(z-D_{1})}+\frac{J_{23}^{2}}{i(z-D_{3})}}\,.

(255)

Multiplying numerator and denominator by
-i
, this can be written as

G_{22}(z)=\frac{1}{z-D_{2}-\displaystyle\frac{J_{12}^{2}}{z-D_{1}}-\frac{J_{23}^{2}}{z-D_{3}}}\,.

(256)

This is exactly the Schur-complement formula for the central diagonal entry of
(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}
.

As a simple check, take

D_{1}=D_{2}=D_{3}=0\,,\hskip 22.99988ptJ_{12}=J_{23}=1\,.

(257)

Then

G_{22}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\frac{1}{z}-\frac{1}{z}}=\frac{z}{z^{2}-2}\,.

(258)

Direct inversion of

z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}=\begin{pmatrix}z&-1&0\\
-1&z&-1\\
0&-1&z\end{pmatrix}

(259)

gives the same result for the
(2,2)
entry. This explicit example shows why the Gaussian cavity message is naturally parametrized by a single inverse variance.

For an ensemble of sparse matrices, one can pass from the fixed-graph cavity equations to an equation for the statistics of a typical message. For a finite undirected graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
, let
\overrightarrow{E}=\{(i,j),(j,i):\{i,j\}\in E\}
be the set of oriented edges. If a Gaussian cavity fixed point assigns an inverse variance
\omega_{i\to j}
to each
(i,j)\in\overrightarrow{E}
, its empirical directed-edge law is

\pi_{N}^{\rm cav}(\omega)=\frac{1}{|\overrightarrow{E}|}\sum_{(i,j)\in\overrightarrow{E}}\delta(\omega-\omega_{i\to j})\,.

The corresponding empirical law of full inverse variances is

\pi_{N,{\rm full}}(\omega)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta(\omega-\omega_{i})\,,

where
\omega_{i}
is obtained by including all neighbors of
i
. When these empirical laws have deterministic large-
N
limits, we denote them by
\pi(\omega)
and
\pi_{\rm full}(\omega)
. In the Erdős–Rényi case, suppressing diagonal disorder and using independent edge weights with distribution
p_{J}(J)
, the limiting cavity law satisfies

\pi(\omega)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}e^{-c}\frac{c^{\ell}}{\ell!}\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}d\omega_{r}\pi(\omega_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\left(\omega-iz-\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}\frac{J_{r}^{2}}{\omega_{r}}\right)\,.

(260)

The limiting law of full inverse variances is then

\pi_{\rm full}(\omega)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-c}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}d\omega_{r}\pi(\omega_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\left(\omega-iz-\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{J_{r}^{2}}{\omega_{r}}\right)\,,

(261)

and the ensemble-averaged spectral density follows from

\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)}=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\int d\omega\pi_{\rm full}(\omega)\frac{i}{\omega}\,.

(262)

For Erdős–Rényi graphs the cavity and full degree distributions are both Poisson with mean
c
, while for a general graph ensemble the corresponding excess-degree and degree distributions differ. This replacement is one of the simplest ways in which graph topology enters the spectral density.

The cavity equations for sparse spectral problems have been developed in several equivalent forms. The replica approach to sparse random matrices was introduced by Rodgers and Bray
[
76
]
; later work clarified the relation with localized states and finite-connectivity effects
[
39
]
. The Gaussian cavity method gives a direct and operational formulation for sparse symmetric matrices and sparse covariance matrices
[
79
]
. A detailed pedagogical account of the equivalence between the cavity and replica approaches for sparse symmetric random matrices is given in
[
83
]
. The same logic extends to non-Hermitian sparse matrices after Hermitization, where each scalar Gaussian message is replaced by a small matrix-valued message
[
77
,
47
]
.

Several qualifications are useful before moving on. First, the cavity equations are exact on trees and become asymptotically exact on locally tree-like random graphs under the assumptions implicit in the replica-symmetric or Bethe description. When the problem has several competing pure states, or when long-range correlations are not negligible, replica-symmetry breaking or more elaborate message structures may be required. Second, the regulator
\epsilon
in
z=\lambda-i\epsilon
is part of the calculation. In sparse matrices, taking
\epsilon
too small at finite
N
reveals isolated poles and localized eigenstates; keeping
\epsilon
finite gives a smoothed density. Third, the ensemble-level equations such as (
239
) and (
260
) are functional fixed-point equations. Their numerical solution is usually obtained by population dynamics, while their single-instance version is ordinary belief propagation on the graph.

The main lesson of this section is that replica and cavity methods are two views of the same finite-connectivity mean-field structure. The replica method starts from the quenched logarithm, introduces copies, and identifies a functional order parameter. The cavity method starts from local tree-likeness, removes a vertex or edge, and writes recursive equations for messages on a fixed graph. In sparse random matrix theory the two viewpoints lead to the same practical outcome: fixed-instance observables are obtained from local resolvent messages, while ensemble-averaged observables are obtained from the corresponding law of a typical message. The next section develops the algorithmic form of this statement through belief propagation and population dynamics.

Exercises for this section

Exercise 4.1 : Quenched versus annealed free energy.

Let
Z>0
be any positive random variable. Use Jensen’s inequality to show that

\overline{\log Z}\leq\log\overline{Z}\,.

(263)

Explain why this inequality captures the difference between quenched and annealed calculations discussed in the section.

Exercise 4.2 : Replica identity.

Starting from

\frac{\mathcal{Z}^{n}-1}{n}\,,

(264)

show that for
\mathcal{Z}>0
,

\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{\mathcal{Z}^{n}-1}{n}=\log\mathcal{Z}\,.

(265)

What is the formal difficulty in using this identity in a disordered many-body problem?

Exercise 4.3 : Replicated order parameter versus overlap.

For
n=2
replicas, let

P_{++}\,,\hskip 11.49994ptP_{+-}\,,\hskip 11.49994ptP_{-+}\,,\hskip 11.49994ptP_{--}

(266)

be the probabilities of the four replicated local configurations. Show that

q_{12}=P_{++}+P_{--}-P_{+-}-P_{-+}\,.

(267)

Construct two different distributions
P
having the same value of
q_{12}
but different magnetizations. Conclude that the full distribution of replicated local configurations contains more information than the overlap alone.

Exercise 4.4 : Belief propagation on a three-spin chain.

Consider the three-spin chain with Hamiltonian

H(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})=-J_{12}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}-J_{23}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}-B_{1}\sigma_{1}-B_{2}\sigma_{2}-B_{3}\sigma_{3}\,.

(268)

Starting directly from the definition

{}_{i\to j}(\sigma_{i})=\frac{1}{Z_{i\to j}}e^{\beta B_{i}\sigma_{i}}\prod_{k\in\partial i\setminus j}\left[\sum_{\sigma_{k}=\pm 1}e^{\beta J_{ik}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{k}}{}_{k\to i}(\sigma_{k})\right]\,,

(269)

derive explicitly the message
{}_{2\to 1}(\sigma_{2})
and the full marginal of
\sigma_{2}
.

Exercise 4.5 : Derivation of the cavity-field map.

Assume that

{}_{k\to i}(\sigma_{k})=\frac{e^{\beta h_{k\to i}\sigma_{k}}}{2\cosh(\beta h_{k\to i})}\,.

(270)

Show that

\sum_{\sigma_{k}=\pm 1}e^{\beta J_{ik}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{k}}{}_{k\to i}(\sigma_{k})\propto e^{\beta u(J_{ik},h_{k\to i})\sigma_{i}}\,,

(271)

with

u(J,h)=\frac{1}{\beta}\operatorname{arctanh}\left[\tanh(\beta J)\tanh(\beta h)\right]\,.

(272)

Exercise 4.6 : Distributional cavity equation.

For an Erdős–Rényi graph with mean degree
c
, fixed external field
B
, and independent couplings drawn from
p_{J}(J)
, let
W(h)
denote the large-
N
law of a cavity field on a uniformly chosen directed edge. Derive the Poisson form

W(h)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}e^{-c}\frac{c^{\ell}}{\ell!}\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}dh_{r}W(h_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\left(h-B-\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}u(J_{r},h_{r})\right)\,.

(273)

from the local tree picture. Explain why, for Erdős–Rényi graphs, the excess-degree distribution seen by a message is again Poisson with mean
c
.

Exercise 4.7 : Bethe free energy on a single edge.

Take the graph consisting of two spins connected by one edge. Compute the exact partition function

\mathcal{Z}=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}e^{\beta J\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}+\beta B_{1}\sigma_{1}+\beta B_{2}\sigma_{2}}\,.

(274)

Then evaluate the Bethe expression

F_{\rm Bethe}=-\frac{1}{\beta}\left(\log Z_{1}+\log Z_{2}-\log Z_{12}\right)

(275)

using the corresponding cavity messages. Verify that it agrees exactly with the true free energy of the two-spin system.

Exercise 4.8 : Gaussian cavity update.

Assume
{\rm Re}\,\omega_{\ell\to i}>0
so that the following Gaussian integral is convergent. Starting from

P_{\ell\to i}(x_{\ell})\propto e^{-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{\ell\to i}x_{\ell}^{2}}\,,

(276)

show by explicit Gaussian integration that

\int dx_{\ell}\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{\ell\to i}x_{\ell}^{2}+iA_{i\ell}x_{i}x_{\ell}\right]\propto\exp\left[-\frac{A_{i\ell}^{2}}{2\omega_{\ell\to i}}x_{i}^{2}\right]\,.

(277)

Deduce the recursion

\omega_{i\to j}=i(z-A_{ii})+\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\frac{A_{i\ell}^{2}}{\omega_{\ell\to i}}\,.

(278)

Exercise 4.9 : Resolvent from inverse variance.

Using the Gaussian parametrization of the full single-site marginal,

P_{i}(x_{i})\propto\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{i}x_{i}^{2}\right]\,,

(279)

show that

\langle x_{i}^{2}\rangle=\frac{1}{\omega_{i}}\,.

(280)

Then use the Edwards–Jones representation to explain why

G_{ii}(z)=\frac{i}{\omega_{i}}\,.

(281)

Exercise 4.10 : Programming exercise: belief propagation and Gaussian cavity on trees.

Fix a finite tree with
N
vertices, small enough that exact summation over the
2^{N}
spin configurations is feasible. For the Ising part, choose couplings
J_{ij}
on the edges, fields
B_{i}
, and an inverse temperature
\beta
, and report these choices. Implement the belief-propagation update

{}_{i\to j}(\sigma_{i})=\frac{1}{Z_{i\to j}}e^{\beta B_{i}\sigma_{i}}\prod_{k\in\partial i\setminus j}\left[\sum_{\sigma_{k}=\pm 1}e^{\beta J_{ik}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{k}}{}_{k\to i}(\sigma_{k})\right]\,,

(282)

and compare the resulting cavity magnetizations with the exact magnetizations obtained by brute-force summation of the partition function for small
N
.

For the Gaussian spectral part, choose a real symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
supported on the same tree, choose
z=\lambda-i\epsilon
with
\epsilon>0
, and solve the Gaussian cavity equations

\omega_{i\to j}=i(z-A_{ii})+\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\frac{A_{i\ell}^{2}}{\omega_{\ell\to i}}\,.

(283)

Then compute the full inverse variances

\omega_{i}=i(z-A_{ii})+\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}\frac{A_{i\ell}^{2}}{\omega_{\ell\to i}}\,.

(284)

Compare the cavity prediction

G_{ii}(z)=\frac{i}{\omega_{i}}

(285)

with the corresponding diagonal entry of
(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}
obtained by direct inversion. Report the values of
N
,
\lambda
,
\epsilon
, and a discrepancy measure such as
\max_{i}|G_{ii}^{\rm cav}(z)-G_{ii}^{\rm inv}(z)|
.

5
Belief propagation and population dynamics

The replica and cavity methods provide a conceptual route from quenched disorder to self-consistency equations. In practice, these equations still have to be solved. For sparse random matrices there are two closely related computational levels. The first is a message-passing method on a single finite graph: given one realization of the matrix, one iterates the cavity equations on its directed edges and obtains approximations to the diagonal resolvent entries. This is the belief-propagation viewpoint. In this setting, belief propagation is simply the fixed-point iteration of the cavity equations on a particular graph. The second is an ensemble-level problem: one studies the large-
N
statistics of messages obtained by choosing a directed edge uniformly in a random sparse instance. This is the population-dynamics viewpoint. In this case the fixed-point iteration acts on the limiting message law, which is represented numerically by a large population of messages. In both cases, the procedure is an operational implementation of the same cavity principle.

Belief propagation is most naturally formulated in the context of graphical models whose probability density factorizes into local terms. For a pairwise model on a simple undirected graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
, with variables
x_{i}
attached to vertices, one writes

P(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{Z}\prod_{i\in V}\phi_{i}(x_{i})\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E}{}_{ij}(x_{i},x_{j})\,,

(286)

with
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
and
V=\{1,\ldots,N\}
. On a tree, marginal probabilities can be computed exactly by passing messages from leaves to the root. In the notation used here, the message from
i
to
j
is a cavity marginal
P_{i\to j}(x_{i})
for
x_{i}
in the graph where the edge
\{i,j\}
has been removed. It satisfies

P_{i\to j}(x_{i})=\frac{\phi_{i}(x_{i})}{Z_{i\to j}}\prod_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\left[\int dx_{\ell}{}_{i\ell}(x_{i},x_{\ell})P_{\ell\to i}(x_{\ell})\right]\,,

(287)

where
\partial i
denotes the set of neighbors of
i
, and
Z_{i\to j}
is a normalization constant. The corresponding single-site belief
P_{i}(x_{i})
is

P_{i}(x_{i})=\frac{\phi_{i}(x_{i})}{Z_{i}}\prod_{\ell\in\partial i}\left[\int dx_{\ell}{}_{i\ell}(x_{i},x_{\ell})P_{\ell\to i}(x_{\ell})\right]\,.

(288)

Equations (
287
) and (
288
) are exact on trees. On graphs with loops, their iterative use is called loopy belief propagation. Its fixed points are stationary points of the Bethe free energy, and this is the sense in which belief propagation is the algorithmic counterpart of the Bethe or replica-symmetric cavity approximation
[
10
,
67
,
38
,
92
,
50
]
.

For the spectral problem, the variables are continuous Gaussian variables introduced by the Edwards–Jones representation. The factorized object is now a complex Gaussian weight rather than a positive probability density, but the message-passing algebra is the same. For a real symmetric diluted matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
and spectral parameter

z=\lambda-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon>0\,,

(289)

the Gaussian partition function may be written as

Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\int\left[\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{dx_{i}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(z-A_{ii})x_{i}^{2}+i\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}A_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}\right]\,.

(290)

Here the edge sum is over unordered edges of the underlying undirected support, so each off-diagonal interaction is counted once. This has precisely the form (
286
), identifying

\phi_{i}(x_{i})=\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}(z-A_{ii})x_{i}^{2}\right]\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{}_{ij}(x_{i},x_{j})=\exp\left[iA_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}\right]\,.

(291)

The choice
{\rm Im}z<0
gives a positive damping factor in the Gaussian integral. In the convention used here, the diagonal resolvent has positive imaginary part for
\epsilon>0
.

The essential simplification is that the cavity messages remain Gaussian. We therefore parametrize them by their quadratic coefficient,

P_{i\to j}(x_{i})\propto\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{i\to j}x_{i}^{2}\right]\,,

(292)

where
\omega_{i\to j}
is a complex inverse variance. The proportionality constant is fixed by normalization once a branch of the complex Gaussian integral is chosen; for the cavity recursion only the quadratic coefficient is needed. Substituting (
292
) into (
287
), one uses the elementary Gaussian identity

\int\frac{dx_{\ell}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{\ell\to i}x_{\ell}^{2}+iA_{i\ell}x_{i}x_{\ell}\right]=\omega_{\ell\to i}^{-1/2}\exp\left[-\frac{A_{i\ell}^{2}}{2\omega_{\ell\to i}}x_{i}^{2}\right]\,,

(293)

and obtains

\omega_{i\to j}=i(z-A_{ii})+\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\frac{A_{i\ell}^{2}}{\omega_{\ell\to i}}\,.

(294)

Equivalently, introducing the cavity Green function

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{i}{\omega_{i\to j}(z)}\,,

(295)

one obtains the more transparent recursion

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{1}{z-A_{ii}-\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}A_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,.

(296)

Once the directed-edge messages have reached a fixed point, the full local resolvent at vertex
i
is

G_{ii}(z)=\frac{1}{z-A_{ii}-\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}A_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,.

(297)

Example 5.1 : One belief-propagation sweep on a three-site chain

Consider the symmetric matrix on the chain
1\!-\!2\!-\!3
,

\boldsymbol{A}=\begin{pmatrix}0&J_{12}&0\\
J_{12}&0&J_{23}\\
0&J_{23}&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz=\lambda-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon>0\,.

(298)

The directed cavity messages are

G_{1\to 2}\,,\hskip 11.49994ptG_{2\to 1}\,,\hskip 11.49994ptG_{2\to 3}\,,\hskip 11.49994ptG_{3\to 2}\,.

(299)

Since vertices
1
and
3
are leaves, the two leaf messages are

G_{1\to 2}(z)=\frac{1}{z}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{3\to 2}(z)=\frac{1}{z}\,.

(300)

The messages sent from the middle vertex to the leaves exclude the recipient vertex. Therefore

G_{2\to 1}(z)=\frac{1}{z-J_{23}^{2}G_{3\to 2}(z)}=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{J_{23}^{2}}{z}}\,,

(301)

and

G_{2\to 3}(z)=\frac{1}{z-J_{12}^{2}G_{1\to 2}(z)}=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{J_{12}^{2}}{z}}\,.

(302)

The full local Green functions are then

G_{1}(z)=\frac{1}{z-J_{12}^{2}G_{2\to 1}(z)}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{3}(z)=\frac{1}{z-J_{23}^{2}G_{2\to 3}(z)}\,,

(303)

and

G_{2}(z)=\frac{1}{z-J_{12}^{2}G_{1\to 2}(z)-J_{23}^{2}G_{3\to 2}(z)}=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{J_{12}^{2}+J_{23}^{2}}{z}}\,.

(304)

In this tree example, belief propagation is not an approximation: the quantities in
(
303
) and (
304
)
are exactly the diagonal entries of
(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}
. The example also shows the meaning of a cavity message:
G_{2\to 1}
is the Green function at vertex
2
in the branch in which the edge to vertex
1
has been removed.

The spectral density of the instance is then estimated as

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A},\epsilon}^{\rm BP}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\rm Im}[G_{ii}(\lambda-i\epsilon)]\,.

(305)

On a tree, (
296
)–(
305
) are exact. On a sparse random graph, they become asymptotically exact for locally tree-like ensembles under the same replica-symmetric assumptions that underlie the cavity method
[
79
,
83
]
.

For a fixed finite graph, belief propagation is implemented by initializing all directed-edge messages
G_{i\to j}^{(0)}
with positive imaginary part and then iterating the map

G_{i\to j}^{(t+1)}=\mathcal{F}_{i\to j}\left(\{G_{\ell\to i}^{(t)}\}_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\right)\,,

(306)

where

\mathcal{F}_{i\to j}\left(\{G_{\ell\to i}\}\right)=\frac{1}{z-A_{ii}-\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}A_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}}\,.

(307)

In practice, the following damped fixed-point iteration is often more stable:

G_{i\to j}^{(t+1)}=(1-\gamma)G_{i\to j}^{(t)}+\gamma\mathcal{F}_{i\to j}\left(\{G_{\ell\to i}^{(t)}\}_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\right)\,,\hskip 22.99988pt0<\gamma\leq 1.

(308)

The regulator
\epsilon
smooths the Dirac delta functions that appear in the empirical spectral density. If
\epsilon
is too large, the density is over-smoothed; if it is too small at finite
N
, isolated poles and localized eigenvectors may dominate the iteration. The preservation of
{\rm Im}[G_{i\to j}]>0
for Hermitian problems with
\epsilon>0
is a useful check on the implementation.

Example 5.2 : Why damping is useful near a fixed point

For a single directed edge update, write the undamped map as

G^{(t+1)}=\mathcal{F}(G^{(t)})\,.

(309)

Suppose
G^{\star}
is a fixed point:

G^{\star}=\mathcal{F}(G^{\star})\,.

(310)

Linearizing around it with

G^{(t)}=G^{\star}+\delta^{(t)}\,,

(311)

one obtains

\delta^{(t+1)}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(G^{\star})\delta^{(t)}+O\left((\delta^{(t)})^{2}\right)\,.

(312)

Thus the undamped update is locally stable if

|\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(G^{\star})|<1\,.

(313)

The damped update is

G^{(t+1)}=(1-\gamma)G^{(t)}+\gamma\mathcal{F}(G^{(t)})\,,\hskip 22.99988pt0<\gamma\leq 1\,.

(314)

Linearizing the damped update gives

\delta^{(t+1)}=\left[1-\gamma+\gamma\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(G^{\star})\right]\delta^{(t)}+O\left((\delta^{(t)})^{2}\right)\,.

(315)

Therefore damping replaces the local multiplier
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(G^{\star})
by

\Lambda=1-\gamma+\gamma\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(G^{\star})\,.

(316)

This does not guarantee convergence in every situation, but it can reduce oscillations and improve stability when the undamped fixed-point iteration is close to marginal. This is why damping is often useful when the regulator
\epsilon
is small or when the spectral parameter is close to a region where local resolvents fluctuate strongly.

The same equations may be viewed not as an algorithm on one finite graph, but as a fixed-point problem for the message statistics generated by an ensemble of locally tree-like graphs. Consider a symmetric diluted matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
of the form

A_{ij}=C_{ij}J_{ij}+D_{i}\delta_{ij}\,,

(317)

where
C_{ij}
is the adjacency matrix of a sparse graph,
J_{ij}=J_{ji}
are independent edge weights with law
p_{J}
, and
D_{i}
are diagonal variables with law
p_{D}
. Let
p_{k}
be the degree distribution and

q_{\ell}=\frac{(\ell+1)p_{\ell+1}}{\sum_{k}kp_{k}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\ell=0,1,2,\ldots\,,

(318)

be the excess-degree distribution seen by following a uniformly chosen edge. For a finite undirected support with edge set
E
, let
\overrightarrow{E}=\{(i,j),(j,i):\{i,j\}\in E\}
be the set of directed edges. A BP fixed point defines the empirical cavity-message law

\mathcal{P}_{{\rm cav},N}(G)=\frac{1}{|\overrightarrow{E}|}\sum_{(i,j)\in\overrightarrow{E}}\delta(G-G_{i\to j})

and the empirical site law

\mathcal{P}_{{\rm site},N}(G)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta(G-G_{ii})\,.

When these empirical laws converge to deterministic limits, we denote them by
\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\rm site}
. The limiting cavity-message law satisfies

\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q_{\ell}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}J_{r}^{2}G_{r}}\right)\,.

(319)

The distribution of a full site Green function is

\mathcal{P}_{\rm site}(G)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}p_{k}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{k}J_{r}^{2}G_{r}}\right)\,.

(320)

The ensemble-averaged spectral density is then

\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A},\epsilon}(\lambda)}=\frac{1}{\pi}{\rm Im}\int dG\mathcal{P}_{\rm site}(G)G\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz=\lambda-i\epsilon\,,

(321)

with the limiting density obtained by taking
\epsilon\downarrow 0
after the thermodynamic limit. In numerical population dynamics one usually works at a small but finite value of
\epsilon
, rather than taking this limit explicitly. A more systematic algorithmic derivation of population dynamics, including the weighted variants used later for tilted ensembles, is given in Appendix
C
.

Equations (
319
)–(
321
) are solved numerically by population dynamics. The idea is to represent
\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}
by a large empirical population

\{G^{(1)},G^{(2)},\ldots,G^{(M)}\}\,.

(322)

One update consists of drawing an excess degree
\ell
from
q_{\ell}
, drawing
\ell
members independently and uniformly, with replacement, from the population, drawing
\ell
edge weights and one diagonal term according to their distributions
p_{J}
and
p_{D}
, and replacing a randomly chosen member of the population by

G_{\rm new}=\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}J_{r}^{2}G^{(a_{r})}}\,.

(323)

After equilibration, observables are computed by drawing a full degree
k
from
p_{k}
, drawing
k
population members,
k
edge weights, and one diagonal term from the same laws, and evaluating

G_{\rm site}=\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{k}J_{r}^{2}G^{(a_{r})}}\,.

(324)

The average of
{\rm Im}[G_{\rm site}]/\pi
over many such samples estimates the integral appearing in Eq. (
321
). Population dynamics is therefore a fixed-point iteration method in which the integrals are evaluated by a Monte Carlo procedure. Note that this is not a diagonalization of finite matrices. It solves the distributional cavity equation directly.

The two computational levels described above are summarized in Figure
2
.

\ell_{1}
\ell_{2}
\ell_{3}

j
G_{\ell_{1}\to i}
G_{\ell_{2}\to i}
G_{\ell_{3}\to i}
G_{i\to j}

\displaystyle G_{i\to j}(z)=\left[z-A_{ii}-\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}A_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)\right]^{-1}

\{G^{(a)}\}_{a=1}^{M}\simeq\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}

\ell\sim q_{\ell}
D\sim p_{D}

J_{1},\ldots,J_{\ell}\sim p_{J}

G^{(a_{1})},\ldots,G^{(a_{\ell})}

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}G_{\rm new}=\left[z-D\right.\\[-2.84526pt]
\left.{}-\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}J_{r}^{2}G^{(a_{r})}\right]^{-1}\end{gathered}
