\displaystyle\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A},\epsilon}(\lambda)}=\pi^{-1}{\rm Im}\,\langle G_{\rm site}\rangle

Figure 2:
Belief propagation and population dynamics as two implementations of the same cavity recursion. On a fixed locally tree-like instance, directed-edge messages are updated by removing the recipient from the incoming neighbourhood. At the ensemble level, the message law is represented by an empirical population, and the same local recursion is sampled as a Monte Carlo fixed-point equation.

Example 5.3 : One population-dynamics update for a Poisson graph

Consider an Erdős–Rényi sparse symmetric ensemble with zero diagonal terms and unit edge weights,

D=0\,,\hskip 22.99988ptJ=1\,.

(325)

The cavity distribution satisfies

\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}e^{-c}\frac{c^{\ell}}{\ell!}\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}G_{r}}\right)\,.

(326)

A single population-dynamics update is therefore the following random experiment. First draw

\ell\sim{\rm Poisson}(c)\,.

(327)

Then draw
\ell
messages from the current population,

G^{(a_{1})},\ldots,G^{(a_{\ell})}\,.

(328)

Finally compute

G_{\rm new}=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}G^{(a_{r})}}\,.

(329)

If
\ell=0
, this gives

G_{\rm new}=\frac{1}{z}\,,

(330)

which is the cavity Green function of an isolated branch. If
\ell=1
, it gives

G_{\rm new}=\frac{1}{z-G^{(a_{1})}}\,,

(331)

which is the update for a branch with one incoming neighbor. Thus population dynamics is not a new approximation beyond the cavity equation: it is the Monte Carlo method for sampling the random recursion itself.

For Erdős–Rényi graphs, the degree distribution and the excess-degree distribution are both Poisson with mean
c
,

p_{k}=e^{-c}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptq_{\ell}=e^{-c}\frac{c^{\ell}}{\ell!}\,.

(332)

For random
c
-regular graphs, they are

p_{k}=\delta_{k,c}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptq_{\ell}=\delta_{\ell,c-1}\,.

(333)

In the unweighted adjacency case,
J=1
and
D=0
, the random-regular population collapses to a single value. The following example makes explicit how the distributional equation reduces to an ordinary algebraic self-consistency equation, and how the Kesten–McKay law is recovered from it
[
37
,
45
]
. Here this calculation is used only as an algorithmic benchmark; the sparse symmetric case will be treated systematically in the next section.

Example 5.4 : Population dynamics collapses to an algebraic equation on a regular graph

For the unweighted adjacency matrix of a random
c
-regular graph, each cavity message has exactly
c-1
incoming neighbors. By symmetry all cavity messages have the same value, say
G_{\rm cav}(z)
. The population equation then collapses to

G_{\rm cav}(z)=\frac{1}{z-(c-1)G_{\rm cav}(z)}\,.

(334)

Multiplying by the denominator gives

(c-1)G_{\rm cav}(z)^{2}-zG_{\rm cav}(z)+1=0\,.

(335)

Thus

G_{\rm cav}(z)=\frac{z-\sqrt{z^{2}-4(c-1)}}{2(c-1)}\,,

(336)

where the branch is chosen so that
G_{\rm cav}(z)\sim 1/z
for large
|z|
and
{\rm Im}\,G_{\rm cav}(\lambda-i\epsilon)>0
. The full site Green function is

G_{\rm site}(z)=\frac{1}{z-cG_{\rm cav}(z)}\,.

(337)

Taking the imaginary part of (
337
) for
z=\lambda-i0^{+}
yields the Kesten–McKay density. This example shows that population dynamics is a distributional generalization of ordinary algebraic self-consistency: in a homogeneous ensemble the population has no width, while in a heterogeneous sparse ensemble it is a genuine distribution.

Sparse covariance and diluted Wishart matrices lead to analogous message-passing equations. As in the bipartite representation introduced earlier, it is natural to express them on the graph associated with the rectangular matrix. Let
\boldsymbol{X}
be an
N\times P
sparse rectangular matrix and consider the linearized Hermitian matrix

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{X}\\
\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}&\boldsymbol{0}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(338)

In this case, the cavity equations on the bipartite graph take the form

G_{i\to\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i\setminus\mu}(X_{i}^{\nu})^{2}\widehat{G}_{\nu\to i}(z)}\,,

(339)

and

\widehat{G}_{\mu\to i}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}(X_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}(z)}\,.

(340)

There are now two different cavity Green functions, corresponding to the two vertex classes of the bipartite graph: variable nodes and factor nodes. The corresponding full Green functions on the two sides are

\begin{split}G_{i}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i}(X_{i}^{\nu})^{2}\widehat{G}_{\nu\to i}(z)}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\widehat{G}_{\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j\in\partial\mu}(X_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}(z)}\,.\end{split}

(341)

The covariance spectral density is the density of
\boldsymbol{W}
normalized by
N
, and it is recovered from the variable side of the linearized problem. The auxiliary matrix
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}
also has its own spectral density, normalized by
N+P
; this density is obtained by tracing over both vertex classes,

\displaystyle\rho_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}},\epsilon}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi(N+P)}{\rm Im}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{i}(\lambda-i\epsilon)+\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\lambda-i\epsilon)\right]\,.

(342)

This is not the covariance density itself. If
\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{r}
are the nonzero eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{W}
, then
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}
has nonzero eigenvalues
\pm\sqrt{\nu_{1}},\ldots,\pm\sqrt{\nu_{r}}
, together with
N+P-2r
zero modes. Thus the two-population formulation is useful both algorithmically and conceptually: the variable-side trace gives the covariance resolvent, while the trace over both sides gives the density of the auxiliary bipartite linearization. In a population-dynamics implementation, the variable-to-factor updates are sampled with the variable excess-degree law, and the factor-to-variable updates with the factor excess-degree law. Full site measurements use the full degree laws on the corresponding side. For the Poisson bipartite ensemble with
P=N/\alpha
, these laws are Poisson with means
d/\alpha
on variable nodes and
d
on factor nodes; for non-Poisson bipartite ensembles the excess and full laws must be kept distinct. This formulation is the natural algorithmic expression of sparse covariance and diluted Wishart ensembles, because the elementary disorder lives on the rectangular matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
, not directly on the induced covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}

[
56
,
79
,
70
,
72
]
.

Example 5.5 : Two populations, covariance density, and linearized density

The two-population representation gives Green functions on the two sides of the bipartite graph. These Green functions are naturally associated with the linearized operator
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}
, but the covariance density is the density of
\boldsymbol{W}=d^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}
. The connection is most transparent at the level of the block resolvent. Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}=d^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{X}
. The variable-variable block of
(z\boldsymbol{I}_{N+P}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}})^{-1}
is obtained by a Schur complement:

\left[z\boldsymbol{I}_{N}-\frac{1}{dz}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\right]^{-1}=z\left[z^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N}-\boldsymbol{W}\right]^{-1}\,.

(343)

Therefore

\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{i}(z)=z\,{\rm Tr}_{N}\left(z^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{N}-\boldsymbol{W}\right)^{-1}\,.

(344)

If
m_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\zeta)=N^{-1}{\rm Tr}(\zeta\boldsymbol{I}_{N}-\boldsymbol{W})^{-1}
, this says that

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{i}(z)=z\,m_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z^{2})\,.

(345)

Thus the covariance resolvent is recovered from the variable side of the linearized problem, after the square map
\zeta=z^{2}
.

The factor-side trace gives the analogous expression for
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}
:

\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\widehat{G}_{\mu}(z)=z\,{\rm Tr}_{P}\left(z^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{P}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}\right)^{-1}\,.

(346)

Since
\boldsymbol{W}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}
have the same nonzero eigenvalues, the factor side carries the same nonzero singular-value information, but with the zero-mode count appropriate to the
P
-dimensional space. If
r={\rm rank}\,\boldsymbol{X}
and
\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{r}
are the nonzero eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{W}
, then

\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{i}(z)=z\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{1}{z^{2}-\nu_{a}}+\frac{N-r}{z}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\widehat{G}_{\mu}(z)=z\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{1}{z^{2}-\nu_{a}}+\frac{P-r}{z}\,.

(347)

Hence

\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\widehat{G}_{\mu}(z)-\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{i}(z)=\frac{P-N}{z}\,.

(348)

The trace over both vertex classes gives the density of the auxiliary linearized matrix,

\rho_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}},\epsilon}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi(N+P)}{\rm Im}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{i}(\lambda-i\epsilon)+\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\lambda-i\epsilon)\right]\,.

(349)

This density is related to, but different from, the covariance density. Away from the zero mode,

\rho_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}}(x)=\frac{2N}{N+P}|x|\,\rho_{\boldsymbol{W}}(x^{2})\,,\hskip 22.99988ptx\neq 0\,,

(350)

where
\rho_{\boldsymbol{W}}
is normalized by
N
.

Let us check these identities on the smallest nontrivial bipartite chain,

i_{1}-\mu-i_{2}\,,

(351)

with nonzero entries
X_{i_{1}}^{\mu}=x_{1}
and
X_{i_{2}}^{\mu}=x_{2}
. The linearized matrix is

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&x_{1}\\
0&0&x_{2}\\
x_{1}&x_{2}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(352)

Since both variable nodes are leaves, the variable-to-factor cavity messages are

G_{i_{1}\to\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{i_{2}\to\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z}\,.

(353)

The factor-to-variable messages are

\widehat{G}_{\mu\to i_{1}}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{x_{2}^{2}}{d}G_{i_{2}\to\mu}(z)}=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{x_{2}^{2}}{dz}}\,,

(354)

and

\widehat{G}_{\mu\to i_{2}}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{d}G_{i_{1}\to\mu}(z)}=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{dz}}\,.

(355)

The full variable Green functions are

G_{i_{1}}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{d}\widehat{G}_{\mu\to i_{1}}(z)}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{i_{2}}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{x_{2}^{2}}{d}\widehat{G}_{\mu\to i_{2}}(z)}\,.

(356)

The full factor Green function is

\widehat{G}_{\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{d}G_{i_{1}\to\mu}(z)-\displaystyle\frac{x_{2}^{2}}{d}G_{i_{2}\to\mu}(z)}\,.

(357)

Writing

\nu=\frac{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}{d}\,,

(358)

one obtains

G_{i_{1}}(z)+G_{i_{2}}(z)=z\left(\frac{1}{z^{2}-\nu}+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\widehat{G}_{\mu}(z)=\frac{z}{z^{2}-\nu}\,.

(359)

This agrees with the general trace formulas: the covariance matrix has eigenvalues
\nu
and
0
, while the factor-side matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{W}}
has the single eigenvalue
\nu
.

Indeed, for this example

\boldsymbol{W}=\frac{1}{d}\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{2}&x_{1}x_{2}\\
x_{1}x_{2}&x_{2}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(360)

so

\rho_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\delta(\lambda-\nu)+\frac{1}{2}\delta(\lambda)\,.

(361)

The linearized matrix has eigenvalues
+\sqrt{\nu}
,
-\sqrt{\nu}
, and
0
, and therefore

\rho_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}}(x)=\frac{1}{3}\delta(x-\sqrt{\nu})+\frac{1}{3}\delta(x+\sqrt{\nu})+\frac{1}{3}\delta(x)\,.

(362)

Thus the two-population cavity equations give the Green functions of the bipartite linearized problem. The variable side recovers the covariance resolvent, while tracing both variable and factor sides gives the density of the auxiliary matrix
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}
.

For non-Hermitian sparse matrices the same principle applies after Hermitization. In the simplest case, each vertex carries a doubled Gaussian variable, and the scalar cavity Green function is replaced by a small
2\times 2
matrix-valued message. In schematic form, on the symmetrized support of the directed matrix, the update becomes

\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}=\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}-\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}\boldsymbol{G}_{\ell\to i}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}^{\dagger}\right]^{-1}\,,

(363)

where
\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}
contains the complex spectral parameter and the Hermitization regulator, while the matrices
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}
are the Hermitized edge blocks encoding the directed couplings. The algorithmic structure is unchanged: belief propagation solves (
363
) on a single sparse instance, and population dynamics solves the corresponding ensemble-level fixed-point equation. This is the computational basis of the cavity approach to sparse non-Hermitian spectra
[
77
,
47
]
.

The origin of population dynamics in this context is broader than random matrix theory. Recursive distributions of Green functions appeared already in self-consistent approaches to localization on Bethe lattices
[
1
]
. In disordered systems, population dynamics became the standard numerical method for solving finite-connectivity cavity equations, especially when the order parameter is a distribution of effective fields rather than a finite set of scalars
[
51
,
52
,
50
]
. In sparse random matrix theory, the same algorithm solves the distributional equations for local resolvents and therefore provides the ensemble-averaged spectral density without diagonalizing large random matrices
[
79
,
83
]
.

Several practical checks are important. The density obtained from (
305
) or (
321
) must be nonnegative and normalized after integration over
\lambda
. The result should be stable under changes of population size, iteration time, damping, and regulator
\epsilon
. In ensembles with known limits, such as random regular graphs, dense Erdős–Rényi matrices, or dense covariance matrices, the algorithm should recover the appropriate benchmark law. Finally, one should remember that finite
\epsilon
computes a smoothed spectral density. In sparse systems this is not only a numerical convenience: the small-
\epsilon
behavior contains information about localization, isolated spectral peaks, and the distinction between absolutely continuous and singular spectral components.

Belief propagation and population dynamics are therefore the operational form of the cavity method. Belief propagation solves the local resolvent equations on a fixed sparse instance; population dynamics solves the corresponding ensemble fixed-point problem for the statistics of a typical message. The first is useful for comparing with a given matrix realization, while the second gives direct access to disorder-averaged spectral observables. The following sections apply these tools to concrete ensembles, beginning with sparse symmetric random matrices.

Exercises for this section

Exercise 5.1 : Belief propagation on a tree.

Starting from the pairwise graphical model

P(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{Z}\prod_{i\in V}\phi_{i}(x_{i})\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E}{}_{ij}(x_{i},x_{j})\,,

(364)

on a simple undirected tree with vertex set
V=\{1,\ldots,N\}
, derive the belief-propagation recursion

P_{i\to j}(x_{i})=\frac{1}{Z_{i\to j}}\phi_{i}(x_{i})\prod_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\left[\int dx_{\ell}{}_{i\ell}(x_{i},x_{\ell})P_{\ell\to i}(x_{\ell})\right]

(365)

by explicitly using the factorization of branches after cutting the edge
\{i,j\}
.

Exercise 5.2 : Gaussian closure of the spectral messages.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}
be an
N\times N
real symmetric matrix supported on a simple undirected graph, and let
z=\lambda-i\epsilon
with
\epsilon>0
. Starting from

\phi_{i}(x_{i})=\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}(z-A_{ii})x_{i}^{2}\right]\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{}_{ij}(x_{i},x_{j})=\exp\left[iA_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}\right]\,,

(366)

and assuming a Gaussian incoming message

P_{\ell\to i}(x_{\ell})\propto\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{\ell\to i}x_{\ell}^{2}\right]\,,

(367)

perform the Gaussian integral over
x_{\ell}
and derive the update

\omega_{i\to j}=i(z-A_{ii})+\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\frac{A_{i\ell}^{2}}{\omega_{\ell\to i}}\,.

(368)

Exercise 5.3 : From inverse variances to cavity Green functions.

Using

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{i}{\omega_{i\to j}(z)}\,,

(369)

show that the inverse-variance recursion in Exercise 5.2 is equivalent to

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{1}{z-A_{ii}-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}A_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,.

(370)

Exercise 5.4 : Exactness on a finite tree.

Consider the three-site chain with adjacency matrix

\boldsymbol{A}=\begin{pmatrix}0&J_{12}&0\\
J_{12}&0&J_{23}\\
0&J_{23}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(371)

Compute
(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}
directly by matrix inversion and verify that the diagonal entries agree with the belief-propagation formulas derived in the worked example.

Exercise 5.5 : Fast update from a full self-energy.

For a fixed finite graph, define

\Sigma_{i}(z)=\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}A_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)\,.

(372)

Show that

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{1}{z-A_{ii}-\Sigma_{i}(z)+A_{ij}^{2}G_{j\to i}(z)}\,.

(373)

Explain why this identity reduces the cost of one belief-propagation sweep from a naive sum over all excluded neighborhoods to a number of operations proportional to the number of directed edges.

Exercise 5.6 : Damping and local stability.

Let
G^{(t+1)}=\mathcal{F}(G^{(t)})
be a scalar fixed-point iteration, and let
G^{\star}
be a fixed point. Derive the linearized stability condition for the damped iteration

G^{(t+1)}=(1-\gamma)G^{(t)}+\gamma\mathcal{F}(G^{(t)})\,,\hskip 22.99988pt0<\gamma\leq 1\,.

(374)

What is the effective linear multiplier in terms of
\gamma
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(G^{\star})
?

Exercise 5.7 : Population dynamics as a Monte Carlo fixed-point equation.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G)
be the distribution of cavity Green functions for a locally tree-like sparse matrix ensemble with excess-degree distribution
q_{\ell}
, diagonal distribution
p_{D}(D)
, and edge-weight distribution
p_{J}(J)
. Starting from

\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q_{\ell}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}J_{r}^{2}G_{r}}\right)\,,

(375)

derive the population update rule

G_{\rm new}=\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}J_{r}^{2}G^{(a_{r})}}\,.

(376)

Explain why the indices
a_{r}
are sampled uniformly from the current population.

Exercise 5.8 : Site law versus cavity law.

Explain why the cavity update uses the excess-degree distribution
q_{\ell}
, whereas the site observable uses the full degree distribution
p_{k}
. Verify explicitly that for an Erdős–Rényi graph both distributions are Poisson with mean
c
, while for a random
c
-regular graph one has

p_{k}=\delta_{k,c}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptq_{\ell}=\delta_{\ell,c-1}\,.

(377)

Exercise 5.9 : Random regular graph benchmark.

For
c\geq 2
, starting from

G_{\rm cav}=\frac{1}{z-(c-1)G_{\rm cav}}\,,

(378)

solve for
G_{\rm cav}(z)
by choosing the branch such that
G_{\rm cav}(z)\sim 1/z
for large
|z|
. Then compute

G_{\rm site}(z)=\frac{1}{z-cG_{\rm cav}(z)}\,.

(379)

Use the imaginary part of
G_{\rm site}(\lambda-i0^{+})
to recover the Kesten–McKay density.

Exercise 5.10 : Bipartite population dynamics.

Let
\boldsymbol{X}
be an
N\times P
sparse rectangular matrix, let
d>0
, and define the linearized sparse covariance matrix

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{0}_{N\times N}&\boldsymbol{X}\\
\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}&\boldsymbol{0}_{P\times P}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(380)

Derive the two message recursions

G_{i\to\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i\setminus\mu}(X_{i}^{\nu})^{2}\widehat{G}_{\nu\to i}(z)}\,,

(381)

and

\widehat{G}_{\mu\to i}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}(X_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}(z)}\,.

(382)

Why are two populations needed in the ensemble-level algorithm?

Exercise 5.11 : Non-Hermitian matrix messages.

Starting from the Hermitized recursion

\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}=\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}-\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}\boldsymbol{G}_{\ell\to i}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}^{\dagger}\right]^{-1}\,,

(383)

explain why the message is now a matrix rather than a scalar. Identify the role of Hermitization in producing this doubled local structure.

Exercise 5.12 : Programming exercise: scalar population dynamics.

Fix a mean degree
c=O(1)
, a regulator
\epsilon>0
, a grid of real values
\lambda
, a population size
M
, a burn-in time
T_{\rm burn}
, and a number
T_{\rm meas}
of site samples used for measurement. Implement population dynamics for the unweighted Erdős–Rényi adjacency ensemble using

G_{\rm new}=\frac{1}{\lambda-i\epsilon-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}G^{(a_{r})}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\ell\sim{\rm Poisson}(c)\,.

(384)

After equilibration, estimate

\rho_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}{\rm Im}\left\langle\frac{1}{\lambda-i\epsilon-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{k}G^{(a_{r})}}\right\rangle\,,\hskip 22.99988ptk\sim{\rm Poisson}(c),

(385)

where the average is over samples from the population and over
k
. For comparison, generate
S
independent sparse Erdős–Rényi adjacency matrices of size
N_{\rm diag}
with entries
A_{ii}=0
,
A_{ij}=A_{ji}
, and
{\rm Prob}(A_{ij}=1)=c/N_{\rm diag}
for
i<j
. Use the same
\lambda
grid and the same
\epsilon
in the population-dynamics and direct-diagonalization estimates, and report
c
,
M
,
T_{\rm burn}
,
T_{\rm meas}
,
N_{\rm diag}
,
S
,
\epsilon
, the grid spacing, and a discrepancy measure.

Exercise 5.13 : Programming exercise: random regular benchmark.

Using the same
\epsilon
,
\lambda
grid,
M
,
T_{\rm burn}
, and
T_{\rm meas}
as in Exercise 5.12, implement population dynamics for the unweighted random
c
-regular ensemble with
c\geq 2
. In this case every cavity update uses
\ell=c-1
incoming messages, and every site sample uses
k=c
incoming messages. Verify numerically that the population collapses to a narrow distribution around the deterministic solution of (
378
). Compare the estimated density with the Kesten–McKay law broadened with the same regulator
\epsilon
and evaluated on the same grid. Report
c
,
M
,
T_{\rm burn}
,
T_{\rm meas}
,
\epsilon
, the grid spacing, and a discrepancy measure.

Exercise 5.14 : Programming exercise: two-population Wishart dynamics.

Fix an aspect ratio
\alpha>0
, a dilution
d>0
, a list of system sizes
N
for which
P=N/\alpha
is an integer, a regulator
\epsilon>0
, a grid of positive real values
\lambda
for the covariance spectral parameter
z_{\rm W}=\lambda-i\epsilon
, a population size
M
, a burn-in time
T_{\rm burn}
, a number
T_{\rm meas}
of site samples used for measurement, and a number
S
of independent samples for direct diagonalization. Generate a sparse rectangular matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
with entries

X_{i}^{\mu}=B_{i}^{\mu}\xi_{i}^{\mu}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Prob}(B_{i}^{\mu}=1)=\frac{d}{N}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Prob}(B_{i}^{\mu}=0)=1-\frac{d}{N}\,,

(386)

where the nonzero weights
\xi_{i}^{\mu}
may be chosen equal to one or drawn independently from a standard Gaussian distribution; report which choice is used. Implement the two-population update associated with the linearized bipartite matrix

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{0}_{N\times N}&\boldsymbol{X}\\
\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}&\boldsymbol{0}_{P\times P}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(387)

Use the variable and factor excess-degree laws for cavity updates, and the corresponding full degree laws for site measurements. The main observable is the covariance density of

\boldsymbol{W}=\frac{1}{d}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\,.

(388)

For each point of the covariance grid, choose
z_{\rm L}
with
z_{\rm L}^{2}=z_{\rm W}
and
{\rm Im}\,z_{\rm L}<0
. From the variable side of the linearized resolvent, estimate

m_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{\rm W})=\frac{1}{Nz_{\rm L}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{i}(z_{\rm L})\,,

(389)

and hence

\rho_{\boldsymbol{W},\epsilon}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}{\rm Im}\,m_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda-i\epsilon)\,.

(390)

Compare this covariance density with direct diagonalization of
\boldsymbol{W}
, using the same
\epsilon
and the same
\lambda
grid.

As an auxiliary check, one may also compute the density of the linearized matrix itself. In that case the trace must include both vertex classes,

\rho_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}},\epsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi(N+P)}{\rm Im}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}G_{i}(x-i\epsilon)+\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\widehat{G}_{\mu}(x-i\epsilon)\right]\,,

(391)

and this density should be compared with direct diagonalization of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}
. This is not the covariance density. Away from the zero mode, the two limiting spectral measures are related by

\rho_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}}(x)=\frac{2N}{N+P}|x|\,\rho_{\boldsymbol{W}}(x^{2})\,,\hskip 22.99988ptx\neq 0\,.

(392)

Check separately the normalization of the covariance density, the zero-mode fraction, and the first moment

\frac{1}{N}{\rm Tr}\boldsymbol{W}\,.

(393)

Report
N
,
P
,
\alpha
,
d
,
M
,
T_{\rm burn}
,
T_{\rm meas}
,
S
,
\epsilon
, the grid spacing, the branch convention for
z_{\rm L}
, the nonzero-weight distribution, and the discrepancy measures used in the covariance and auxiliary linearized comparisons.

6
Spectral density of sparse symmetric random matrices

We now specialize the general formalism to sparse real symmetric matrices. This is the first central example because it contains, in its simplest form, the main conceptual differences between dense random matrix theory and finite-connectivity random matrix theory. In dense Wigner-type ensembles, each row contains a diverging number of weakly correlated entries, and the limiting spectral density is controlled by self-averaging at the level of a scalar resolvent. In sparse symmetric ensembles, each row contains only a finite number of nonzero entries in the thermodynamic limit. The local environment of a vertex therefore remains random. At the ensemble level, the spectral density is obtained from the statistics of local Green functions rather than from a closed equation for a single scalar resolvent.

The ensemble considered in this section is

A_{ij}=D_{i}\delta_{ij}+C_{ij}J_{ij}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptA_{ij}=A_{ji},

(394)

where
\boldsymbol{C}
is the adjacency matrix of a sparse undirected graph,
J_{ij}=J_{ji}
are edge weights, and
D_{i}
are diagonal terms. Unless stated otherwise, the graph is locally tree-like in the thermodynamic limit, the weights on distinct edges are independent with law
p_{J}(J)
, and the diagonal terms are independent with law
p_{D}(D)
. The adjacency matrix of an unweighted graph is obtained by setting
J_{ij}=1
and
D_{i}=0
, while graph Laplacians and Anderson-type operators correspond to different choices of
D_{i}
and
J_{ij}
. For instance, the adjacency operator is

A_{ij}=C_{ij}\,,

(395)

whereas the combinatorial Laplacian is

L_{ij}=\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}C_{i\ell}\right)\delta_{ij}-C_{ij}\,.

(396)

Both are sparse symmetric matrices, but their spectra encode different graph-theoretic and physical information.

The spectral density of sparse symmetric matrices was one of the first random-matrix problems treated by statistical-mechanics methods. Edwards and Jones introduced the Gaussian integral representation of the density of states for large symmetric random matrices
[
26
]
. Rodgers and Bray applied replica ideas to sparse random matrices and showed that finite connectivity leads to a functional order parameter rather than to a scalar self-consistency equation
[
76
]
. Later work clarified the role of localized states, finite-connectivity corrections, and approximate recursive descriptions
[
11
,
80
,
39
]
. The cavity formulation developed in
[
79
]
gives a direct message-passing derivation of the spectral density for locally tree-like sparse symmetric matrices and also connects naturally with sparse covariance ensembles. A pedagogical account of the equivalence between the replica and cavity formulations is given in
[
83
]
. There is also a rigorous parallel literature based on local weak convergence and resolvent methods, which relates sparse random graphs to limiting random trees and proves refined results for random regular graphs in several regimes
[
14
,
22
,
9
]
.

Recall that for a fixed realization of
\boldsymbol{A}
, the resolvent is

\boldsymbol{G}(z)=(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz=\lambda-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon>0\,,

(397)

so that the regularized spectral density is given by

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A},\epsilon}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\rm Im}[\boldsymbol{G}_{ii}(\lambda-i\epsilon)]\,.

(398)

The aim is to compute the large-
N
limit of (
398
), and then to understand the limit
\epsilon\downarrow 0
. In sparse systems these two limits are delicate. At finite
N
, the spectrum is a sum of delta peaks. At finite
\epsilon
, the resolvent smooths these peaks into Lorentzians. In the thermodynamic limit, the limiting spectral measure may contain absolutely continuous parts, isolated atoms, and singular structures associated with localized states or finite graph components. The regulator is therefore both a technical device and a diagnostic probe. In most numerical applications one therefore works at small but finite
\epsilon
, rather than attempting to take the limit explicitly.

The same cavity equations can also be derived directly from Schur complements, without starting from the probabilistic cavity interpretation. Indeed, consider first a tree, and let
G_{i\to j}(z)
denote the diagonal resolvent at vertex
i
in the branch obtained by deleting the edge between
i
and
j
. On a tree, removing
i
disconnects its neighboring branches. The matrix
z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}
restricted to the branch rooted at
i
can then be written in block form as

\begin{pmatrix}z-D_{i}&-\boldsymbol{a}^{\rm T}\\
-\boldsymbol{a}&\boldsymbol{B}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(399)

where the vector
\boldsymbol{a}
has components
A_{i\ell}=J_{i\ell}
for
\ell\in\partial i\setminus j
, and
\boldsymbol{B}
is block diagonal because the branches attached to distinct neighbors
\ell\in\partial i\setminus j
are disconnected. The Schur complement gives

G_{i\to j}(z)=\left[z-D_{i}-\boldsymbol{a}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}\boldsymbol{a}\right]^{-1}\,.

(400)

Since
\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}
is block diagonal, only the diagonal resolvent of the neighboring branch contributes, and one obtains the exact tree recursion

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,.

(401)

The full diagonal resolvent at
i
is obtained by including all neighbors,

G_{i}(z)=\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,.

(402)

Equations (
401
) and (
402
) are exact on trees. On locally tree-like random graphs they become the cavity equations for the thermodynamic spectral density.

The same equations can be written in the inverse-variance notation that follows directly from the Gaussian integral representation introduced in the previous section. Recalling that

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{i}{\omega_{i\to j}(z)}\,,

(403)

then (
401
) becomes

\omega_{i\to j}(z)=i(z-D_{i})+\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\frac{J_{i\ell}^{2}}{\omega_{\ell\to i}(z)}.

(404)

This is the form obtained by assuming Gaussian cavity marginals in the Edwards–Jones partition function. The Green-function version (
401
) is often more intuitive, while the inverse-variance version (
404
) makes the Gaussian nature of the cavity solution explicit.

For an uncorrelated locally tree-like graph ensemble, one describes the statistics of a cavity message selected from the directed edges of a large instance. If
E
is the undirected edge set of a finite support, let
\overrightarrow{E}=\{(i,j),(j,i):\{i,j\}\in E\}
be the set of oriented edges. A fixed point of (
401
) defines the empirical cavity-message law
\mathcal{P}_{{\rm cav},N}(G)=|\overrightarrow{E}|^{-1}\sum_{(i,j)\in\overrightarrow{E}}\delta(G-G_{i\to j})
. When this law has a deterministic thermodynamic limit, we denote it by
\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G)
. Let
p_{k}
be the asymptotic degree distribution and recall that the excess-degree distribution seen by following a uniformly chosen edge is

q_{\ell}=\frac{(\ell+1)p_{\ell+1}}{\sum_{k}kp_{k}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\ell=0,1,2,\ldots\,.

(405)

The limiting cavity-message law satisfies

\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q_{\ell}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}J_{r}^{2}G_{r}}\right)\,.

(406)

The full diagonal Green functions define the empirical site law
\mathcal{P}_{{\rm site},N}(G)=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta(G-G_{i})
. Its deterministic thermodynamic limit is

\mathcal{P}_{\rm site}(G)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}p_{k}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{k}J_{r}^{2}G_{r}}\right)\,.

(407)

The ensemble-averaged spectral density is then

\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A},\epsilon}(\lambda)}=\frac{1}{\pi}{\rm Im}\int dG\mathcal{P}_{\rm site}(G)G\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz=\lambda-i\epsilon\,.

(408)

This is the basic cavity formula for sparse symmetric random matrices. It replaces the scalar Pastur-type equation of dense random matrix theory by a distributional fixed-point equation.

For the sparse Erdős–Rényi ensemble,
p_{k}
is Poisson with mean
c
, and the excess-degree distribution is the same Poisson law:

p_{k}=e^{-c}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptq_{\ell}=e^{-c}\frac{c^{\ell}}{\ell!}\,.

(409)

For a random
c
-regular graph,

p_{k}=\delta_{k,c}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptq_{\ell}=\delta_{\ell,c-1}\,.

(410)

The random regular case, already used above as an algorithmic benchmark, is especially useful here because it gives the first closed-form solution of the sparse symmetric cavity equations. In the locally tree-like
c
-regular limit, the cavity law collapses to a delta peak. For the unweighted adjacency matrix with
J=1
and
D=0
, the representative cavity Green function
G_{\rm cav}
satisfies

G_{\rm cav}(z)=\frac{1}{z-(c-1)G_{\rm cav}(z)}\,.

(411)

Solving the quadratic equation gives

G_{\rm cav}(z)=\frac{z-\sqrt{z^{2}-4(c-1)}}{2(c-1)}\,,

(412)

where the branch is chosen so that
G_{\rm cav}(z)\sim 1/z
for large
|z|
and
{\rm Im}[G_{\rm cav}(\lambda-i\epsilon)]>0
. The full site Green function is

G_{\rm site}(z)=\frac{1}{z-cG_{\rm cav}(z)}\,,

(413)

and hence

\rho_{\rm RRG}(\lambda)=\frac{c}{2\pi}\frac{\sqrt{4(c-1)-\lambda^{2}}}{c^{2}-\lambda^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{|\lambda|\leq 2\sqrt{c-1}}\,.

(414)

This is the Kesten–McKay law
[
37
,
45
]
. For a connected
c
-regular graph, the Perron eigenvalue
\lambda=c
has spectral weight
1/N
and therefore does not contribute to the limiting empirical density, although it may be important for dynamical applications. More generally, the multiplicity of the eigenvalue
c
is the number of connected components.

Example 6.1 : The
c=2
random regular graph and the arcsine law

The Kesten–McKay density provides a useful check in the special case
c=2
. A
2
-regular graph is a disjoint union of cycles. For large cycles, the adjacency spectrum is the same local object as the spectrum of the infinite one-dimensional chain.

Setting
c=2
in (
414
), we obtain

\rho_{\rm RRG}^{(c=2)}(\lambda)=\frac{2}{2\pi}\frac{\sqrt{4-\lambda^{2}}}{4-\lambda^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{|\lambda|\leq 2}=\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{4-\lambda^{2}}}\mathbf{1}_{|\lambda|\leq 2}\,.

(415)

This is the arcsine law.

Let us recover the same result directly from a large cycle. The adjacency matrix of a cycle with
L
vertices has eigenvalues

\lambda_{m}=2\cos\left(\frac{2\pi m}{L}\right)\,,\hskip 22.99988ptm=0,\ldots,L-1\,.

(416)

As
L\to\infty
, the angle

\theta=\frac{2\pi m}{L}

(417)

becomes uniformly distributed on
[0,2\pi)
. Since

\lambda=2\cos\theta\,,

(418)

we have

\left|\frac{d\lambda}{d\theta}\right|=2|\sin\theta|=\sqrt{4-\lambda^{2}}\,.

(419)

For each
\lambda\in(-2,2)
there are two angles in
[0,2\pi)
with the same value of
\lambda
. Therefore

\rho(\lambda)=2\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{4-\lambda^{2}}}=\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{4-\lambda^{2}}}\,,

(420)

which agrees with (
415
). This example shows how the random-regular cavity formula reduces, in the case
c=2
, to the familiar density of states of the one-dimensional tight-binding chain.

The Erdős–Rényi adjacency matrix is less trivial. Even when
J=1
and
D=0
, the random number of incoming messages in (
406
) prevents the distribution
\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}
from collapsing to a delta function. The finite-connectivity spectral density contains features that are absent in dense Wigner matrices. Isolated vertices give an atom at
\lambda=0
. Finite tree components and dangling structures generate additional singular contributions. High-degree vertices and local defects produce localized states and spectral tails. These effects were central in the early replica and effective-medium analyses of sparse spectra
[
76
,
11
,
80
,
39
]
. In the cavity formulation they appear naturally through the distribution of messages: rare local neighborhoods generate rare values of the local Green function. The same low-connectivity picture appeared earlier as a quenched-versus-annealed check; here it is used instead to identify finite graph components as explicit spectral atoms.

Example 6.2 : The first terms of the sparse Erdős–Rényi spectrum at small connectivity

The statement that local graph structures produce singular spectral contributions can be seen already at the first order in the mean degree
c
. Consider the unweighted Erdős–Rényi adjacency matrix,

A_{ij}=C_{ij}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Prob}(C_{ij}=1)=\frac{c}{N}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptC_{ij}=C_{ji}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptC_{ii}=0\,.

(421)

For small
c
, most vertices are isolated, while the first nontrivial components are isolated edges. Components with two or more adjacent edges have probability of order
c^{2}
from the viewpoint of a uniformly chosen vertex.

An isolated vertex contributes the one-dimensional adjacency matrix

\boldsymbol{A}_{\rm iso}=(0),

(422)

and therefore contributes one eigenvalue at
\lambda=0
. The probability that a uniformly chosen vertex is isolated is

\left(1-\frac{c}{N}\right)^{N-1}=e^{-c}+o(1)=1-c+O(c^{2})\,.

(423)

Thus isolated vertices contribute, to first order in
c
,

(1-c)\delta(\lambda)\,.

(424)

Now consider an isolated edge. Its adjacency matrix is

\boldsymbol{A}_{\rm edge}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(425)

with eigenvalues

\lambda_{+}=1\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\lambda_{-}=-1\,.

(426)

The expected number of isolated edges is

\binom{N}{2}\frac{c}{N}\left(1-\frac{c}{N}\right)^{2(N-2)}=\frac{cN}{2}e^{-2c}+o(N)\,.

(427)

Therefore the expected fraction of vertices belonging to isolated edges is

\frac{2}{N}\binom{N}{2}\frac{c}{N}\left(1-\frac{c}{N}\right)^{2(N-2)}=c+O(c^{2})\,.

(428)

Since each isolated edge has two eigenvalues, one at
+1
and one at
-1
, isolated edges contribute

\frac{c}{2}\delta(\lambda-1)+\frac{c}{2}\delta(\lambda+1)+O(c^{2})\,.

(429)

Combining the isolated vertices and isolated edges gives the first-order small-
c
spectral density

\rho(\lambda)=(1-c)\delta(\lambda)+\frac{c}{2}\delta(\lambda-1)+\frac{c}{2}\delta(\lambda+1)+O(c^{2})\,.

(430)

This calculation is elementary, but it is conceptually important. The first correction to the isolated-vertex spectrum is not a smooth perturbation of a dense random-matrix law. It is a contribution from a finite graph component. This is precisely the type of local structure that the cavity distribution of Green functions keeps track of.

The local density of states at vertex
i
is

\rho_{i}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}[G_{i}(\lambda-i\epsilon)]\,.

(431)

The distribution of
\rho_{i}(\lambda)
over vertices is therefore obtained from the distribution of the full Green function (
407
). Extended states are associated with a broad set of vertices having comparable local weights, while localized states are associated with large local spectral weights concentrated on atypical neighborhoods. This connection between local resolvents, random-tree recursions, and localization is closely related to the self-consistent Green-function approach to localization on Bethe lattices
[
1
]
. In sparse random matrices, the same recursion simultaneously gives the spectral density and probes the local structure of eigenvectors.

Example 6.3 : A high-degree vertex creates localized outlying modes

A simple way to see how local structure can create large eigenvalues is to study the star graph with one central vertex connected to
K
leaves. Its adjacency matrix has the block form

\boldsymbol{A}_{\rm star}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\boldsymbol{1}_{K}^{\rm T}\\
\boldsymbol{1}_{K}&\boldsymbol{0}_{K\times K}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(432)

where
\boldsymbol{1}_{K}=(1,\ldots,1)^{\rm T}
. Let an eigenvector have central component
a
and equal leaf components
b
:

\boldsymbol{u}=(a,b,\ldots,b)^{\rm T}.

(433)

The eigenvalue equation
\boldsymbol{A}_{\rm star}\boldsymbol{u}=\lambda\boldsymbol{u}
gives

Kb=\lambda a\,,\hskip 22.99988pta=\lambda b\,.

(434)

Eliminating
a
and
b
gives

\lambda^{2}=K\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\lambda_{\pm}=\pm\sqrt{K}\,.

(435)

The remaining
K-1
eigenvalues are zero, corresponding to leaf vectors orthogonal to
\boldsymbol{1}_{K}
.

After normalization, the eigenvectors associated with
\lambda_{\pm}
have

|a|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt|b|^{2}=\frac{1}{2K}\,.

(436)

Thus half of the eigenvector weight sits on the central vertex, while the other half is spread over the leaves. For large
K
, the eigenvalue scale
\sqrt{K}
is much larger than the typical eigenvalue scale of a bounded-degree region. This example illustrates why high-degree vertices in sparse graphs can create spectral tails or outlying localized modes. Such effects are invisible in a scalar dense effective-medium approximation but are naturally encoded in the distribution of local Green functions.

It is useful to compare the cavity equation with the dense limit and check whether Wigner’s semicircle law is recovered. For this check, set
D_{i}=0
. Suppose that the mean degree
c
tends to infinity and the weights are scaled as

J_{ij}=\frac{\widetilde{J}_{ij}}{\sqrt{c}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\int d\widetilde{J}p_{\widetilde{J}}(\widetilde{J})\widetilde{J}=0\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\int d\widetilde{J}p_{\widetilde{J}}(\widetilde{J})\,\widetilde{J}^{2}=\sigma^{2}\,.

(437)

If the distribution of cavity messages concentrates in this limit, then the sum in (
401
) self-averages by the law of large numbers to

\sum_{r=1}^{k}J_{r}^{2}G_{r}\longrightarrow\sigma^{2}g(z)\,,

(438)

and the limiting Green function satisfies

g(z)=\frac{1}{z-\sigma^{2}g(z)}\,.

(439)

The dense-connectivity reduction of the sparse symmetric cavity equation is derived explicitly in Appendix
B
. This is the usual self-consistent equation for the Stieltjes transform of the Wigner semicircle law. Thus the cavity equations contain the dense Wigner result as a limiting case, but at finite connectivity they retain the full distributional structure of the sparse graph. Figure
3
displays this finite-connectivity benchmark and its dense-connectivity semicircle limit at the level of analytic densities.

-2
-1

0
0.1
0.2
0.3
0.4
\lambda
\rho(\lambda)

c=10

Figure 3:
Kesten–McKay versus the semicircle law in the dense-connectivity scaling. The finite-
c
curves are the analytic random-regular densities after rescaling eigenvalues by
c^{-1/2}
; the dotted curve is the Wigner semicircle law obtained when the cavity message distribution concentrates.

The same observation explains the role of effective-medium approximations. If one replaces
\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G)
by a delta function, or otherwise suppresses the fluctuations of the local Green function, one obtains a closed scalar approximation. Such approximations can capture broad features of the continuous part of the spectrum but miss important sparse-graph effects such as atoms, tails, and localization. The single-defect approximation improves on this by treating one atypical local environment against an effective background
[
11
]
. The full cavity or population-dynamics solution keeps the distribution of local environments and is therefore the natural finite-connectivity theory.

Several consistency checks follow directly from the equations. For an isolated vertex,
k=0
, equation (
402
) gives
G_{i}(z)=(z-D_{i})^{-1}
, so the corresponding contribution to the density is a delta peak at
D_{i}
as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
. For an unweighted random regular graph, the distributional equation collapses to the scalar recursion (
411
), and the Kesten–McKay law (
414
) is recovered. In the large-connectivity scaling (
437
), the distributional recursion collapses instead to the Wigner equation (
439
). These three checks test, respectively, the local-defect limit, the homogeneous finite-connectivity limit, and the dense random-matrix limit.

From a computational point of view, equations (
406
)–(
408
) are solved by population dynamics. One represents
\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}
by a large population of complex numbers, repeatedly updates a randomly chosen member by sampling an excess degree, edge weights, diagonal disorder, and incoming cavity messages according to (
406
), and then samples full site Green functions according to (
407
). The output is the regularized density at the chosen value of
\lambda
and
\epsilon
. Repeating the procedure over a grid of
\lambda
gives the spectral density. The same equations can also be iterated as belief-propagation equations on a single large graph, in which case (
398
) gives the spectral density of that realization.

The conclusion of this section is that sparse symmetric random matrices are governed by random local resolvents. The spectral density is the average of the imaginary part of a full Green function, but that Green function is itself distributed according to the random rooted-tree environment of a typical vertex. This is the fundamental departure from dense random matrix theory. It is also the reason why the same formalism can be extended naturally to graph ensembles with degree correlations, modular structure, covariance-type bipartite ensembles, large deviations of eigenvalue counts, and non-Hermitian sparse matrices.

Exercises for this section

Exercise 6.1 : Schur complement derivation of the cavity recursion.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}
be a real symmetric matrix supported on a finite tree with vertex set
V=\{1,\ldots,N\}
. Write
A_{ii}=D_{i}
and
A_{i\ell}=J_{i\ell}
when vertices
i
and
\ell
are connected. Isolate a vertex
i
from the branches attached to its neighbors and write the block decomposition of
z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}
in which the first block corresponds to vertex
i
. Using the Schur complement, derive

G_{i}(z)=\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,.

(440)

Then repeat the derivation with the edge between
i
and
j
removed and obtain

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,.

(441)

Exercise 6.2 : Inverse-variance and Green-function parametrizations.

Starting from

\omega_{i\to j}(z)=i(z-D_{i})+\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\frac{J_{i\ell}^{2}}{\omega_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,,

(442)

and using

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{i}{\omega_{i\to j}(z)}\,,

(443)

derive the Green-function recursion (
441
). Explain why the Green-function form is usually more directly connected to the spectral density.

Exercise 6.3 : Isolated vertices and atoms.

(444)

Deduce that this vertex contributes a delta peak at

to the local density of states. Specialize to the adjacency matrix of an Erdős–Rényi graph on
N
vertices, with

for

Exercise 6.5 : Small-connectivity expansion.

For the unweighted Erdős–Rényi adjacency matrix with

for

(446)

Which finite connected components would contribute at order
c^{2}
?

Exercise 6.6 : Distributional cavity equation.

Starting from the local recursion

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,,

(447)

derive the distributional equation

\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q_{\ell}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}J_{r}^{2}G_{r}}\right)\,.

(448)

Explain why
q_{\ell}
is an excess-degree law rather than the full degree law.

Exercise 6.7 : Site law versus cavity law.

Given the cavity law
\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G)
, derive the site law

\mathcal{P}_{\rm site}(G)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}p_{k}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{k}J_{r}^{2}G_{r}}\right)\,.

(449)

Use it to prove

\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A},\epsilon}(\lambda)}=\frac{1}{\pi}{\rm Im}\int dG\mathcal{P}_{\rm site}(G)G\,.

(450)

Exercise 6.8 : Random regular graph calculation.

For the unweighted adjacency matrix in the locally tree-like
c
-regular ensemble, equivalently on the infinite
c

G_{\rm cav}=\frac{1}{z-(c-1)G_{\rm cav}}\,,

(451)

for large

and

(454)

of a cycle of length

.

(455)

Using the Poisson excess-degree distribution, close the distributional equation by matching the mean cavity Green function and derive the scalar self-consistency equation

G_{\star}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}e^{-c}\frac{c^{\ell}}{\ell!}\frac{1}{z-\ell G_{\star}}\,.

Which features of the sparse spectrum are lost by this approximation?

7
Spectra of random graphs with topological constraints

The sparse ensembles considered so far are the simplest locally tree-like graph ensembles. In the Erdős–Rényi ensemble the degrees fluctuate independently around a Poisson law, while in the random regular ensemble all vertices have the same degree. Real sparse networks, and many synthetic ensembles used to model them, contain additional topological information: heterogeneous degrees, degree–degree correlations, assortative or disassortative mixing, block structure, communities, and modular organization. From the spectral point of view these features are not perturbative decorations. They modify the statistics of the local neighborhoods seen by the resolvent, which, in turn, modify the self-consistency equations for the spectral density.

In this section we discuss how the cavity description of sparse symmetric matrices is modified when the underlying graph is sampled from an ensemble with topological constraints. The guiding example is the adjacency matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
of an undirected graph,

A_{ij}=C_{ij}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptC_{ij}=C_{ji}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptC_{ii}=0\,,

(466)

although the same construction applies, with minor notational changes, to weighted adjacency matrices, Laplacians, and sparse symmetric operators with diagonal disorder. The essential point is that the local recursion for a fixed tree remains unchanged whenever deleting an edge or vertex separates the adjacent branches:

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}G_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{i}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}G_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,.

(467)

What changes is the probability law of the rooted neighborhood, and therefore the probability law of the incoming cavity messages. The problem is thus not to alter the local resolvent identity, but to compute the correct distributional recursion induced by the constrained graph ensemble.

The need for such a formulation was already apparent in early numerical studies of complex network spectra, where the spectral density of empirical or synthetic networks was found to depart strongly from the semicircle law
[
28
]
. Scale-free degree distributions, hubs, and local inhomogeneities generate tails and localized eigenvectors; degree correlations change the way high- and low-degree vertices are coupled; and modular structure produces isolated or nearly isolated eigenvalues carrying mesoscopic information. Analytical approaches to locally tree-like networks with correlations were developed in several forms, including recursive Green-function equations for complex networks
[
21
]
, replica calculations for constrained ensembles
[
78
]
, and later message-passing and random-matrix treatments of networks with arbitrary degrees, expected degrees, and communities
[
55
,
93
,
58
]
. The formulation below follows the statistical-mechanics viewpoint of
[
78
]
: topological constraints are encoded in the ensemble measure, and the spectral density is obtained from the corresponding distribution of cavity messages. Although
[
78
]
presents the calculation through the replica method, here we use the cavity method as the more direct pedagogical route.

The simplest topological constraint beyond the mean degree is a prescribed degree distribution. Let
p_{k}
denote the asymptotic probability that a uniformly chosen vertex has degree
k
, and let

\langle k\rangle=\sum_{k\geq 0}kp_{k}\,.

(468)

For an uncorrelated configuration model, the degree of a vertex reached by following a uniformly chosen edge is distributed according to the size-biased law

\frac{kp_{k}}{\langle k\rangle}\,.

(469)

Equivalently, the number of remaining neighbors of such a vertex is distributed according to the excess-degree law

q_{\ell}=\frac{(\ell+1)p_{\ell+1}}{\langle k\rangle}\,.

(470)

This replacement of the Poisson excess law by (
470
) is already enough to change the spectral density. A broad degree distribution creates a broad distribution of local resolvents, and high-degree vertices can generate spectral tails or isolated eigenvalues. This is one of the most elementary ways in which topology enters the sparse spectral problem.

Degree–degree correlations impose a stronger constraint. Let
e_{kk^{\prime}}
be the probability that a uniformly chosen oriented edge starts at a vertex of degree
k
and ends at a vertex of degree
k^{\prime}
. This probability obviously satisfies

\sum_{k,k^{\prime}}e_{kk^{\prime}}=1\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\sum_{k^{\prime}}e_{kk^{\prime}}=\frac{kp_{k}}{\langle k\rangle}.

(471)

For undirected graphs one has
e_{kk^{\prime}}=e_{k^{\prime}k}
. The corresponding conditional probability that a neighbor of a degree-
k
vertex has degree
k^{\prime}
is

P(k^{\prime}|k)=\frac{e_{kk^{\prime}}}{\sum_{\ell}e_{k\ell}}=\frac{\langle k\rangle}{kp_{k}}e_{kk^{\prime}}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptk\geq 1\,.

(472)

The uncorrelated configuration model is recovered when

P(k^{\prime}|k)=\frac{k^{\prime}p_{k^{\prime}}}{\langle k\rangle}\,,

(473)

independently of
k
. Deviations from (
473
) encode assortative or disassortative mixing by degree. Assortative mixing means that high-degree vertices tend to connect to high-degree vertices, while disassortative mixing means that high-degree vertices preferentially connect to low-degree vertices
[
59
,
60
,
63
]
.

In a locally tree-like graph ensemble in which (
472
) is the nearest-neighbor degree law and distinct remaining neighbor degrees are conditionally independent at the local-tree level, the cavity message distribution can be conditioned on the degree of the source vertex. In the sums below, neighbor degrees are understood to satisfy
k_{r}\geq 1
, since a vertex reached by following an edge cannot have degree zero; degree-zero vertices enter only through the site law. Let
\mathcal{P}_{k}(G)
be the distribution of a cavity Green function sent by a degree-
k
vertex. Then

\mathcal{P}_{k}(G)=\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{k-1}}\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k-1}P(k_{r}|k)\right]\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k-1}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{k_{r}}(G_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{k-1}G_{r}}\right)\,,\hskip 22.99988ptk\geq 1\,.

(474)

The corresponding distribution of a full local Green function at a degree-
k
vertex is

\mathcal{P}^{(\rm site)}_{k}(G)=\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{k}}\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}P(k_{r}|k)\right]\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{k_{r}}(G_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{k}G_{r}}\right)\,.

(475)

The spectral density is then

\rho(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\sum_{k\geq 0}p_{k}\int dG\mathcal{P}^{(\rm site)}_{k}(G)G\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz=\lambda-i\epsilon\,.

(476)

Equations (
474
)–(
476
) should be read as the degree-conditioned version of the sparse resolvent recursion. For
k=0
, the site law is understood as

\mathcal{P}^{(\rm site)}_{0}(G)=\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z}\right)\,.

(477)

In the uncorrelated case, (
473
) removes the dependence of the neighbor degree on the source degree. Defining the mixed cavity law

\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{kp_{k}}{\langle k\rangle}\mathcal{P}_{k}(G)\,,

(478)

one recovers the ordinary configuration-model cavity equation with the excess-degree distribution (
470
). In the regular case,
p_{k}=\delta_{k,c}
, the distribution collapses to the Kesten–McKay case.

Example 7.1 : A two-degree correlated ensemble

Consider an unweighted graph ensemble with only two possible degrees,

p(k)=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k,1}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k,3}\,.

(479)

The mean degree is

\langle k\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot 1+\frac{1}{2}\cdot 3=2\,.

(480)

The degree distribution seen from a uniformly chosen oriented edge is therefore

s_{k}=\frac{kp(k)}{\langle k\rangle}\,,\hskip 22.99988pts_{1}=\frac{1}{4}\,,\hskip 22.99988pts_{3}=\frac{3}{4}\,.

(481)

To introduce degree–degree correlations, let
e_{kk^{\prime}}
be the probability that an oriented edge starts at a vertex of degree
k
and ends at a vertex of degree
k^{\prime}
. For an undirected graph we impose
e_{kk^{\prime}}=e_{k^{\prime}k}
and the consistency condition

\sum_{k^{\prime}}e_{kk^{\prime}}=s_{k}\,.

(482)

A one-parameter family satisfying these constraints is

e_{11}=a\,,\hskip 22.99988pte_{13}=e_{31}=\frac{1}{4}-a\,,\hskip 22.99988pte_{33}=\frac{1}{2}+a\,,\hskip 22.99988pt0\leq a\leq\frac{1}{4}\,.

(483)

The conditional probabilities are

P(1|1)=4a\,,\hskip 22.99988ptP(3|1)=1-4a\,,

(484)

and

P(1|3)=\frac{1-4a}{3}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptP(3|3)=\frac{2+4a}{3}\,.

(485)

The uncorrelated configuration model corresponds to

e_{kk^{\prime}}=s_{k}s_{k^{\prime}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pta=s_{1}^{2}=\frac{1}{16}\,.

(486)

Larger values of
a
make the graph more assortative by degree, because degree-one vertices connect more often to degree-one vertices and degree-three vertices connect more often to degree-three vertices.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{1}(G)
and
\mathcal{P}_{3}(G)
be the cavity Green-function distributions sent by degree-one and degree-three vertices, respectively. For the unweighted adjacency matrix with zero diagonal terms, a degree-one vertex has no remaining neighbors when sending a cavity message. Hence

\mathcal{P}_{1}(G)=\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z}\right)\,.

(487)

A degree-three vertex has two remaining neighbors in a cavity graph. Therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{3}(G)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\in\{1,3\}}P(k_{1}|3)P(k_{2}|3)\int dG_{1}dG_{2}\mathcal{P}_{k_{1}}(G_{1})\mathcal{P}_{k_{2}}(G_{2})\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-G_{1}-G_{2}}\right)\,.

(488)

The corresponding full site distributions are

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\rm site}(G)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1}\in\{1,3\}}P(k_{1}|1)\int dG_{1}\mathcal{P}_{k_{1}}(G_{1})\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-G_{1}}\right)\,,

(489)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{3}^{\rm site}(G)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in\{1,3\}}\left[\prod_{r=1}^{3}P(k_{r}|3)\right]\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{3}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{k_{r}}(G_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-G_{1}-G_{2}-G_{3}}\right)\,.

(490)

The averaged density is then

\rho(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int dG\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\rm site}(G)G+\frac{1}{2}\int dG\mathcal{P}_{3}^{\rm site}(G)G\right]\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz=\lambda-i\epsilon\,.

(491)

This example shows explicitly how degree correlations enter the cavity equations: the local recursion is the same tree recursion, but the law of the incoming messages depends on the degree of the vertex that sends the message.

The previous equations assume that, once the degree of a vertex is fixed, the degrees of its different neighbors are independent draws from
P(k^{\prime}|k)
. More refined constrained ensembles keep track of correlations among the degrees, or generalized degrees, of different neighbors of the same vertex. A useful way to organize such constraints is through generalized degrees. Define

k_{i}^{(0)}=1\,,\hskip 22.99988ptk_{i}^{(r)}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{ij}k_{j}^{(r-1)}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptr=1,\ldots,L\,.

(492)

Thus
k_{i}^{(1)}
is the ordinary degree of vertex
i
, while
k_{i}^{(2)}
is the sum of the degrees of the neighbors of
i
. More generally,
k_{i}^{(r)}
counts the number of walks of length
r
emanating from
i
. We denote the generalized degree vector by

\boldsymbol{k}_{i}=\left(k_{i}^{(1)},\ldots,k_{i}^{(L)}\right)\,.

(493)

Fixing the empirical law of
\boldsymbol{k}_{i}
imposes hierarchical constraints on the local topology. For
L=1
one fixes only the degree distribution. For
L=2
one constrains information about the degrees of neighboring vertices. Increasing
L
imposes progressively deeper information about the rooted neighborhood. This hierarchy was used in
[
78
]
to derive spectral-density equations for graph ensembles with generalized degree–degree correlations.

A microcanonical ensemble with prescribed generalized degrees may be written formally as

P_{L}(\boldsymbol{C}|\{\boldsymbol{k}_{i}\})=\frac{1}{Z_{L}(\{\boldsymbol{k}_{i}\})}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}_{i}(\boldsymbol{C}),\boldsymbol{k}_{i}}\prod_{i<j}\delta_{C_{ij},C_{ji}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{C_{ii},0}\,.

(494)

The local compatibility condition follows from Eqs (
492
). If a vertex has generalized degree
\boldsymbol{k}
and neighbors with generalized degrees
\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{q}_{k^{(1)}}
, then

\sum_{r=1}^{k^{(1)}}q_{r}^{(m-1)}=k^{(m)}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptm=1,\ldots,L\,,

(495)

where
q_{r}^{(0)}=1
. In a cavity message from a vertex of type
\boldsymbol{k}
to a neighbor of type
\boldsymbol{q}
, the remaining neighbors must therefore satisfy

\sum_{r=1}^{k^{(1)}-1}q_{r}^{(m-1)}=k^{(m)}-q^{(m-1)},\hskip 22.99988pt\,m=1,\ldots,L\,.

(496)

This is the topological origin of the more complicated order parameters in the replica calculation: the message is no longer characterized only by the degree of the source vertex, but by the generalized degree of the source and, in general, by the generalized degree of the removed neighbor.

Example 7.2 : Generalized-degree compatibility for
L=2

For
L=2
, the generalized degree of a vertex is

\boldsymbol{k}=(k^{(1)},k^{(2)})\,,

(497)

where
k^{(1)}
is the ordinary degree and

k^{(2)}=\sum_{j\in\partial i}k_{j}^{(1)}

(498)

is the sum of the degrees of the neighbors. Suppose a vertex has generalized degree

\boldsymbol{k}=(3,7)\,.

(499)

This means that the vertex has three neighbors, and the ordinary degrees of those three neighbors must add up to seven.

Now consider a cavity message sent from this vertex to a neighbor of ordinary degree

q^{(1)}=2\,.

(500)

After removing the edge to this neighbor, two neighbors remain in the cavity branch. The compatibility condition

\sum_{r=1}^{k^{(1)}-1}q_{r}^{(m-1)}=k^{(m)}-q^{(m-1)}

(501)

gives two constraints. For
m=1
, since
q_{r}^{(0)}=1
, one obtains

\sum_{r=1}^{2}q_{r}^{(0)}=2=k^{(1)}-q^{(0)}=3-1\,.

(502)

This simply says that two neighbors remain. For
m=2
, one obtains

q_{1}^{(1)}+q_{2}^{(1)}=k^{(2)}-q^{(1)}=7-2=5\,.

(503)

Thus the remaining two neighbors must have ordinary degrees adding up to five. The allowed ordered possibilities are, for instance,

(q_{1}^{(1)},q_{2}^{(1)})=(1,4),(2,3),(3,2),(4,1)\,,

(504)

subject to whichever degree types are actually present in the ensemble.

This is the local meaning of generalized-degree constraints. They do not change the algebraic form of the resolvent recursion, but they restrict which incoming message types can appear together at a vertex. In a population-dynamics implementation, such constraints are enforced by sampling only compatible local neighborhoods.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{k}\to\boldsymbol{q}}(G)
be the distribution of a cavity Green function sent from a vertex of generalized degree
\boldsymbol{k}
to a vertex of generalized degree
\boldsymbol{q}
. Let

\mathcal{W}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\left(\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{q}_{k^{(1)}-1}\right)

(505)

denote the probability, in the constrained ensemble, that the remaining neighbors have generalized degrees
\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{q}_{k^{(1)}-1}
, conditional on the cavity edge
\boldsymbol{k}\to\boldsymbol{q}
. This weight is supported only on configurations satisfying (
496
). The ensemble-averaged cavity equations are then

\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{k}\to\boldsymbol{q}}(G)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{q}_{k^{(1)}-1}}\mathcal{W}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\left(\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{q}_{k^{(1)}-1}\right)\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k^{(1)}-1}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{q}_{r}\to\boldsymbol{k}}(G_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{k^{(1)}-1}G_{r}}\right)\,.

(506)

Similarly, if
\mathcal{W}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{(\rm site)}(\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{q}_{k^{(1)}})
is the corresponding full-neighborhood probability, supported on (
495
), then

\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{(\rm site)}(G)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{q}_{k^{(1)}}}\mathcal{W}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{(\rm site)}\left(\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{q}_{k^{(1)}}\right)\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k^{(1)}}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{q}_{r}\to\boldsymbol{k}}(G_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{k^{(1)}}G_{r}}\right)\,.

(507)

If
p(\boldsymbol{k})
is the empirical distribution of generalized degrees, the spectral density is

\rho(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}p(\boldsymbol{k}){\rm Im}\int dG\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{(\rm site)}(G)G\,.

(508)

Equations (
506
)–(
508
) are the cavity counterpart of the replica consistency equations for hierarchically constrained graph ensembles. They show explicitly why a scalar effective-medium approximation is insufficient: the resolvent must be averaged over the ensemble of rooted neighborhoods compatible with the imposed topology.

Another important topological constraint is community structure. In a stochastic block model, each vertex carries a group label
a\in\{1,\ldots,B\}
, with group proportions
p_{a}
, and edges are drawn with probabilities depending on the pair of groups
[
35
,
63
,
36
]
. In the sparse undirected case one may write

{\rm Prob}(C_{ij}=1|g_{i}=a,g_{j}=b)=\frac{c_{ab}}{N}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptc_{ab}=c_{ba}\,.

(509)

Up to corrections that vanish as
N\to\infty
, the expected number of neighbors in group
b
of a vertex in group
a
is
p_{b}c_{ab}
, and the expected degree of a group-
a
vertex is

c_{a}=\sum_{b=1}^{B}p_{b}c_{ab}\,.

(510)

For a Poisson block ensemble, the numbers of neighbors in the different groups are asymptotically independent Poisson random variables. Let
\mathcal{P}_{a}(G)
be the distribution of a cavity Green function sent by a vertex in group
a
. The block-conditioned cavity equation is

\mathcal{P}_{a}(G)=\sum_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{B}\geq 0}\left[\prod_{b=1}^{B}e^{-p_{b}c_{ab}}\frac{(p_{b}c_{ab})^{n_{b}}}{n_{b}!}\right]\int\prod_{b=1}^{B}\prod_{r=1}^{n_{b}}\left[dG_{br}\mathcal{P}_{b}(G_{br})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{b=1}^{B}\sum_{r=1}^{n_{b}}G_{br}}\right)\,.

(511)

For the Poisson block model the cavity and full-neighborhood count distributions coincide in the thermodynamic limit, because conditioning on one existing edge leaves the other edge counts Poisson. Therefore the averaged spectral density is

\rho(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\sum_{a=1}^{B}p_{a}{\rm Im}\int dG\mathcal{P}_{a}(G)G\,.

(512)

If the block constraints are microcanonical, or if one fixes the number of neighbors of each block type, then the Poisson factors in (
511
) are replaced by the appropriate constrained neighborhood law. The structure of the recursion, however, remains the same.

Example 7.3 : Two-block Poisson graph and group-conditioned messages

Consider a sparse graph with two groups,
a=1,2
, with group proportions

p_{1}=p\,,\hskip 22.99988ptp_{2}=1-p\,.

(513)

Edges are drawn independently with probabilities

{\rm Prob}(C_{ij}=1|g_{i}=a,g_{j}=b)=\frac{c_{ab}}{N}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptc_{ab}=c_{ba}\,.

(514)

A vertex in group
1
has an asymptotically Poisson number of neighbors in group
1
with mean
pc_{11}
, and an asymptotically Poisson number of neighbors in group
2
with mean
(1-p)c_{12}
. Similarly, a vertex in group
2
has mean numbers
pc_{21}
and
(1-p)c_{22}
of neighbors in groups
1
and
2
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{1}(G)
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}(G)
be the cavity Green-function distributions sent by vertices in groups
1
and
2
. For the unweighted adjacency matrix with zero diagonal terms, the group-conditioned recursions are

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{1}(G)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\geq 0}e^{-pc_{11}}\frac{(pc_{11})^{n_{1}}}{n_{1}!}e^{-(1-p)c_{12}}\frac{[(1-p)c_{12}]^{n_{2}}}{n_{2}!}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{n_{1}}dG_{r}^{(1)}\mathcal{P}_{1}(G_{r}^{(1)})\right]\left[\prod_{s=1}^{n_{2}}dG_{s}^{(2)}\mathcal{P}_{2}(G_{s}^{(2)})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{n_{1}}G_{r}^{(1)}-\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{n_{2}}G_{s}^{(2)}}\right)\,,

(515)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{2}(G)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\geq 0}e^{-pc_{21}}\frac{(pc_{21})^{n_{1}}}{n_{1}!}e^{-(1-p)c_{22}}\frac{[(1-p)c_{22}]^{n_{2}}}{n_{2}!}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{n_{1}}dG_{r}^{(1)}\,\mathcal{P}_{1}(G_{r}^{(1)})\right]\left[\prod_{s=1}^{n_{2}}dG_{s}^{(2)}\,\mathcal{P}_{2}(G_{s}^{(2)})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{n_{1}}G_{r}^{(1)}-\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{n_{2}}G_{s}^{(2)}}\right)\,.

(516)

The corresponding density is

\rho(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\left[p\int dG\mathcal{P}_{1}(G)G+(1-p)\int dG\mathcal{P}_{2}(G)G\right]\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz=\lambda-i\epsilon\,.

(517)

If
c_{11}=c_{12}=c_{21}=c_{22}=c
, then the group labels carry no information and the two equations collapse to the ordinary Erdős–Rényi population equation with mean degree
c
. If the rows of the matrix
(c_{ab})
are different, the two groups have different local environments, and the cavity order parameter becomes a pair of distributions rather than a single one.

Community structure affects the spectrum in two complementary ways. First, it changes the continuous part of the density through the group-dependent local neighborhoods in (
511
). Second, it can generate isolated eigenvalues associated with the low-rank block structure of the mean adjacency matrix. These isolated eigenvalues have vanishing weight in the normalized empirical density when the number of blocks is fixed, but they are crucial for spectral community detection. The transition at which informative eigenvalues merge into the bulk is the spectral manifestation of detectability loss in block-structured random graphs
[
54
,
93
,
68
]
. Thus the spectral density and the outlier spectrum encode different aspects of the same topology: the former describes the typical local environment, while the latter can reveal mesoscopic organization.

The block model also clarifies the relation between communities and degree heterogeneity. A simple stochastic block model controls group membership but not broad degree fluctuations. Degree-corrected block models incorporate both ingredients by assigning each vertex a group label and a degree parameter
[
36
]
. In cavity language this simply enlarges the type space: messages are conditioned on both the degree information and the block label. The resulting equations have the same form as (
506
), with the generalized type
\boldsymbol{k}
replaced by a composite type containing both degree and community data.

This is conceptually important because empirical networks often contain both broad degree distributions and community structure; treating only one of these constraints can give a misleading spectral null model.

All the ensembles discussed in this section remain locally tree-like. This assumption is essential for the direct cavity recursions above. Generalized degrees and block labels encode correlations in rooted neighborhoods, but they do not, by themselves, impose a finite density of short loops. Short cycles, clustering, and motifs create additional dependencies between branches and therefore require enlarged messages or different approximations. Message-passing methods for spectra of networks with short loops have been developed in other contexts
[
61
]
, but they are not part of the locally tree-like framework used in the main line of these notes.

The limiting cases provide useful checks. If all degree correlations are removed, (
474
) reduces to the configuration-model equation. If the degree distribution is
p_{k}=\delta_{k,c}
, it reduces to the random-regular scalar recursion and hence to the Kesten–McKay law. If the block model has a single group, (
511
) reduces to the Erdős–Rényi population equation. If one takes a dense limit with appropriate centering and scaling, the message distribution concentrates and one recovers the corresponding dense random-matrix or finite-rank-deformation description. These checks are important because the constrained equations interpolate between ordinary sparse random matrix theory, network null models, and random graphs with mesoscopic structure.

The conclusion is that topological constraints enter sparse spectra through the law of the cavity environment. The local resolvent recursion is universal on a tree, but the distribution of its inputs is ensemble-dependent. Degree distributions, degree–degree correlations, generalized degrees, and community labels all modify the population of messages and hence the spectral density. This is the central reason why sparse random matrix theory is richer than its dense counterpart: in finite connectivity, the graph topology survives the thermodynamic limit and becomes part of the spectral order parameter.

Exercises for this section

Exercise 7.1 : Edge-type consistency.

Let
p_{k}
be a degree distribution with mean
\langle k\rangle
, and define

s_{k}=\frac{kp_{k}}{\langle k\rangle}\,.

(518)

Suppose
e_{kk^{\prime}}
is the probability that a uniformly chosen oriented edge starts at a vertex of degree
k
and ends at a vertex of degree
k^{\prime}
. Show that

\sum_{k^{\prime}}e_{kk^{\prime}}=s_{k}\,.

(519)

For an undirected graph, explain why one also requires
e_{kk^{\prime}}=e_{k^{\prime}k}
.

Exercise 7.2 : Conditional neighbor law.

For
k\geq 1
with
p_{k}>0
, starting from
e_{kk^{\prime}}
, derive

P(k^{\prime}|k)=\frac{e_{kk^{\prime}}}{\sum_{\ell}e_{k\ell}}=\frac{\langle k\rangle}{kp_{k}}e_{kk^{\prime}}\,.

(520)

Then verify that

\sum_{k^{\prime}}P(k^{\prime}|k)=1\,.

(521)

Exercise 7.3 : Uncorrelated configuration model.

Show that the uncorrelated configuration model corresponds to

P(k^{\prime}|k)=\frac{k^{\prime}p_{k^{\prime}}}{\langle k\rangle}\,,

(522)

independently of
k
. Substitute this into the degree-conditioned cavity recursion. Then define

\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{kp_{k}}{\langle k\rangle}\mathcal{P}_{k}(G)\,,

(523)

and show that the mixed law satisfies the ordinary configuration-model recursion with the excess-degree distribution.

Exercise 7.4 : Assortativity in the two-degree example.

For the two-degree ensemble introduced above, compute the conditional probabilities
P(k^{\prime}|k)
from (
483
). Show that
a=1/16
gives the uncorrelated case. What happens at the two endpoints
a=0
and
a=1/4
?

Exercise 7.5 : Degree-conditioned cavity equations.

For a graph with degrees
k\in\{2,4\}
and conditional probabilities
P(k^{\prime}|k)
, take the unweighted adjacency matrix with zero diagonal terms and spectral parameter
z=\lambda-i\epsilon
,
\epsilon>0
. Write explicitly the coupled equations for
\mathcal{P}_{2}(G)
and
\mathcal{P}_{4}(G)
. Then write the corresponding full site distributions.

Exercise 7.6 : Random regular reduction.

For
c\geq 2
, starting from the degree-conditioned recursion, set

p_{k}=\delta_{k,c}\,.

(524)

Show that the message distribution collapses to a single scalar recursion,

G_{\rm cav}=\frac{1}{z-(c-1)G_{\rm cav}}\,.

(525)

Explain why this is the same recursion that leads to the Kesten–McKay law.

Exercise 7.7 : Generalized-degree compatibility.

For
L=2
, consider a vertex with generalized degree

\boldsymbol{k}=(4,10)\,.

(526)

Suppose that one removed neighbor has ordinary degree
3
. Assuming that the remaining neighbor degrees are positive integers and that no further restriction on degree types is imposed, list all possible ordered triples of ordinary degrees of the remaining neighbors that satisfy the compatibility condition.

Exercise 7.8 : Walk interpretation of generalized degrees.

Let
\boldsymbol{C}
be the
N\times N
adjacency matrix of a simple undirected graph with vertex set
V=\{1,\ldots,N\}
. Using

k_{i}^{(r)}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{ij}k_{j}^{(r-1)}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptk_{i}^{(0)}=1\,,

(527)

show that
k_{i}^{(r)}
counts the number of walks of length
r
starting at vertex
i
. Work out explicitly the cases
r=1,2,3
.

Exercise 7.9 : From generalized degrees to constrained neighborhoods.

For
L=2
, show that fixing
\boldsymbol{k}_{i}=(k_{i}^{(1)},k_{i}^{(2)})
fixes the degree of vertex
i
and the sum of the degrees of its neighbors, but does not fix the degrees of each neighbor individually. Explain why this leads to a constrained sum over compatible neighborhoods in the cavity equation.

Exercise 7.10 : Two-block expected degrees.

Consider a sparse block model on
N
vertices with group labels
g_{i}\in\{1,\ldots,B\}
. Suppose that a fraction
p_{b}
of vertices belongs to group
b
, and that

{\rm Prob}(C_{ij}=1|g_{i}=a,g_{j}=b)=\frac{c_{ab}}{N}\,,

(528)

with
c_{ab}=c_{ba}
for an undirected graph. Show that, up to corrections that vanish as
N\to\infty
, the expected degree of a vertex in group

is

c_{a}=\sum_{b=1}^{B}p_{b}c_{ab}\,.

(529)

Exercise 7.11 : Two-block cavity equations.

For two groups with proportions
p
and

, derive the equations (

) and (

). Explain why the number of neighbors of each group is Poisson in the sparse limit.

Exercise 7.12 : Collapse to Erdős–Rényi.

(530)

Show that
\mathcal{P}_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{2}
and that the common equation is the Erdős–Rényi cavity equation with mean degree
