13
Large deviations for diluted Wishart matrices

We now apply the spectral-count formalism to diluted Wishart matrices. This section is deliberately more explicit than the preceding overview, because the diluted Wishart case is the first example in which the spectral-count large-deviation problem combines two structures at once: the determinant representation of the index number and the bipartite factor-graph structure of sparse covariance matrices. The main reference for this problem is
[
70
]
, which extends to diluted Wishart matrices the sparse-graph index-number formalism introduced in
[
48
]
. The comparison with invariant random matrices is useful but secondary: for Gaussian invariant ensembles the index large deviations are naturally formulated as a Coulomb-gas problem with speed
N^{2}

[
20
,
42
]
, whereas for finite-connectivity Wishart ensembles the natural object is a distribution of local cavity messages and the large-deviation speed is
N
.

We consider again an
N\times P
diluted rectangular matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
, with

P=\frac{N}{\alpha},\hskip 22.99988pt\alpha>0\,,

(987)

and entries

X_{i}^{\mu}=B_{i}^{\mu}\xi_{i}^{\mu}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Prob}(B_{i}^{\mu}=1)=\frac{d}{N}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Prob}(B_{i}^{\mu}=0)=1-\frac{d}{N}\,.

(988)

The nonzero weights
\xi_{i}^{\mu}
are independent random variables with distribution
p_{\xi}(\xi)
. The associated diluted Wishart matrix is

\boldsymbol{W}=\frac{1}{d}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptW_{ij}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}X_{i}^{\mu}X_{j}^{\mu}\,.

(989)

The matrix
\boldsymbol{W}
is real symmetric and positive semidefinite. In the dense limit
d\to\infty
, with the nonzero weights normalized so that the dense covariance scaling has unit second moment, its spectral density reduces to the Marčenko–Pastur law, after accounting for the normalization convention
[
90
,
43
]
. At finite
d
, however, the sparse bipartite graph underlying
\boldsymbol{X}
remains visible, and the spectral statistics are controlled by finite-connectivity cavity equations
[
56
,
79
]
.

Let
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N}
be the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{W}
. Since
\boldsymbol{W}
is positive semidefinite,
\lambda_{i}\geq 0
. For a threshold
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, define the Wishart index number

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Theta(\lambda-\lambda_{i}).

(990)

This is the number of eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{W}
below the threshold
\lambda
, with eigenvalues exactly at the threshold treated separately, or by a fixed endpoint convention. For
\lambda<0
it is identically zero. For
\lambda
larger than the top of the spectrum it is
N
. For intermediate values,
\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)
fluctuates from sample to sample. For
\lambda>0
, its typical intensive value is

k_{\star}(\lambda)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)}=\int_{0}^{\lambda}d\lambda^{\prime}\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda^{\prime})}\,,

(991)

where the lower limit includes the zero-mode atom according to the same endpoint convention, and the expression is understood whenever the limiting density has no atom at the threshold. The problem of this section is not merely to compute
k_{\star}(\lambda)
, but to compute the probability of observing an atypical value

k=\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)\,.

(992)

The large-deviation principle is written as

{\rm Prob}\left[\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=k\right]\asymp\exp\left[-N\Phi_{\lambda}(k)\right]\,,

(993)

where
\Phi_{\lambda}(k)
is the rate function. The speed is
N
because the sparse matrix is controlled by
O(N)
local graph variables. This is different from the
N^{2}
speed of invariant dense ensembles, where changing the index requires rearranging a Coulomb gas of
N
strongly repelling eigenvalues. The corresponding scaled cumulant-generating function is

{}_{\lambda}(s)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\overline{\exp\left[s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)\right]}\,.

(994)

When the Legendre transform is regular,

\Phi_{\lambda}(k)=\sup_{s}\left\{sk-{}_{\lambda}(s)\right\}\,.

(995)

The derivatives at
s=0
give the cumulants per degree of freedom:

{}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(0)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)},\hskip 22.99988pt{}_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(0)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}{\rm Var}\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)\,,

(996)

and analogously for higher cumulants. Thus the same object
{}_{\lambda}(s)
contains both central fluctuations and large deviations.

We first derive the determinant representation of the index. Introduce

z_{-}=\lambda-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz_{+}=\lambda+i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon>0\,,

(997)

and define

D_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)=\det(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{W})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}(z-\lambda_{i})\,.

(998)

For a real number
x\neq 0
, with the principal branch of the logarithm,

\Theta(x)=1+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log(x-i\epsilon)-\log(x+i\epsilon)\right]\,.

(999)

Indeed, if
x>0
the two logarithms have the same limiting phase and the second term vanishes; if
x<0
the limiting phases are
-\pi
and
+\pi
, so the second term is
-1
. Applying (
999
) to
x=\lambda-\lambda_{i}
and summing over
i
gives

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=N+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log D_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{-})-\log D_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{+})\right]\,.

(1000)

This equation is the starting point of the large-deviation calculation. It expresses a discontinuous spectral count as the phase difference of two determinants evaluated on the two sides of the real axis.

Example 13.1 : The Wishart index from the phase of a determinant

Consider the one-dimensional diluted Wishart matrix

\boldsymbol{W}=(w)\,,\hskip 22.99988ptw\geq 0\,.

(1001)

Its index below a threshold
\lambda
is simply

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=\Theta(\lambda-w)\,.

(1002)

Let us recover this from the determinant representation. The characteristic determinant is

D_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)=z-w\,.

(1003)

Equation (
1000
) gives, for
N=1
,

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=1+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log(\lambda-w-i\epsilon)-\log(\lambda-w+i\epsilon)\right]\,.

(1004)

There are two cases. If
\lambda>w
, then
\lambda-w>0
, and the two logarithms have the same limiting phase. Hence

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=1\,.

(1005)

If
\lambda<w
, then
\lambda-w<0
. With the principal branch,

\log(\lambda-w-i0^{+})=\log|\lambda-w|-i\pi\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\log(\lambda-w+i0^{+})=\log|\lambda-w|+i\pi\,.

(1006)

Therefore

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\left[\log(\lambda-w-i0^{+})-\log(\lambda-w+i0^{+})\right]=-1\,,

(1007)

and

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=0\,.

(1008)

Thus the determinant phase formula reproduces the elementary step function. This is the simplest version of the mechanism used later for large deviations: the eigenvalue count is encoded in a discontinuity of the logarithm across the real axis.

We now reuse the Edwards–Jones representation in the spectral-count setting. The Gaussian integral is the same object used for the density, but here its logarithmic phase generates the index. Define

Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)=\int\left[\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{du_{i}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm T}(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{W})\boldsymbol{u}\right]\,.

(1009)

For
{\rm Im}z<0
the integral is convergent, and for the conjugate value
z_{+}
it is understood by analytic continuation. Up to a
z
-independent phase,

Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)=\det\left[i(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{W})\right]^{-1/2}\,.

(1010)

The constant phase cancels in the difference between
z_{-}
and
z_{+}
. Therefore

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=N-\frac{1}{\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{-})-\log Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{+})\right]\,.

(1011)

Exponentiating this identity gives

\exp\left[s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)\right]=e^{sN}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{-})\right]^{-s/(\pi i)}\left[Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{+})\right]^{s/(\pi i)}\,.

(1012)

The powers in (
1012
) are complex when
s
is real and must be understood through the same logarithm branches used in the determinant-phase identity. This is the origin of the replica continuation in the index problem.

The bipartite structure of the diluted Wishart ensemble enters when we substitute (
989
) into the quadratic form. We have

\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{W}\boldsymbol{u}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}^{\mu}u_{i}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\left(\sum_{i\in\partial\mu}\xi_{i}^{\mu}u_{i}\right)^{2}\,,

(1013)

where
\partial\mu
is the set of variable nodes connected to factor node
\mu
in the bipartite graph. Hence

Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)=\int\left[\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{du_{i}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}z\sum_{i=1}^{N}u_{i}^{2}+\frac{i}{2d}\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\left(\sum_{i\in\partial\mu}\xi_{i}^{\mu}u_{i}\right)^{2}\right]\,.

(1014)

This is a Gaussian factor graph. Variable nodes carry the quadratic term
-izu_{i}^{2}/2
, and factor nodes carry rank-one interactions. The index number is therefore the phase difference of two Gaussian factor-graph partition functions, one at
z_{-}
and one at
z_{+}
.

Example 13.2 : The bipartite Gaussian factor for a single sample node

Take
N=2
,
P=1
, and
d=1
, so that

\boldsymbol{X}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}\\
\xi_{2}\end{pmatrix},\hskip 22.99988pt\boldsymbol{W}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\,.

(1015)

Then

\boldsymbol{W}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}^{2}&\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\\
\xi_{1}\xi_{2}&\xi_{2}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(1016)

For a Gaussian integration variable
\boldsymbol{u}=(u_{1},u_{2})^{\rm T}
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{W}\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=\xi_{1}^{2}u_{1}^{2}+2\xi_{1}\xi_{2}u_{1}u_{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}u_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\xi_{1}u_{1}+\xi_{2}u_{2}\right)^{2}\,.

(1017)

Therefore the Edwards–Jones partition function is

Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)=\int\frac{du_{1}du_{2}}{2\pi}\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}z(u_{1}^{2}+u_{2}^{2})+\frac{i}{2}\left(\xi_{1}u_{1}+\xi_{2}u_{2}\right)^{2}\right]\,.

(1018)

This is the simplest example of the bipartite factor structure. The factor node
\mu=1
does not create an arbitrary interaction among
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
; it creates the square of one linear combination of the variables. This rank-one structure is what makes the diluted Wishart cavity equations close on scalar self-energies.

The eigenvalues of (
1016
) are

\lambda_{1}=\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\lambda_{2}=0\,.

(1019)

Thus the index below a positive threshold
\lambda
has the following form:

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=\begin{cases}1,&0<\lambda<\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2},\\
2,&\lambda>\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2},\end{cases}

(1020)

with the zero mode contributing immediately for any positive threshold. This illustrates why zero modes and rank constraints are central in the diluted Wishart index problem.

The replica representation follows directly. For positive integers
n_{-}
and
n_{+}
, introduce

\mathcal{Q}_{N}(n_{-},n_{+})=\overline{\left[Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{-})\right]^{n_{-}}\left[Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{+})\right]^{n_{+}}}\,.

(1021)

The desired scaled cumulant-generating function is obtained by the analytic continuation

n_{-}=-\frac{s}{\pi i}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptn_{+}=\frac{s}{\pi i}\,,

(1022)

namely

{}_{\lambda}(s)=s+\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\mathcal{Q}_{N}\left(-\frac{s}{\pi i},\frac{s}{\pi i}\right)\,.

(1023)

Example 13.3 : Why the replica numbers become complex

The index is a phase of a determinant. This is why the replica numbers in the large-deviation calculation are analytically continued to complex values. To see this explicitly, suppress all irrelevant normalization factors and write

Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)=D_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)^{-1/2}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptD_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)=\det(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{W})\,.

(1024)

From (
1000
),

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=N+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\left[\log D_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{-})-\log D_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{+})\right]\,,

(1025)

where
z_{\pm}=\lambda\pm i\epsilon
. Since

\log D_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)=-2\log Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)\,,

(1026)

we get

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=N-\frac{1}{\pi i}\left[\log Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{-})-\log Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{+})\right]\,.

(1027)

Exponentiating,

\displaystyle e^{s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)}

\displaystyle=e^{sN}\exp\left[-\frac{s}{\pi i}\log Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{-})+\frac{s}{\pi i}\log Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{+})\right]

\displaystyle=e^{sN}\left[Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{-})\right]^{-s/(\pi i)}\left[Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{+})\right]^{s/(\pi i)}\,.

(1028)

Therefore, if one first computes

\overline{[Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{-})]^{n_{-}}[Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{+})]^{n_{+}}}

(1029)

for integer
n_{-}
and
n_{+}
, the desired index generating function is obtained by the analytic continuation

n_{-}=-\frac{s}{\pi i}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptn_{+}=\frac{s}{\pi i}\,.

(1030)

The complex replica numbers are not an arbitrary complication; they come directly from the fact that an index is a phase, not a modulus.

For integer
n_{\pm}
, write the replicated variables as

\underline{u}_{i}^{-}=(u_{i}^{-,1},\ldots,u_{i}^{-,n_{-}})\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\underline{u}_{i}^{+}=(u_{i}^{+,1},\ldots,u_{i}^{+,n_{+}})\,.

(1031)

Then

\displaystyle[Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{-})]^{n_{-}}[Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{+})]^{n_{+}}

\displaystyle=\int\left[\prod_{i=1}^{N}d\underline{u}_{i}^{-}\,d\underline{u}_{i}^{+}\right]\exp\Bigg[-\frac{i}{2}z_{-}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{a=1}^{n_{-}}(u_{i}^{-,a})^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\frac{i}{2}z_{+}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{b=1}^{n_{+}}(u_{i}^{+,b})^{2}+\frac{i}{2d}\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\sum_{a=1}^{n_{-}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}^{\mu}u_{i}^{-,a}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{i}{2d}\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\sum_{b=1}^{n_{+}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}^{\mu}u_{i}^{+,b}\right)^{2}\Bigg]\,,

(1032)

where the normalization constants in the measures are immaterial for the saddle-point equations and for the spectral-count derivatives. Since the columns of
\boldsymbol{X}
are independent, the disorder average over
\mu
factorizes. In the sparse ensemble, a column contains a Poisson number of nonzero entries of mean
d
in the thermodynamic limit. Thus a typical factor node selects
k
variable nodes, each carrying the replicated variables, with probability
e^{-d}d^{k}/k!
, draws
k
weights from
p_{\xi}
, and contributes

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\rm f}[Q]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-d}\frac{d^{k}}{k!}\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}d\underline{u}_{r}^{-}d\underline{u}_{r}^{+}Q(\underline{u}_{r}^{-},\underline{u}_{r}^{+})d\xi_{r}p_{\xi}(\xi_{r})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\exp\Bigg[\frac{i}{2d}\sum_{a=1}^{n_{-}}\left(\sum_{r=1}^{k}\xi_{r}u_{r}^{-,a}\right)^{2}+\frac{i}{2d}\sum_{b=1}^{n_{+}}\left(\sum_{r=1}^{k}\xi_{r}u_{r}^{+,b}\right)^{2}\Bigg]\,,

(1033)

where

Q(\underline{u}^{-},\underline{u}^{+})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta(\underline{u}^{-}-\underline{u}_{i}^{-})\delta(\underline{u}^{+}-\underline{u}_{i}^{+})

(1034)

is the empirical distribution of replicated variables. The replicated disorder average therefore takes the large-
N
form

\mathcal{Q}_{N}(n_{-},n_{+})=\int\mathcal{D}Q\exp\left[N\mathcal{S}_{n_{-},n_{+}}[Q]\right]\,,

(1035)

with action

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{n_{-},n_{+}}[Q]

\displaystyle=-\int d\underline{u}^{-}d\underline{u}^{+}Q(\underline{u}^{-},\underline{u}^{+})\log Q(\underline{u}^{-},\underline{u}^{+})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.49994pt-\frac{i}{2}z_{-}\int d\underline{u}^{-}d\underline{u}^{+}Q(\underline{u}^{-},\underline{u}^{+})\sum_{a=1}^{n_{-}}(u^{-,a})^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.49994pt-\frac{i}{2}z_{+}\int d\underline{u}^{-}d\underline{u}^{+}Q(\underline{u}^{-},\underline{u}^{+})\sum_{b=1}^{n_{+}}(u^{+,b})^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\mathcal{Z}_{\rm f}[Q]\,.

(1036)

The factor
1/\alpha
appears because there are
P=N/\alpha
factor nodes. The saddle point of (
1036
) gives the replicated free energy. This is the replica derivation of the large-deviation problem: the index number has been converted into a replicated Gaussian factor-graph theory with a functional order parameter.

One could continue from this point within the replica formalism. For the finite-connectivity diluted Wishart ensemble this is possible but notationally cumbersome, because the order parameter is a distribution over replicated Gaussian variables on a bipartite factor graph. Since the locally tree-like structure gives the same replica-symmetric fixed point more directly, we now switch to the cavity formulation. At
s=0
, or equivalently
n_{-}=n_{+}=0
, the corresponding distribution reduces to the ordinary population of cavity Green functions used for the typical diluted Wishart density. For
s\neq 0
, the two spectral parameters
z_{-}
and
z_{+}
are coupled through the counting field, and the message must carry information about both sides of the branch cut. Thus one introduces paired cavity messages

\mathfrak{g}_{i\to\mu}=\left(G_{i\to\mu}^{-},G_{i\to\mu}^{+}\right)\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{i\to\mu}^{\pm}=G_{i\to\mu}(z_{\pm})\,.

(1037)

For a fixed bipartite tree, these two components obey the ordinary diluted-Wishart cavity recursion at the two spectral parameters:

U_{\mu\to i}^{\pm}=\frac{(\xi_{i}^{\mu})^{2}}{1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}(\xi_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}^{\pm}}\,,

(1038)

and

G_{i\to\mu}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{z_{\pm}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i\setminus\mu}U_{\nu\to i}^{\pm}}\,.

(1039)

The full local Green functions are

G_{i}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{z_{\pm}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i}U_{\nu\to i}^{\pm}}\,.

(1040)

Equations (
1038
)–(
1040
) are not yet the large-deviation equations; they are the deterministic local recursions on which the tilted problem is built. The counting field
s
changes the probability law of the messages by reweighting local configurations according to their contribution to the phase of the determinant.

To make this reweighting explicit, it is useful to write the determinant in Bethe form. Define the full variable inverse Green function

H_{i}^{\pm}=(G_{i}^{\pm})^{-1}=z_{\pm}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i}U_{\nu\to i}^{\pm}\,,

(1041)

the full factor denominator

R_{\mu}^{\pm}=1-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i\in\partial\mu}(\xi_{i}^{\mu})^{2}G_{i\to\mu}^{\pm}\,,

(1042)

and the edge denominator

E_{i\mu}^{\pm}=1-\frac{1}{d}G_{i\to\mu}^{\pm}U_{\mu\to i}^{\pm}\,.

(1043)

For a tree factor graph, the Gaussian Bethe factorization gives

D_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{\pm})=\det(z_{\pm}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{W})=\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}H_{i}^{\pm}\prod_{\mu=1}^{P}R_{\mu}^{\pm}}{\displaystyle\prod_{(i,\mu)}E_{i\mu}^{\pm}}\,.

(1044)

Here and below, products and sums over
(i,\mu)
run over occupied bipartite edges, equivalently over pairs with
i\in\partial\mu
.

Example 13.4 : Bethe determinant factorization for one factor connected to two variables

Consider again one factor node connected to two variables, with

\boldsymbol{X}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}\\
\xi_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptd=1\,.

(1045)

Then

\boldsymbol{W}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}^{2}&\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\\
\xi_{1}\xi_{2}&\xi_{2}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(1046)

The exact determinant is

D_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)=\det(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{W})=z\left(z-\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2}\right)\,.

(1047)

Now compute the same determinant using the Bethe factorization. Since each variable is a leaf when the factor is removed,

G_{1\to\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{2\to\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z}\,.

(1048)

The full factor denominator is

R_{\mu}(z)=1-\xi_{1}^{2}G_{1\to\mu}(z)-\xi_{2}^{2}G_{2\to\mu}(z)=1-\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}}{z}\,.

(1049)

The factor-to-variable self-energies are

U_{\mu\to 1}(z)=\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}}{1-\xi_{2}^{2}/z}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptU_{\mu\to 2}(z)=\frac{\xi_{2}^{2}}{1-\xi_{1}^{2}/z}\,.

(1050)

The full variable inverse Green functions are

H_{1}(z)=z-U_{\mu\to 1}(z)\,,\hskip 22.99988ptH_{2}(z)=z-U_{\mu\to 2}(z)\,.

(1051)

The edge denominators are

E_{1\mu}(z)=1-G_{1\to\mu}(z)U_{\mu\to 1}(z)\,,\hskip 22.99988ptE_{2\mu}(z)=1-G_{2\to\mu}(z)U_{\mu\to 2}(z)\,.

(1052)

The Bethe determinant formula gives

D_{\boldsymbol{W}}^{\rm Bethe}(z)=\frac{H_{1}(z)H_{2}(z)R_{\mu}(z)}{E_{1\mu}(z)E_{2\mu}(z)}\,.

(1053)

Substituting (
1049
)–(
1052
) and simplifying gives

D_{\boldsymbol{W}}^{\rm Bethe}(z)=z\left(z-\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2}\right)\,,

(1054)

which agrees with the exact determinant (
1047
). This explicit check shows the meaning of the variable, factor, and edge terms in the diluted Wishart Bethe determinant.

Let us verify where the three factors come from. The term
H_{i}^{\pm}
is the inverse variance of the full Gaussian marginal at variable node
i
. The term
R_{\mu}^{\pm}
is the rank-one Gaussian integral produced by factor node
\mu
when all incoming variable-to-factor messages are combined:

\int\prod_{i\in\partial\mu}du_{i}\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{i\in\partial\mu}(G_{i\to\mu}^{\pm})^{-1}u_{i}^{2}+\frac{i}{2d}\left(\sum_{i\in\partial\mu}\xi_{i}^{\mu}u_{i}\right)^{2}\right]\propto(R_{\mu}^{\pm})^{-1/2}\,.

(1055)

The term
E_{i\mu}^{\pm}
removes the double counting of the edge contribution between variable
i
and factor
\mu
. For a single factor attached to one variable,
R_{\mu}^{\pm}=E_{i\mu}^{\pm}
, provided the edge denominator is defined with the same
d^{-1}U_{\mu\to i}
convention as in (
1043
). Then (
1044
) reduces to the exact one-dimensional determinant
z_{\pm}-(\xi_{i}^{\mu})^{2}/d
. This elementary check fixes the relative signs in (
1044
).

Substituting (
1044
) into (
1000
) gives an additive decomposition of the index:

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=N+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\kappa_{i}+\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\kappa_{\mu}-\sum_{(i,\mu)}\kappa_{i\mu}\,,

(1056)

where

\displaystyle\kappa_{i}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log H_{i}^{-}-\log H_{i}^{+}\right]\,,

(1057)

\displaystyle\kappa_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log R_{\mu}^{-}-\log R_{\mu}^{+}\right]\,,

(1058)

\displaystyle\kappa_{i\mu}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log E_{i\mu}^{-}-\log E_{i\mu}^{+}\right]\,.

(1059)

The logarithms in (
1057
)–(
1059
) must be evaluated with branches consistent with the determinant phase in (
1000
). The individual variable, factor, and edge phases are branch-dependent, while the Bethe combination in (
1056
) reproduces the determinant phase. Equation (
1056
) is one of the most useful formulas in this section. It shows explicitly that the index number is an extensive sum of local contributions on a sparse bipartite graph. It also explains why the large-deviation speed is
N
: the atypical index is produced by an atypical empirical distribution of local messages, not by an
N^{2}
-scale Coulomb-gas rearrangement.

Example 13.5 : Variable, factor, and edge contributions to the index

The Bethe decomposition of the index has the form

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=N+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\kappa_{i}+\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\kappa_{\mu}-\sum_{(i,\mu)}\kappa_{i\mu}.

(1060)

The signs are the same as in the Bethe determinant: variable and factor terms are added, while edge terms correct for overcounting.

For the one-factor, two-variable example of the previous example, the exact eigenvalues are

0\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}\,.

(1061)

Take a threshold satisfying

0<\lambda<\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}\,.

(1062)

Then the exact index is

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=1\,.

(1063)

The determinant-phase formula gives the same result because

D_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)=z\left(z-\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2}\right)\,.

(1064)

At
z=\lambda-i0^{+}
, the factor
z
has zero phase because
\lambda>0
, while the factor
z-\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2}
has phase
-\pi
because it is negative and approached from below. Therefore

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=2+\frac{1}{\pi}(-\pi)=1\,.

(1065)

The Bethe index decomposition is a local way of producing the same total phase. The variable terms, factor term, and edge corrections distribute the phase of the determinant across the bipartite graph, but the final sum must reproduce the integer count of eigenvalues below the threshold.

At
s=0
, the paired-message distribution is obtained by solving the ordinary population dynamics at
z_{-}
and
z_{+}
. For the Poisson bipartite ensemble, let
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{g})
be the distribution of the paired variable-to-factor message and
\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{u})
the distribution of the paired factor-to-variable self-energy,

\mathfrak{u}=(U^{-},U^{+})\,.

(1066)

The factor update is

\mathfrak{u}=\mathcal{U}\left(\xi;\{\xi_{r},\mathfrak{g}_{r}\}_{r=1}^{k}\right)\,,

(1067)

with components

U^{\pm}=\frac{\xi^{2}}{1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{r=1}^{k}\xi_{r}^{2}G_{r}^{\pm}}\,,

(1068)

where
k
is Poisson with mean
d
. The variable update is

\mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{G}\left(\{\mathfrak{u}_{r}\}_{r=1}^{\ell}\right)\,,

(1069)

with components

G^{\pm}=\frac{1}{z_{\pm}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}U_{r}^{\pm}}\,,

(1070)

where
\ell
is Poisson with mean
d/\alpha
. These equations generate the typical population. The large-deviation calculation replaces this population by a tilted population. Formally, configurations of messages are reweighted by the factor

\exp\left[s\left(N+\sum_{i}\kappa_{i}+\sum_{\mu}\kappa_{\mu}-\sum_{(i,\mu)}\kappa_{i\mu}\right)\right]\,,

(1071)

with the local terms given by (
1057
)–(
1059
). In the replica derivation, this reweighting is generated automatically by the analytic continuation (
1022
). In a population-dynamics implementation, it appears as a biased sampling of local variable and factor neighborhoods. The scaled cumulant-generating function is the Bethe free energy of this tilted message distribution.

It is useful to write the tilted ensemble explicitly. Let

P_{s}(\boldsymbol{W})=\frac{P(\boldsymbol{W})\exp\left[s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)\right]}{\overline{\exp\left[s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)\right]}}\,.

(1072)

Then

k(s)={}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(s)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\left\langle\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)\right\rangle_{s}\,,

(1073)

where
\langle\cdots\rangle_{s}
denotes expectation with respect to
P_{s}
. The rate function is obtained parametrically as

\Phi_{\lambda}(k(s))=sk(s)-{}_{\lambda}(s)\,.

(1074)

The tilted ensemble is not simply another diluted Wishart ensemble with a different mean degree or a different weight distribution. It is an ensemble biased by a spectral property. The cavity messages therefore acquire a biased law that depends on
\lambda
and
s
.

The first check is obtained by setting
s=0
. Then
P_{s}
is the original diluted Wishart ensemble, and

{}_{\lambda}(0)=0\,.

(1075)

The first derivative gives the typical index fraction:

{}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(0)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)}\,.

(1076)

When the limiting density has no atom at the threshold, and with the endpoint convention fixed, this may be written as

{}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(0)=\int_{0}^{\lambda}d\lambda^{\prime}\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda^{\prime})}\,.

This follows directly from (
990
). It also follows from the determinant representation, because differentiating the logarithmic phase recovers the imaginary part of the resolvent. The second derivative gives the asymptotic variance of the number of eigenvalues below
\lambda
,

{}_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(0)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\left[\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)^{2}}-\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)}^{2}\right]\,.

(1077)

When
{}_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(0)>0
, the curvature of the rate function at its minimum is

\Phi_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(k_{\star})=\frac{1}{{}_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(0)}\,.

(1078)

Example 13.6 : Trivial thresholds and zero modes

The positivity of
\boldsymbol{W}
gives two exact checks on the index large-deviation formalism. Since all eigenvalues satisfy
\lambda_{a}\geq 0
, for any threshold
\lambda<0
,

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=0

(1079)

for every matrix in the ensemble. Therefore

{}_{\lambda}(s)=0\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\Phi_{\lambda}(k)=\begin{cases}0,&k=0,\\
+\infty,&k\neq 0\,.\end{cases}

(1080)

Similarly, if
\lambda
is above the largest eigenvalue for every realization under consideration, or with probability one at the large-deviation exponential scale, then

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=N\,,

(1081)

and

{}_{\lambda}(s)=s\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\Phi_{\lambda}(k)=\begin{cases}0,&k=1,\\
+\infty,&k\neq 1.\end{cases}

(1082)

There is also a nontrivial zero-mode check. If
P<N
, then

{\rm rank}\boldsymbol{W}\leq P\,,

(1083)

so at least
N-P
eigenvalues are exactly zero. With
\alpha=N/P
, this means that the zero-mode fraction is at least

1-\frac{1}{\alpha}\hskip 22.99988pt(\alpha>1)\,.

(1084)

Therefore, for any positive threshold
\lambda
, the index must satisfy

\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)\geq 1-\frac{1}{\alpha}\,.

(1085)

A numerical or analytical solution violating this inequality cannot be correct.

The dense limit provides another important check. When
d\to\infty
after the thermodynamic limit, and the weights are normalized so that the corresponding dense covariance entries have unit second moment, the diluted Wishart ensemble approaches the ordinary dense Wishart ensemble at the level of typical spectral observables. With the normalization used here,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}=\alpha\boldsymbol{W}
has the conventional Marčenko–Pastur density
\rho_{\rm MP}
. Therefore, for a threshold
\lambda
of
\boldsymbol{W}
, the typical fraction of eigenvalues below
\lambda
approaches

k_{\star}^{\rm MP}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{\alpha\lambda}dx\,\rho_{\rm MP}(x)=\int_{0}^{\lambda}d\lambda^{\prime}\,\alpha\rho_{\rm MP}(\alpha\lambda^{\prime})\,.

(1086)

At the level of large deviations, however, one must be careful. The finite-connectivity theory has speed
N
and is controlled by local graph fluctuations. The invariant dense Wishart large-deviation problem has a Coulomb-gas structure and a different speed. Thus the dense-limit check applies directly to the typical density and typical index, while the full rate function may have a singular crossover as
d
grows with
N
.

The practical algorithm suggested by the derivation is as follows. One fixes a threshold
\lambda
and a regulator
\epsilon
. One stores a population of paired messages
\mathfrak{g}=(G^{-},G^{+})
. A factor update samples a Poisson number
k
of incoming variable messages, draws the corresponding weights, and computes the paired self-energy using (
1068
). A variable update samples a Poisson number
\ell
of incoming paired self-energies and computes the new paired Green function using (
1070
). At
s=0
this gives the ordinary population dynamics and hence the typical index. For
s\neq 0
, the same local maps are used, but the sampling is tilted by the local phase contributions (
1057
)–(
1059
); the normalization accumulated by this tilted dynamics gives
{}_{\lambda}(s)
. The rate function then follows from (
1074
).

The conceptual outcome is parallel to the sparse symmetric case but with an important bipartite modification. The number of eigenvalues below
\lambda
is a phase of a determinant. The determinant is represented by a Gaussian factor graph. Because the factor graph is locally tree-like, its determinant admits a Bethe decomposition into variable, factor, and edge contributions. Large deviations of the index are therefore described by a tilted distribution of paired cavity messages. The ordinary spectral density corresponds to the unbiased one-point message distribution; the large-deviation theory requires the biased two-point distribution associated with
z_{-}
and
z_{+}
. This is the finite-connectivity mechanism behind the large-deviation theory for diluted Wishart matrices.

Exercises for this section

Exercise 13.1 : Wishart index in a finite example.

Let

\boldsymbol{X}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptd=1\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\boldsymbol{W}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\,.

(1087)

Compute the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{W}
and determine
\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)
for several thresholds
\lambda
. Identify the zero-mode contribution.

Exercise 13.2 : Determinant representation of the index.

Assuming that the threshold is not exactly equal to an eigenvalue, or after fixing an endpoint convention, starting from the identity

\Theta(x)=1+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log(x-i\epsilon)-\log(x+i\epsilon)\right]\,,

(1088)

derive

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=N+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log D_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda-i\epsilon)-\log D_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda+i\epsilon)\right]\,.

(1089)

Exercise 13.3 : Partition-function representation.

Using

Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)=D_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)^{-1/2}

(1090)

up to
z
-independent factors, derive

e^{s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)}=e^{sN}\left[Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{-})\right]^{-s/(\pi i)}\left[Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{+})\right]^{s/(\pi i)}\,.

(1091)

Explain why this leads to the analytic continuation
n_{-}=-s/(\pi i)
and
n_{+}=s/(\pi i)
.

Exercise 13.4 : Bipartite quadratic form.

Starting from

\boldsymbol{W}=\frac{1}{d}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\,,

(1092)

show that

\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{W}\boldsymbol{u}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\left(\sum_{i\in\partial\mu}\xi_{i}^{\mu}u_{i}\right)^{2}\,.

(1093)

Then derive the bipartite Gaussian representation of
Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z)
.

Exercise 13.5 : Replica sectors.

Write explicitly the replicated variables associated with
[Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{-})]^{n_{-}}
and
[Z_{\boldsymbol{W}}(z_{+})]^{n_{+}}
. Explain why the two sectors are coupled by the same underlying disorder after averaging over
\boldsymbol{X}
.

Exercise 13.6 : Poisson factor average.

For one column
\mu
of
\boldsymbol{X}
, show that the number of nonzero entries converges to a Poisson random variable of mean
d
. Use this to motivate the factor functional appearing in the replicated calculation.

Exercise 13.7 : Paired cavity messages.

Starting from the ordinary diluted Wishart recursions at one spectral parameter, derive the paired recursions

U_{\mu\to i}^{\pm}=\frac{(\xi_{i}^{\mu})^{2}}{1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}(\xi_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}^{\pm}}\,,

(1094)

and

G_{i\to\mu}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{z_{\pm}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i\setminus\mu}U_{\nu\to i}^{\pm}}\,.

(1095)

Why are the two components statistically coupled even though the maps are componentwise?

Exercise 13.8 : Rank-one factor denominator.

For a factor node
\mu
, prove that

R_{\mu}^{\pm}=1-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i\in\partial\mu}(\xi_{i}^{\mu})^{2}G_{i\to\mu}^{\pm}

(1096)

is the determinant correction generated by the rank-one Gaussian integration at the factor node.

Exercise 13.9 : Edge denominator.

Using the variable-to-factor Green function
G_{i\to\mu}^{\pm}
and the factor-to-variable self-energy
U_{\mu\to i}^{\pm}
, derive the edge denominator

E_{i\mu}^{\pm}=1-\frac{1}{d}G_{i\to\mu}^{\pm}U_{\mu\to i}^{\pm}\,.

(1097)

Explain its role as an overcounting correction in the Bethe determinant.

Exercise 13.10 : Bethe determinant for one factor.

For the matrix

\boldsymbol{X}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}\\
\xi_{2}\\
\xi_{3}\end{pmatrix},\hskip 22.99988ptd=1\,,

(1098)

compute
\det(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T})
directly and show that it is

z^{2}\left(z-\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2}-\xi_{3}^{2}\right)\,.

(1099)

Then verify the Bethe determinant factorization using the variable, factor, and edge terms.

Exercise 13.11 : Bethe index decomposition.

Starting from the Bethe determinant formula, derive

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=N+\sum_{i}\kappa_{i}+\sum_{\mu}\kappa_{\mu}-\sum_{(i,\mu)}\kappa_{i\mu}\,.

(1100)

Make explicit which logarithmic phase produces each contribution.

Exercise 13.12 : Trivial threshold checks.

Prove that for

,

(1101)

for every realization. Then show that if

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=N\,.

(1102)

Derive the corresponding forms of
{}_{\lambda}(s)
and

.

Exercise 13.13 : Zero-mode lower bound.

(1103)

(1104)

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)/N
for every positive threshold
\lambda
.

Exercise 13.14 : First derivative of the cumulant-generating function.

Starting from

{}_{\lambda}(s)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\overline{e^{s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)}}\,,

(1105)

show formally that

{}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(0)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)}.

(1106)

Relate this to the integrated spectral density, stating explicitly the convention used if the limiting density has an atom at the threshold.

Exercise 13.15 : Second derivative and variance.

(1107)

Explain why this quantity measures the extensive part of index fluctuations.

Exercise 13.16 : Legendre relation.

Assuming differentiability of

(1108)

Exercise 13.17 : Dense-limit typical index.

In the dense limit, the conventionally normalized matrix
\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}=\alpha\boldsymbol{W}
has the Marčenko–Pastur density. For a threshold
\lambda
of
\boldsymbol{W}
, write the typical index as

k_{\star}^{\rm MP}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{\alpha\lambda}dx\,\rho_{\rm MP}(x)\,.

(1109)

For

\rho_{\rm MP}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{4-\lambda}{\lambda}}\mathbf{1}_{0<\lambda<4}\,.

Exercise 13.18 : Programming exercise: empirical index distribution.

Fix a list of system sizes

X_{i}^{\mu}=B_{i}^{\mu}\xi_{i}^{\mu}\,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Prob}(B_{i}^{\mu}=1)=\frac{d}{N}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Prob}(B_{i}^{\mu}=0)=1-\frac{d}{N}\,,

(1110)

with nonzero weights

drawn independently from

, and form

(1111)

For each value of

over the

,

,

,

,

,

,

, and the threshold convention.

,

.

14
Conditioned spectral density of non-invariant random matrices

The large-deviation formalism of the previous sections answers the following question: what is the probability that a random matrix has an atypical number of eigenvalues in a prescribed region? There is a second, more refined, question. If the matrix is forced to have such an atypical number of eigenvalues, what does its spectrum look like? This is the question of the conditioned spectral density. It is a natural continuation of the index-number problem because the index is only one scalar constraint, while the conditioned spectral density describes the whole empirical measure under that constraint.

For invariant random matrix ensembles this problem can be formulated in terms of a constrained Coulomb gas. The eigenvalues have an explicit joint probability density, and conditioning the index amounts to minimizing the Coulomb-gas energy under an additional constraint on the mass placed below a threshold
[
20
,
42
,
30
]
. For the sparse and diluted ensembles emphasized in these notes, no such eigenvalue gas is available. The matrices are non-invariant: their probability law is not invariant under orthogonal conjugation, and eigenvalues are not the only natural variables. The correct order parameter is instead graphical. The conditioned spectral density must be computed from the distribution of local resolvent messages in a tilted ensemble. This is the point of the theory developed in
[
71
]
.

We consider first a general real symmetric random matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N}
. Let
x
be the threshold used for the conditioning. The index number below
x
is

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Theta(x-\lambda_{i})\,.

(1114)

The empirical spectral density is

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta(\lambda-\lambda_{i})\,.

(1115)

The conditioned spectral density at fixed intensive index
k
is defined by

\rho_{x}(\lambda|k)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)\delta\left(\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)-Nk\right)}}{\overline{\delta\left(\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)-Nk\right)}}\,.

(1116)

Here the overbar denotes the average over the matrix ensemble. At finite
N
, the symbol
\delta
in (
1116
) should be read as a Kronecker delta on the integer-valued count, or equivalently as a narrow count window in a large-deviation formulation;
Nk
is understood to be compatible with the chosen finite-
N
sequence. Eigenvalues exactly at the threshold
x
are treated separately, or by a fixed endpoint convention used consistently in all integrals below
x
. The threshold is denoted by
x
, while
\lambda
denotes the spectral variable at which the density is measured. This separation of notation is useful because the conditioned density is a function of the whole spectral variable
\lambda
, not only of the threshold
x
.

Example 14.1 : A finite conditioned spectral density

Consider an ensemble with only two possible
2\times 2
diagonal matrices,

\boldsymbol{A}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&2\end{pmatrix}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\boldsymbol{A}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-3&0\\
0&-2\end{pmatrix}\,,

(1117)

each chosen with probability
1/2
. Let the conditioning threshold be

x=0\,.

(1118)

The index numbers are

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}_{1}}(0)=1\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}_{2}}(0)=2\,.

(1119)

The empirical spectral densities are

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}_{1}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\delta(\lambda+1)+\frac{1}{2}\delta(\lambda-2)\,,

(1120)

and

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}_{2}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\delta(\lambda+3)+\frac{1}{2}\delta(\lambda+2).

(1121)

Conditioning on the event

\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(0)=\frac{1}{2}

(1122)

selects only
\boldsymbol{A}_{1}
, so the conditioned density is

\rho_{0}\left(\lambda\middle|\frac{1}{2}\right)=\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}_{1}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\delta(\lambda+1)+\frac{1}{2}\delta(\lambda-2)\,.

(1123)

Conditioning on

\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(0)=1

(1124)

selects only
\boldsymbol{A}_{2}
, and therefore

\rho_{0}(\lambda|1)=\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}_{2}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\delta(\lambda+3)+\frac{1}{2}\delta(\lambda+2)\,.

(1125)

In both cases the conditioned density satisfies the defining constraint:

\int_{-\infty}^{0}d\lambda\rho_{0}\left(\lambda\middle|\frac{1}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\int_{-\infty}^{0}d\lambda\rho_{0}(\lambda|1)=1\,.

(1126)

This finite example is deliberately simple: it shows that the conditioned spectral density is not the density of a new matrix formula, but the ordinary empirical density averaged over those samples that satisfy a spectral constraint.

The conditioning imposes the constraint

\int_{-\infty}^{x}d\lambda\rho_{x}(\lambda|k)=k\,,

(1127)

and the density is normalized:

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\lambda\rho_{x}(\lambda|k)=1\,.

(1128)

These two equations are not assumptions; they follow directly from (
1116
). They are the first checks that any calculation of a conditioned density must satisfy. If
k
equals its typical value,

k_{\star}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}d\lambda\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)}\,,

(1129)

then the conditioned density must reduce to the ordinary spectral density:

\rho_{x}(\lambda|k_{\star})=\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)}\,.

(1130)

To compute (
1116
), we first replace the hard conditioning by a tilted ensemble. Define the scaled cumulant-generating function of the index number,

{}_{x}(s)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\overline{\exp\left[s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)\right]}\,.

(1131)

When the corresponding rate function is obtained by a regular Legendre–Fenchel transform,
\Phi_{x}(k)=\sup_{s}\{sk-{}_{x}(s)\}
. The tilted ensemble is

P_{s}(\boldsymbol{A})=\frac{P(\boldsymbol{A})\exp\left[s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)\right]}{\overline{\exp\left[s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)\right]}}\,.

(1132)

Let

\langle O\rangle_{s}=\frac{\overline{O(\boldsymbol{A})\exp\left[s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)\right]}}{\overline{\exp\left[s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)\right]}}

(1133)

denote expectation in this tilted ensemble. If
{}_{x}(s)
is differentiable, the typical index in the tilted ensemble is

k(s)={}_{x}^{\prime}(s)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\left\langle\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)\right\rangle_{s}\,.

(1134)

The hard-conditioned density at
k=k(s)
is then equivalent, at the level of thermodynamic local observables, to the tilted density

\rho_{x}(\lambda|k(s))=\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\langle\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)\right\rangle_{s}\,.

(1135)

Example 14.2 : Soft tilting versus hard conditioning in a toy ensemble

The equivalence between hard conditioning and exponential tilting becomes transparent in the two-matrix ensemble of the previous example. The tilted probability of a matrix is

P_{s}(\boldsymbol{A})=\frac{P(\boldsymbol{A})e^{s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(0)}}{\overline{e^{s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(0)}}}\,.

(1136)

Since

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}_{1}}(0)=1\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}_{2}}(0)=2\,,

(1137)

we obtain

P_{s}(\boldsymbol{A}_{1})=\frac{e^{s}}{e^{s}+e^{2s}}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptP_{s}(\boldsymbol{A}_{2})=\frac{e^{2s}}{e^{s}+e^{2s}}\,.

(1138)

The tilted mean intensive index is

k(s)=\frac{1}{2}\left[P_{s}(\boldsymbol{A}_{1})\cdot 1+P_{s}(\boldsymbol{A}_{2})\cdot 2\right]=\frac{1}{2}\frac{e^{s}+2e^{2s}}{e^{s}+e^{2s}}\,.

(1139)

As
s\to-\infty
,

P_{s}(\boldsymbol{A}_{1})\to 1\,,\hskip 22.99988ptk(s)\to\frac{1}{2}\,,

(1140)

while as
s\to+\infty
,

P_{s}(\boldsymbol{A}_{2})\to 1\,,\hskip 22.99988ptk(s)\to 1\,.

(1141)

The tilted density is

\rho_{s}(\lambda)=P_{s}(\boldsymbol{A}_{1})\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}_{1}}(\lambda)+P_{s}(\boldsymbol{A}_{2})\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}_{2}}(\lambda)\,.

(1142)

Thus the hard-conditioned densities at
k=1/2
and
k=1
are recovered as limiting tilted densities. In large random-matrix ensembles the same principle holds at the thermodynamic level: for differentiable
{}_{x}(s)
, the ensemble tilted by
s
realizes the conditioned density at
k={}_{x}^{\prime}(s)
.

Let us derive this equivalence explicitly at the large-deviation level. Using a formal inverse-Laplace representation of the count constraint,

\delta\left(\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)-Nk\right)=\int_{\gamma-i\infty}^{\gamma+i\infty}\frac{ds}{2\pi i}\exp\left[s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)-sNk\right]\,,

(1143)

the denominator of (
1116
) becomes, at exponential order,

\overline{\delta\left(\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)-Nk\right)}\asymp\int ds\exp\left[N\{{}_{x}(s)-sk\}\right]\,.

(1144)

The saddle point satisfies

{}_{x}^{\prime}(s)=k\,.

(1145)

The numerator has the same exponential factor, but with the observable
\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)
evaluated in the tilted measure at the saddle. Hence (
1135
) follows. When
x
is not differentiable, the equivalence between the hard-conditioned and tilted ensembles may fail or may require a convexified description; in these notes we stay within the differentiable regime.

We now connect the tilted density with determinants and resolvents. Introduce

z_{x}^{-}=x-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz_{x}^{+}=x+i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon>0\,,

(1146)

and

\Delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\det(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})\,.

(1147)

For a real number
u\neq 0
,

\Theta(u)=1+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log(u-i\epsilon)-\log(u+i\epsilon)\right]\,.

(1148)

If
u>0
, the logarithms have the same phase and the second term vanishes. If
u<0
, the two phases differ by
-2\pi
, and the second term is
-1
. Applying this identity to
u=x-\lambda_{i}
and summing over eigenvalues gives

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)=N+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log\Delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{x}^{-})-\log\Delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{x}^{+})\right]\,.

(1149)

The logarithms in (
1149
) are understood with the same branch convention as in (
1148
). Thus the tilted ensemble (
1132
) is a determinant-biased ensemble.

Example 14.3 : The determinant phase fixes the constraint

Let

\boldsymbol{A}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&2\end{pmatrix}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptx=0\,.

(1150)

The characteristic determinant is

\Delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=(z+1)(z-2)\,.

(1151)

At
z_{x}^{-}=-i\epsilon
, with
\epsilon>0
,

\Delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}(-i\epsilon)=(1-i\epsilon)(-2-i\epsilon)\,.

(1152)

As
\epsilon\downarrow 0
, the first factor has phase
0
and the second factor has phase
-\pi
. Thus

\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\log\Delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}(-i\epsilon)=-\pi\,.

(1153)

Similarly,

\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\log\Delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}(+i\epsilon)=+\pi\,.

(1154)

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(0)

\displaystyle=2+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\left[\log\Delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}(-i0^{+})-\log\Delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}(+i0^{+})\right]

\displaystyle=2+\frac{1}{2\pi i}(-2\pi i)=1\,.

(1155)

Thus the determinant phase is exactly the scalar constraint that defines the tilted ensemble. The conditioned spectral density is obtained by measuring a separate probe resolvent in the ensemble biased by this phase.

The spectral density at the point
\lambda
is obtained from a separate resolvent. Let

z_{\lambda}=\lambda-i\eta\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\eta>0\,.

(1156)

The regularized density is

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi N}{\rm Im}{\rm Tr}(z_{\lambda}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\,.

(1157)

Equivalently, using the Edwards–Jones Gaussian partition function

Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\int\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{du_{i}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm T}(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})\boldsymbol{u}\right]\,,

(1158)

one has

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(\lambda)=-\frac{2}{\pi N}{\rm Im}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{\lambda})\,.

(1159)

The tilted conditioned density can therefore be computed from the tilted quenched free energy

\mathcal{F}_{x}^{(s)}(z_{\lambda})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\left\langle\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{\lambda})\right\rangle_{s}\,,

(1160)

through

\rho_{x}(\lambda|k(s))=-\frac{2}{\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\mathcal{F}_{x}^{(s)}(\lambda-i\eta).

(1161)

The replica representation of (
1160
) is obtained by adding a spectator replica at the probe spectral parameter. Define

\varphi_{x}(s,n;z_{\lambda})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\overline{\exp\left[s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)\right][Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{\lambda})]^{n}}\,.

(1162)

Then

\mathcal{F}_{x}^{(s)}(z_{\lambda})=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial n}\varphi_{x}(s,n;z_{\lambda})\right|_{n=0}\,.

(1163)

The field
s
is associated with the determinant ratio at
z_{x}^{-}
and
z_{x}^{+}
, while the auxiliary replica number
n
probes the density at
z_{\lambda}
. The limit
n\to 0
ensures that the probe does not change the tilted ensemble; it only measures a local resolvent inside it. One could continue from this point within the replica formalism. For finite-connectivity ensembles this is possible but notationally cumbersome, because the order parameter must keep track of the replicated fields associated with the two threshold parameters and with the probe resolvent. The corresponding three-sector replica-symmetric saddle is described in Appendix
D
. Since the locally tree-like structure gives the same replica-symmetric fixed point more directly, we now switch to the cavity formulation.

We now specialize these formulas to sparse symmetric matrices, because in that case the graphical meaning is transparent. Let

A_{ij}=D_{i}\delta_{ij}+C_{ij}J_{ij}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptA_{ij}=A_{ji}\,.

(1164)

For every directed edge
i\to j
we introduce three cavity Green functions:

\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}=\left(G_{i\to j}^{-},G_{i\to j}^{+},G_{i\to j}^{0}\right)\,,

(1165)

where

G_{i\to j}^{-}=G_{i\to j}(z_{x}^{-})\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{i\to j}^{+}=G_{i\to j}(z_{x}^{+})\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{i\to j}^{0}=G_{i\to j}(z_{\lambda})\,.

(1166)

On a tree, or within the locally tree-like cavity approximation, these three components obey the same Schur-complement recursion at their respective spectral parameters:

G_{i\to j}^{a}=\frac{1}{z_{a}-D_{i}-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}^{a}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pta\in\{-,+,0\}\,,

(1167)

with

z_{-}=z_{x}^{-}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz_{+}=z_{x}^{+}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz_{0}=z_{\lambda}\,.

(1168)

The corresponding full local Green functions are

G_{i}^{a}=\frac{1}{z_{a}-D_{i}-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}^{a}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pta\in\{-,+,0\}\,.

(1169)

The two components
G^{-}
and
G^{+}
encode the index constraint, while
G^{0}
probes the density at
\lambda
.

Example 14.4 : Why three Green functions appear in the conditioned density

In the conditioned-density problem one has two different spectral tasks. The first task is to impose the index constraint below the threshold
x
. This requires the two boundary values

z_{-}=x-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz_{+}=x+i\epsilon\,.

(1170)

The second task is to measure the density at a possibly different spectral point
\lambda
. This requires the probe parameter

z_{0}=\lambda-i\eta\,.

(1171)

Therefore a cavity message must carry the three components

\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}=\left(G_{i\to j}^{-},G_{i\to j}^{+},G_{i\to j}^{0}\right)\,.

(1172)

For example, consider the two-site graph

\boldsymbol{A}=\begin{pmatrix}0&J\\
J&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(1173)

Since each vertex is a leaf in the cavity graph,

G_{1\to 2}^{a}=\frac{1}{z_{a}}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{2\to 1}^{a}=\frac{1}{z_{a}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pta\in\{-,+,0\}\,.

(1174)

The full Green functions are

G_{1}^{a}=G_{2}^{a}=\frac{1}{z_{a}-J^{2}/z_{a}}=\frac{z_{a}}{z_{a}^{2}-J^{2}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pta\in\{-,+,0\}\,.

(1175)

The pair
(G^{-},G^{+})
determines the phase of the determinant at the threshold
x
, while
G^{0}
gives the local contribution to the density at
\lambda
. Thus the triple message is not an arbitrary complication: it is the minimal object that simultaneously enforces the index constraint and measures the conditioned density.

The local decomposition of the determinant makes the tilted measure explicit. On a tree one has

\Delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}G_{i}(z)^{-1}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E}\left[1-J_{ij}^{2}G_{i\to j}(z)G_{j\to i}(z)\right]^{-1}\,.

(1176)

Example 14.5 : Bethe determinant identity for a two-site graph

For the weighted two-site graph

\boldsymbol{A}=\begin{pmatrix}0&J\\
J&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(1177)

the exact determinant is

\Delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=z^{2}-J^{2}\,.

(1178)

The cavity Green functions are

G_{1\to 2}(z)=\frac{1}{z}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{2\to 1}(z)=\frac{1}{z}\,.

(1179)

The full local Green functions are

G_{1}(z)=G_{2}(z)=\frac{1}{z-J^{2}/z}=\frac{z}{z^{2}-J^{2}}\,.

(1180)

Therefore the product of site contributions is

\prod_{i=1}^{2}G_{i}(z)^{-1}=\left(\frac{z^{2}-J^{2}}{z}\right)^{2}\,.

(1181)

The edge correction is

\left[1-J^{2}G_{1\to 2}(z)G_{2\to 1}(z)\right]^{-1}=\left[1-\frac{J^{2}}{z^{2}}\right]^{-1}=\frac{z^{2}}{z^{2}-J^{2}}\,.

(1182)

Multiplying site and edge terms gives

\left(\frac{z^{2}-J^{2}}{z}\right)^{2}\frac{z^{2}}{z^{2}-J^{2}}=z^{2}-J^{2}\,,

(1183)

which is the exact determinant. This example explains why the index decomposition has site terms minus edge terms: the determinant itself factorizes into site contributions corrected by edge overcounting factors.

Let us indicate the derivation. For a branch rooted at
i
with the edge to
j
removed, the Schur complement gives

G_{i\to j}(z)^{-1}=z-D_{i}-\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z).

(1184)

The determinant of the same branch is the product of the determinants of the neighboring branches times the Schur complement (
1184
). Iterating this identity over a tree gives (
1176
). The edge factors remove the overcounting produced by multiplying all full site contributions.

Substituting (
1176
) into (
1149
) gives

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)=N+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\kappa_{i}-\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}\kappa_{ij}\,,

(1185)

with site contribution

\kappa_{i}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log(G_{i}^{-})^{-1}-\log(G_{i}^{+})^{-1}\right]\,,

(1186)

and edge contribution

\kappa_{ij}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log\left(1-J_{ij}^{2}G_{i\to j}^{-}G_{j\to i}^{-}\right)-\log\left(1-J_{ij}^{2}G_{i\to j}^{+}G_{j\to i}^{+}\right)\right]\,.

(1187)

The logarithms in (
1186
) and (
1187
) must be evaluated with branches consistent with the determinant phase in (
1149
). The individual site and edge phases are branch-dependent, while the Bethe combination in (
1185
) is the integer index fixed by the determinant. Equivalently,

\exp\left[s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)\right]=e^{sN}\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{s\kappa_{i}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E}e^{-s\kappa_{ij}}\,.

(1188)

This formula is important. It says that the matrix ensemble conditioned on an atypical index becomes, at the Bethe level, a graphical model whose local weights are phases of cavity determinants at
z_{x}^{-}
and
z_{x}^{+}
. The probe resolvent
G^{0}
is then propagated through this biased graphical model.

Figure
7
summarizes the separation between the conditioning threshold, the probe spectral parameter, and the three-sector cavity message used to compute the conditioned density.

\displaystyle G^{a}_{i\to j}=\left[z_{a}-D_{i}-\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}J_{i\ell}^{2}G^{a}_{\ell\to i}\right]^{-1},\hskip 16.09991ptz_{-}=z_{x}^{-},\hskip 8.04996ptz_{+}=z_{x}^{+},\hskip 8.04996ptz_{0}=z_{\lambda},\hskip 8.04996pta\in\{-,+,0\}

Figure 7:
Threshold/probe separation and three-sector messages for the conditioned spectral density. The threshold
x
fixes the index constraint through the two determinant boundary values
z_{x}^{-}
and
z_{x}^{+}
, while the spectral parameter
\lambda
is a separate probe used to measure the density. In the cavity formulation each directed edge therefore carries the triple
(G^{-},G^{+},G^{0})
: the first two sectors define the tilted Bethe measure, and the third sector is averaged in that tilted measure to obtain the conditioned density.

Let
\mathbb{P}_{s}
denote the replica-symmetric tilted law of the message triples (
1165
). This law is obtained from the usual population dynamics, but with the site and edge weights in (
1188
) included in the Bethe measure. The conditioned density is

\rho_{x}(\lambda|k(s))=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\int dG^{-}dG^{+}dG^{0}\mathcal{P}_{\rm site}^{(s)}(G^{-},G^{+},G^{0})G^{0}\,,

(1189)

where
\mathcal{P}_{\rm site}^{(s)}
is the tilted distribution of full site Green-function triples. More explicitly, for a vertex of degree
m
, a full site triple is generated from incoming cavity triples by

G^{a}=\frac{1}{z_{a}-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{m}J_{r}^{2}G_{r}^{a}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pta\in\{-,+,0\}\,,

(1190)

and the tilted law weights the generated configuration by the index contribution

e^{s\kappa_{\rm site}}=\exp\left\{\frac{s}{2\pi i}\left[\log(G^{-})^{-1}-\log(G^{+})^{-1}\right]\right\}\,,

(1191)

together with the corresponding edge corrections

e^{-s\kappa_{\rm edge}}=\exp\left\{-\frac{s}{2\pi i}\left[\log\left(1-J^{2}G_{i\to j}^{-}G_{j\to i}^{-}\right)-\log\left(1-J^{2}G_{i\to j}^{+}G_{j\to i}^{+}\right)\right]\right\}\,.

(1192)

Equations (
1167
)–(
1189
) are the practical cavity formulation of the conditioned spectral density. At
s=0
, the weights (
1191
) and (
1192
) are equal to one, and the ordinary spectral-density population dynamics is recovered. For
s\neq 0
, neighborhoods that increase or decrease the number of eigenvalues below
x
are statistically favored according to the sign and magnitude of
s
.

The same construction applies to diluted Wishart matrices, with the ordinary graph replaced by the bipartite factor graph. Let

\boldsymbol{W}=\frac{1}{d}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\,.

(1193)

For every directed variable-to-factor edge
i\to\mu
we introduce

\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to\mu}=\left(G_{i\to\mu}^{-},G_{i\to\mu}^{+},G_{i\to\mu}^{0}\right)\,,

(1194)

and for every factor-to-variable edge
\mu\to i
we introduce the corresponding self-energy triple

\boldsymbol{U}_{\mu\to i}=\left(U_{\mu\to i}^{-},U_{\mu\to i}^{+},U_{\mu\to i}^{0}\right)\,.

(1195)

The local recursions are, for
a\in\{-,+,0\}
,

U_{\mu\to i}^{a}=\frac{(\xi_{i}^{\mu})^{2}}{1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}(\xi_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}^{a}}\,,

(1196)

and

G_{i\to\mu}^{a}=\frac{1}{z_{a}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i\setminus\mu}U_{\nu\to i}^{a}}\,.

(1197)

The full local Green functions are

G_{i}^{a}=\frac{1}{z_{a}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i}U_{\nu\to i}^{a}}\,.

(1198)

The index decomposition now contains variable, factor, and edge terms:

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(x)=N+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\kappa_{i}+\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\kappa_{\mu}-\sum_{(i,\mu)}\kappa_{i\mu}.

(1199)

Example 14.6 : Conditioning in a one-factor Wishart example

Let

\boldsymbol{X}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}\\
\xi_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptd=1\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\boldsymbol{W}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\,.

(1200)

The eigenvalues are

0\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}\,.

(1201)

If the conditioning threshold satisfies

0<x<\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}\,,

(1202)

then

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(x)=1\,.

(1203)

The empirical density is

\rho_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\delta(\lambda)+\frac{1}{2}\delta\left(\lambda-\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2}\right)\,.

(1204)

Therefore, with the convention that the zero mode is included in the count below any positive threshold,

\int_{-\infty}^{x}d\lambda\rho_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\,,

(1205)

which agrees with
\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(x)/N=1/2
. In larger diluted Wishart ensembles, the same statement becomes nontrivial: the tilted cavity equations bias the bipartite graph so that the resulting local density satisfies the prescribed index constraint.

The variable contribution is

\kappa_{i}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log(G_{i}^{-})^{-1}-\log(G_{i}^{+})^{-1}\right]\,,

(1206)

the factor contribution is

\kappa_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log R_{\mu}^{-}-\log R_{\mu}^{+}\right]\,,

(1207)

with

R_{\mu}^{a}=1-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i\in\partial\mu}(\xi_{i}^{\mu})^{2}G_{i\to\mu}^{a}\,,\hskip 22.99988pta\in\{-,+\}\,,

(1208)

and the edge contribution is

\kappa_{i\mu}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log E_{i\mu}^{-}-\log E_{i\mu}^{+}\right]\,,

(1209)

with

E_{i\mu}^{a}=1-\frac{1}{d}G_{i\to\mu}^{a}U_{\mu\to i}^{a}\,,\hskip 22.99988pta\in\{-,+\}\,.

(1210)

The logarithms in (
1206
)–(
1209
) must be evaluated with branches consistent with the determinant phase. The individual variable, factor, and edge phases are branch-dependent, while the Bethe combination in (
1199
) gives the index. The conditioned Wishart density is then

\rho_{x}^{(\rm W)}(\lambda|k(s))=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\int dG^{-}dG^{+}dG^{0}\mathcal{P}_{{\rm site},\,{\rm W}}^{(s)}(G^{-},G^{+},G^{0})G^{0}\,.

(1211)

The meaning of (
1211
) is exactly the same as in the sparse symmetric case: the ordinary local density is averaged in the ensemble tilted by the index below
x
. The only difference is that the local graph is bipartite and the Bethe determinant contains variable, factor, and edge contributions.

We can now summarize the calculation in a way that is useful for implementation. One first fixes the conditioning threshold
x
and the desired fraction
k
. Instead of imposing
k
directly, one solves the tilted large-deviation problem for a value of
s
such that

{}_{x}^{\prime}(s)=k\,.

(1212)

One then propagates, in the corresponding tilted population, an additional probe resolvent at
z_{\lambda}=\lambda-i\eta
. The conditioned density is obtained from the imaginary part of the full probe Green function. In equations, for sparse symmetric matrices,

\rho_{x}(\lambda|k)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\left\langle G^{0}_{\rm site}\right\rangle_{s(k)}\,.

(1213)

For diluted Wishart matrices the same formula holds with the bipartite site distribution:

\rho_{x}^{(\rm W)}(\lambda|k)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\left\langle G^{0}_{\rm variable}\right\rangle_{s(k)}\,.

(1214)

The notation
\langle\cdot\rangle_{s(k)}
means that the average is not taken in the original matrix ensemble, but in the tilted ensemble whose typical index is
k
.

Several checks follow immediately. First, setting
s=0
gives
k=k_{\star}(x)
and the tilted measure becomes the original measure. Thus (
1213
) reduces to the ordinary spectral density. Second, integrating the conditioned density below the threshold gives

\int_{-\infty}^{x}d\lambda\rho_{x}(\lambda|k(s))=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\left\langle\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)\right\rangle_{s}={}_{x}^{\prime}(s)=k(s)\,,

(1215)

which verifies the conditioning. Third, integrating over all
\lambda
gives one, because every matrix in the tilted ensemble has a normalized empirical spectral measure. Fourth, if the threshold is below the entire spectrum or above the entire spectrum, the tilted ensemble is trivial: it conditions on an event that is already deterministic, and the conditioned density is again the ordinary density.

The physical interpretation is different from the Coulomb-gas picture. In an invariant ensemble, imposing an atypical index forces a collective rearrangement of a gas of strongly repelling eigenvalues. In a sparse non-invariant ensemble, imposing an atypical index biases the distribution of local graph environments and local resolvent messages. The conditioned density is therefore obtained by averaging local spectral weights in a biased graphical ensemble. This is why the conditioned density of sparse matrices can behave very differently from the conditioned density of invariant random matrices: the constraint is not implemented by moving a continuum of repelling charges, but by changing the relative statistical weight of local structures that contribute eigenvalues below the threshold.

The main lesson of this section is that the conditioned spectral density is not an independent object requiring a completely new method. It is the ordinary resolvent density evaluated in the large-deviation ensemble of the index number. The determinant identity (
1149
) creates the tilted ensemble; the Edwards–Jones identity (
1159
) inserts a probe resolvent; and the cavity method computes the resulting local Green function in the tilted graphical measure. This is the natural extension of the spectral-density and index-number calculations to the full spectrum conditioned on an atypical spectral count.

Exercises for this section

Exercise 14.1 : Definition of the conditioned density.

Starting from

\rho_{x}(\lambda|k)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)\delta(\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)-Nk)}}{\overline{\delta(\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)-Nk)}}\,,

(1216)

show that

\int d\lambda\rho_{x}(\lambda|k)=1

(1217)

provided each empirical density is normalized.

Exercise 14.2 : Index constraint.

Using the same definition, prove that

\int_{-\infty}^{x}d\lambda\rho_{x}(\lambda|k)=k\,.

(1218)

What assumptions about eigenvalues exactly at the threshold are being made?

Exercise 14.3 : Typical density as a special case.

Let

k_{\star}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}d\lambda\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)}\,.

(1219)

Explain why one expects

\rho_{x}(\lambda|k_{\star})=\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)}

(1220)

when the conditioning imposes the typical value of the index, assuming the same convention for possible eigenvalues at the threshold
x
on both sides.

Exercise 14.4 : Formal Laplace representation of the constraint.

Using the formal large-deviation representation

\delta(\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)-Nk)=\int_{\gamma-i\infty}^{\gamma+i\infty}\frac{ds}{2\pi i}e^{s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)-sNk}\,,

(1221)

derive the saddle-point condition

{}_{x}^{\prime}(s)=k\,.

(1222)

Exercise 14.5 : Equivalence between hard conditioning and tilting.

Using the saddle-point calculation, show that

\rho_{x}(\lambda|k(s))=\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\langle\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)\right\rangle_{s}\,,

(1223)

where
\langle\cdots\rangle_{s}
denotes expectation in the tilted ensemble.

Exercise 14.6 : Determinant representation of the index.

Use, for
u\neq 0
and with eigenvalues at the threshold treated by the fixed endpoint convention,

\Theta(u)=1+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log(u-i\epsilon)-\log(u+i\epsilon)\right]

(1224)

to derive

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)=N+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\log\Delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x-i\epsilon)-\log\Delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x+i\epsilon)\right]\,.

(1225)

Exercise 14.7 : Probe resolvent.

Show that

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(\lambda)=-\frac{2}{\pi N}{\rm Im}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda-i\eta)\,.

(1226)

Explain why the conditioned density calculation needs both the threshold parameters
x\mp i\epsilon
and the probe parameter
\lambda-i\eta
.

Exercise 14.8 : Three-sector replica structure.

Explain why the joint generating function

\overline{e^{s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)}[Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{\lambda})]^{n}}

(1227)

contains two sectors associated with the index constraint and one additional sector associated with the density probe.

Exercise 14.9 : Triple cavity recursion.

Starting from the ordinary sparse symmetric cavity recursion, derive

G_{i\to j}^{a}=\frac{1}{z_{a}-D_{i}-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}^{a}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pta\in\{-,+,0\}\,.

(1228)

Explain why the three components are statistically coupled even though the recursion is componentwise.

Exercise 14.10 : Bethe determinant on a two-site graph.

For

\boldsymbol{A}=\begin{pmatrix}0&J\\
J&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(1229)

verify the Bethe determinant identity

\Delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\prod_{i}G_{i}(z)^{-1}\prod_{\{i,j\}}\left[1-J^{2}G_{i\to j}(z)G_{j\to i}(z)\right]^{-1}\,.

(1230)

Exercise 14.11 : Index decomposition.

Starting from the Bethe determinant, derive

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)=N+\sum_{i}\kappa_{i}-\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}\kappa_{ij}\,.

(1231)

Give explicit formulas for
\kappa_{i}
and
\kappa_{ij}
.

Exercise 14.12 : Local tilt factorization.

Show that

e^{s\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)}=e^{sN}\prod_{i}e^{s\kappa_{i}}\prod_{\{i,j\}}e^{-s\kappa_{ij}}\,.

(1232)

Explain why this is the graphical origin of the tilted cavity measure.

Exercise 14.13 : Conditioned density formula.

Using the tilted site distribution, derive

\rho_{x}(\lambda|k(s))=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\int dG^{-}dG^{+}dG^{0}\mathcal{P}_{\rm site}^{(s)}(G^{-},G^{+},G^{0})G^{0}\,.

(1233)

Exercise 14.14 : Wishart triple messages.

For the diluted Wishart case, derive the triple recursions

U_{\mu\to i}^{a}=\frac{(\xi_{i}^{\mu})^{2}}{1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}(\xi_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}^{a}}\,,

(1234)

and

G_{i\to\mu}^{a}=\frac{1}{z_{a}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i\setminus\mu}U_{\nu\to i}^{a}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pta\in\{-,+,0\}\,.

(1235)

Exercise 14.15 : Wishart Bethe index decomposition.

Derive the bipartite decomposition

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(x)=N+\sum_{i}\kappa_{i}+\sum_{\mu}\kappa_{\mu}-\sum_{(i,\mu)}\kappa_{i\mu}\,.

(1236)

Explain why factor terms appear in addition to variable and edge terms.

Exercise 14.16 : Conditioned Wishart density.

Show that the conditioned Wishart density can be written as

\rho_{x}^{(\rm W)}(\lambda|k(s))=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\left\langle G_{\rm variable}^{0}\right\rangle_{s}\,.

(1237)

Exercise 14.17 : Trivial conditioning.

Show that if
x
is below the entire spectrum or above the entire spectrum, then the index is deterministic and the conditioned density is the ordinary density.

Exercise 14.18 : Invariant versus sparse mechanism.

Explain in words why, in an invariant ensemble, conditioning the index suggests a Coulomb-gas constrained minimization, while in a sparse non-invariant ensemble it suggests a tilted distribution of local graph environments.

Exercise 14.19 : Programming exercise: rejection conditioning.

Fix a list of system sizes
N
, a mean degree
c=O(1)
, a number
M
of independent samples for each
N
, a threshold
x
, an endpoint convention for eigenvalues exactly at
x
, and a common density-estimation convention, such as a histogram bin width or a Lorentzian regulator. Generate sparse Erdős–Rényi adjacency matrices
\boldsymbol{A}
with zero diagonal entries,
A_{ij}=A_{ji}
, and

{\rm Prob}(A_{ij}=1)=\frac{c}{N}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Prob}(A_{ij}=0)=1-\frac{c}{N}\,,\hskip 22.99988pti<j\,.

(1238)

Compute their spectra. For each value of
N
, choose an integer target count
K_{\rm target}
, define
k_{N}=K_{\rm target}/N
, and retain only samples satisfying

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)=K_{\rm target}\,.

(1239)

Estimate the empirical conditioned density from the retained samples and compare it with the unconditioned density using the same density-estimation convention. Report
N
,
c
,
M
,
x
, the endpoint convention,
K_{\rm target}
, the number of retained samples, and the bin width or regulator.

Exercise 14.20 : Programming exercise: soft tilting.

Using the same samples and the same density-estimation convention as in the previous exercise, let
\rho_{m}(\lambda)
be the empirical spectral density of sample
m
and let
\mathcal{K}_{m}(x)
be its index below
x
. Fix a grid of real values of
s
and estimate the tilted density

\rho_{s}(\lambda)=\frac{\sum_{m}e^{s\mathcal{K}_{m}(x)}\rho_{m}(\lambda)}{\sum_{m}e^{s\mathcal{K}_{m}(x)}}\,.

(1240)

For each
s
, estimate the tilted mean intensive index

k(s)=\frac{1}{N}\frac{\sum_{m}e^{s\mathcal{K}_{m}(x)}\mathcal{K}_{m}(x)}{\sum_{m}e^{s\mathcal{K}_{m}(x)}}\,.

Compare
\rho_{s}(\lambda)
with the hard-conditioned density at nearby values of
k_{N}
when enough retained samples are available. Report the
s
grid and an effective sample-size diagnostic for the tilted weights.

Exercise 14.21 : Programming exercise: conditioned Wishart spectra.

Fix a list of system sizes
N
, an aspect ratio
\alpha>0
such that
P=N/\alpha
is an integer for each
N
, a dilution

, a number

of independent samples for each parameter set, a nonzero-weight distribution
p_{\xi}
with zero mean and unit variance, a positive threshold

, an endpoint convention for eigenvalues exactly at
