, and a common density-estimation convention. Generate diluted Wishart matrices from sparse rectangular matrices

X_{i}^{\mu}=B_{i}^{\mu}\xi_{i}^{\mu}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Prob}(B_{i}^{\mu}=1)=\frac{d}{N}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Prob}(B_{i}^{\mu}=0)=1-\frac{d}{N}\,,

(1241)

with independent nonzero weights
\xi_{i}^{\mu}
drawn from
p_{\xi}
, and form

\boldsymbol{W}=\frac{1}{d}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\,.

(1242)

For each sample, compute
\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(x)
and the empirical spectral density. Compare conditioned densities for samples with unusually many and unusually few eigenvalues below
x
, using either explicit count windows or selected quantiles of the empirical distribution of
\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{W}}(x)
. Report
N
,
P
,
\alpha
,
d
,
M
,
p_{\xi}
,
x
, the endpoint convention, the count windows or quantiles used for conditioning, and the density-estimation convention.

15
Number statistics of non-Hermitian random matrices

The Hermitian index number counts eigenvalues on one side of a point on the real axis, or inside an interval. For non-Hermitian matrices the eigenvalues are complex, and the analogous observable is the number of eigenvalues inside a two-dimensional domain of the complex plane. This section develops the corresponding formalism explicitly. The main point is that the number of eigenvalues inside a domain bounded by a contour can be written either as a winding number of the characteristic polynomial or as a boundary integral of the Hermitized logarithmic potential. These two representations are equivalent. The first one is the direct non-Hermitian analogue of the argument-principle formula in complex analysis; the second one is the form naturally adapted to Gaussian integrals, Hermitization, and cavity methods. The statistical-mechanics approach to this problem was developed in
[
75
]
, building on the Hermitization ideas used in non-Hermitian random matrix theory
[
33
,
29
,
84
]
and on the cavity treatment of sparse non-Hermitian matrices
[
77
,
47
]
.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}
be a general
N\times N
real or complex non-Hermitian matrix, with eigenvalues

z_{1},\ldots,z_{N}\in\mathbb{C}\,.

(1243)

The empirical spectral density in the complex plane is

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta^{(2)}(z-z_{i})\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz=x+iy\,.

(1244)

Let
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{C}
be a bounded domain with positively oriented boundary
\partial\mathcal{D}=\Gamma
, and assume for the moment that no eigenvalue lies exactly on
\Gamma
. The number of eigenvalues in
\mathcal{D}
is

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{z_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}=N\int_{\mathcal{D}}d^{2}z\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)\,.

(1245)

Example 15.1 : Counting eigenvalues in a domain

Consider the diagonal non-Hermitian matrix

\boldsymbol{A}=\begin{pmatrix}1+i&0&0\\
0&-1+\frac{i}{2}&0\\
0&0&2-2i\end{pmatrix}\,.

(1246)

Its eigenvalues are

z_{1}=1+i\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz_{2}=-1+\frac{i}{2}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz_{3}=2-2i\,.

(1247)

Let
\mathcal{D}_{R}
be the disk of radius
R
centered at the origin,

\mathcal{D}_{R}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|<R\}\,.

(1248)

The moduli of the eigenvalues are

|z_{1}|=\sqrt{2}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt|z_{2}|=\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{4}}=\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt|z_{3}|=\sqrt{8}=2\sqrt{2}\,.

(1249)

Therefore, if

\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2}<R<\sqrt{2}\,,

(1250)

only
z_{2}
lies in the disk and

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D}_{R})=1\,.

(1251)

If

\sqrt{2}<R<2\sqrt{2}\,,

(1252)

then
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
lie in the disk, and

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D}_{R})=2\,.

(1253)

Finally, if

R>2\sqrt{2},

(1254)

all three eigenvalues lie in the disk, so

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D}_{R})=3\,.

(1255)

In terms of the empirical density,

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\frac{1}{3}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\delta^{(2)}(z-z_{i})\,,

(1256)

the intensive count is

\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D}_{R})=\int_{\mathcal{D}_{R}}d^{2}z\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)\,.

(1257)

This finite example fixes the meaning of the observable: in the non-Hermitian problem the spectral count is a two-dimensional count of eigenvalues inside a domain, not a one-dimensional count across an interval.

The intensive number is

k_{\mathcal{D}}=\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})\,.

(1258)

For a fixed domain, the first task is to compute the distribution of
\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})
over the random matrix ensemble. The mean count is
N\int_{\mathcal{D}}d^{2}z\,\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)}
, or equivalently the mean intensive count is obtained by integrating the average density. The variance, higher cumulants, and large deviations require a genuine number-statistics calculation.

The most elementary representation follows from the argument principle. Define the characteristic polynomial

p_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\det(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}(z-z_{i})\,.

(1259)

For
z\notin\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{N}\}
,

\partial_{z}\log p_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)={\rm Tr}(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{z-z_{i}}\,.

(1260)

Therefore

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}dz\partial_{z}\log p_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\oint_{\Gamma}\frac{dz}{z-z_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{z_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}=\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})\,.

(1261)

Equivalently,

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}dz{\rm Tr}(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\,.

(1262)

This is the non-Hermitian counterpart of the Hermitian formula expressing the number of eigenvalues in an interval as an integral of the resolvent discontinuity. The difference is geometric: the Hermitian count uses the boundary values across a segment of the real axis, while the non-Hermitian count uses a contour integral in the complex plane.

Example 15.2 : The argument principle for two eigenvalues

Let

p(z)=(z-a)(z-b)\,,

(1263)

and let
\Gamma
be a positively oriented simple closed contour. Assume that
a
lies inside
\Gamma
and
b
lies outside
\Gamma
. Then

\frac{d}{dz}\log p(z)=\frac{1}{z-a}+\frac{1}{z-b}\,.

(1264)

The argument-principle integral is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}dz\frac{d}{dz}\log p(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}\frac{dz}{z-a}+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}\frac{dz}{z-b}

\displaystyle=1+0=1\,.

(1265)

Thus the contour integral counts exactly the zeros of
p
inside the contour.

For a non-Hermitian matrix, the characteristic polynomial is

p_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\det(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})\,,

(1266)

and its zeros are the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{A}
. Therefore

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}dz\partial_{z}\log p_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)

(1267)

counts the eigenvalues inside
\Gamma
. This is the direct non-Hermitian analogue of counting Hermitian eigenvalues in an interval by integrating the resolvent discontinuity.

Let us make the winding interpretation explicit. Parametrize the contour by

z=z(\theta)\,,\hskip 22.99988pt0\leq\theta\leq 2\pi\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz(2\pi)=z(0)\,,

(1268)

with positive orientation. Then

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\frac{d}{d\theta}\log p_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z(\theta))\,.

(1269)

Although
p_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z(2\pi))=p_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z(0))
, the logarithm need not return to the same branch. The change in the continuous argument of
p_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z(\theta))
is
2\pi
times the number of zeros enclosed by the contour:

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\Delta_{\Gamma}\arg p_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)\,.

(1270)

This is why a naive product of determinant ratios around a closed discrete contour would appear to cancel if one ignored the branch of the logarithm. The observable is not the final value of the determinant, but the winding of its phase along the contour.

Example 15.3 : The winding number for a single eigenvalue

Let

p(z)=z-a

(1271)

and let
\Gamma
be the circle

z(\theta)=a+Re^{i\theta}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt0\leq\theta\leq 2\pi\,.

(1272)

Then

p(z(\theta))=Re^{i\theta}\,.

(1273)

As
\theta
goes from
0
to
2\pi
, the phase of
p(z(\theta))
increases by
2\pi
. Therefore

\frac{1}{2\pi}\Delta_{\Gamma}\arg p(z)=1\,.

(1274)

The contour winds once around the zero.

Now take instead a circle not enclosing
a
, for example

z(\theta)=Re^{i\theta}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptR<|a|\,.

(1275)

Then the curve
p(z(\theta))=Re^{i\theta}-a
is a circle centered at
-a
with radius
R
, and it does not enclose the origin. Its net change of argument is zero:

\Delta_{\Gamma}\arg p(z)=0\,.

(1276)

This is the geometric content of the argument principle. The number statistic counts how many times the characteristic polynomial winds around the origin as the spectral parameter moves along the boundary of the domain.

The generating function of the number statistic is

\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D},N}(s)=\overline{\exp\left[s\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})\right]}\,,

(1277)

and the scaled cumulant-generating function, in the finite-connectivity setting emphasized in these notes, is

{}_{\mathcal{D}}(s)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D},N}(s)\,.

(1278)

When a large-deviation principle with speed
N
holds, for admissible values of
k
along a finite-
N
sequence, or equivalently for small count windows around
k
, one writes

{\rm Prob}\left[\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})=k\right]\asymp\exp\left[-N\Phi_{\mathcal{D}}(k)\right]\,,

(1279)

with

\Phi_{\mathcal{D}}(k)=\sup_{s}\left\{sk-{}_{\mathcal{D}}(s)\right\}

(1280)

whenever the Legendre-Fenchel transform is regular. The derivatives of
D
at the origin give the cumulants per degree of freedom:

{}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(0)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\overline{\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime\prime}(0)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}{\rm Var}\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})\,.

(1281)

For dense invariant non-Hermitian ensembles, macroscopic number large deviations may have a different speed because of Coulomb-gas eigenvalue interactions, as happens in Hermitian invariant ensembles
[
20
,
42
]
. In sparse and other non-invariant finite-connectivity ensembles, the natural statistical-mechanics object is instead a tilted graphical measure, and the speed
N
is the analogue of the Hermitian sparse index problem.

Although the argument-principle formula is conceptually simple, it is not the most convenient starting point for Gaussian integral methods. We now reuse Hermitization in a different role: the same doubled determinant used earlier for non-Hermitian densities is integrated through its normal derivative along the counting boundary. The reason is that the non-Hermitian resolvent is unstable near the spectrum, and ordinary bosonic Gaussian integrals involving
z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}
are not convergent in general. Hermitization cures this. Define, for
\eta>0
,

\boldsymbol{H}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)=(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})(z^{*}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger})+\eta^{2}\boldsymbol{I}\,.

(1282)

Then

\log\det\boldsymbol{H}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log\left(|z-z_{i}|^{2}+\eta^{2}\right)

(1283)

only when
\boldsymbol{A}
is normal. For a general non-normal matrix, the singular values of
z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}
replace the eigenvalue distances. Nevertheless, Girko’s Hermitization principle states that the regularized logarithmic potential

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})=\log\det\left[(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})(z^{*}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger})+\eta^{2}\boldsymbol{I}\right]

(1284)

recovers the eigenvalue density through

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi N}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\partial_{z^{*}}\partial_{z}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})\,.

(1285)

Since

\Delta_{z}=\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{y}^{2}=4\partial_{z^{*}}\partial_{z}\,,

(1286)

this can also be written as

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\frac{1}{4\pi N}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\Delta_{z}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})\,.

(1287)

Integrating (
1287
) over
\mathcal{D}
gives another representation of the number:

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\int_{\mathcal{D}}d^{2}z\,\Delta_{z}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})\,.

(1288)

Using Green’s theorem,

\int_{\mathcal{D}}d^{2}z\Delta_{z}f(z,z^{*})=\oint_{\Gamma}ds\partial_{n}f(z,z^{*})\,,

(1289)

where
\partial_{n}
denotes the outward normal derivative on the boundary, we obtain the boundary formula

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\oint_{\Gamma}ds\partial_{n}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})\,.

(1290)

This equation is the Hermitized version of the argument principle. It is especially useful for statistical mechanics because
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}
is the logarithm of a positive regularized determinant.

Example 15.4 : Boundary formula for a single eigenvalue in a disk

Consider the
1\times 1
matrix
\boldsymbol{A}=(a)
and the disk

\mathcal{D}_{R}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z-a|<R\}\,.

(1291)

The answer should be

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D}_{R})=1\,.

(1292)

Let us verify this from the Hermitized boundary formula. For one eigenvalue,

\Phi_{\eta}(z,z^{*})=\log\left(|z-a|^{2}+\eta^{2}\right)\,.

(1293)

On the boundary of the disk, write

z=a+Re^{i\theta}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptds=Rd\theta\,.

(1294)

The outward normal derivative is the radial derivative with respect to
R
:

\partial_{n}\Phi_{\eta}=\frac{\partial}{\partial R}\log(R^{2}+\eta^{2})=\frac{2R}{R^{2}+\eta^{2}}\,.

(1295)

Therefore

\frac{1}{4\pi}\oint_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{R}}ds\,\partial_{n}\Phi_{\eta}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}Rd\theta\frac{2R}{R^{2}+\eta^{2}}=\frac{R^{2}}{R^{2}+\eta^{2}}\,.

(1296)

Taking
\eta\downarrow 0
gives

\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\frac{R^{2}}{R^{2}+\eta^{2}}=1\,,

(1297)

as expected.

If instead the disk does not enclose
a
, the logarithmic potential is harmonic inside the disk in the limit
\eta\downarrow 0
, and the boundary integral gives zero. Thus the Hermitized boundary formula reproduces the same count as the argument principle.

To express this determinant as a Gaussian theory, introduce the
2N\times 2N
Hermitized block matrix

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)=\begin{pmatrix}i\eta\boldsymbol{I}&z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}\\
z^{*}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger}&i\eta\boldsymbol{I}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(1298)

Up to a
z
-independent phase,

\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)=\det\left[(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})(z^{*}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger})+\eta^{2}\boldsymbol{I}\right]\,.

(1299)

Thus

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})=\log\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)+\text{constant}\,,

(1300)

where the constant is independent of
z
and does not contribute to normal derivatives. Whenever
\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}
is used below in place of the positive Hermitized determinant, this
z
-independent phase is understood to be fixed consistently. The logarithm of the determinant is generated by replicas through

\log\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}=\lim_{n\to 0}\frac{[\det(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}})]^{n}-1}{n}\,,

(1301)

or, equivalently, by Gaussian and Grassmann integral representations depending on the sign of the determinant power. The important point for the present lecture notes is that the number statistic is a functional of a family of Hermitized Gaussian theories indexed by points on the boundary
\Gamma
.

The boundary representation can be discretized in a way that makes the replica structure explicit. Let
z_{m}
,
m=1,\ldots,M
, be points along
\Gamma
, with arc-length increments
\Delta s_{m}
, and let
n_{m}
be the outward unit normal. For a small normal displacement
\delta>0
, define

z_{m}^{\rm out}=z_{m}+\frac{\delta}{2}n_{m}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz_{m}^{\rm in}=z_{m}-\frac{\delta}{2}n_{m}\,.

(1302)

Then

\partial_{n}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z_{m},z_{m}^{*})=\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z_{m}^{\rm out},(z_{m}^{\rm out})^{*})-\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z_{m}^{\rm in},(z_{m}^{\rm in})^{*})}{\delta}\,.

(1303)

Substituting this into (
1290
) gives

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})=\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\lim_{M\to\infty}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\Delta s_{m}}{4\pi\delta}\Big[\log\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{m}^{\rm out},\eta)-\log\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{m}^{\rm in},\eta)\Big]\,.

(1304)

Example 15.5 : Discretizing a circular boundary

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a disk of radius
R
centered at the origin. A convenient boundary discretization is

z_{m}=Re^{2\pi im/M}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptm=0,\ldots,M-1\,.

(1305)

The outward unit normal is

n_{m}=e^{2\pi im/M}\,,

(1306)

and the arc-length increment is

\Delta s_{m}=\frac{2\pi R}{M}\,.

(1307)

For a small normal displacement
\delta>0
, the outer and inner points are

z_{m}^{\rm out}=\left(R+\frac{\delta}{2}\right)e^{2\pi im/M}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz_{m}^{\rm in}=\left(R-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)e^{2\pi im/M}\,.

(1308)

The boundary approximation to the number statistic is

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}\frac{\Delta s_{m}}{\delta}\left[\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z_{m}^{\rm out},(z_{m}^{\rm out})^{*})-\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z_{m}^{\rm in},(z_{m}^{\rm in})^{*})\right]\,.

(1309)

Substituting
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}=\log\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}
gives precisely the product of outer determinant powers divided by inner determinant powers that appears in the replicated boundary representation. Thus the many-replica object in the non-Hermitian number-statistics calculation is just the discretized version of a normal derivative along the counting contour.

Exponentiating, one obtains a product of powers of Hermitized determinants:

\displaystyle\exp\left[s\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\lim_{M\to\infty}\prod_{m=1}^{M}\left[\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{m}^{\rm out},\eta)\right]^{s\Delta s_{m}/(4\pi\delta)}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\prod_{m=1}^{M}\left[\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{m}^{\rm in},\eta)\right]^{-s\Delta s_{m}/(4\pi\delta)}\,.

(1310)

This is the non-Hermitian analogue of the determinant powers used in the Hermitian index problem. The replicas are now attached to boundary points of a two-dimensional domain rather than to two endpoints of an interval. Formula (
1310
) is formal until the determinant powers are first taken as integers and then analytically continued, but it makes the structure of the calculation clear.

Rather than carrying out the boundary-replica saddle explicitly, we now translate the same determinant structure into cavity language for sparse non-Hermitian matrices. The local recursion is the same Hermitized recursion used for the non-Hermitian spectral density, but the message now has to be evaluated along the counting boundary. Consider a sparse matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
with pairwise support

A_{ij}=C_{ij}J_{ij}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptA_{ij}\neq A_{ji}\hskip 11.49994pt\text{in general}\,.

(1311)

Here the set of edges
E
denotes the symmetrized support: an unordered pair
\{i,j\}
belongs to
E
whenever at least one of
A_{ij}
or
A_{ji}
is nonzero. For each
z
and
\eta
, the Hermitized cavity message on a directed edge
i\to j
is a
2\times 2
matrix

\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}(z,\eta)\,.

(1312)

With

\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}(z,\eta)=\begin{pmatrix}i\eta&z-A_{ii}\\
z^{*}-A_{ii}^{*}&i\eta\end{pmatrix}\,,

(1313)

and

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}=\begin{pmatrix}0&A_{ij}\\
A_{ji}^{*}&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(1314)

the message recursion on a tree is

\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}(z,\eta)=\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}(z,\eta)-\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}\boldsymbol{G}_{\ell\to i}(z,\eta)\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}^{\dagger}\right]^{-1}\,.

(1315)

With this definition,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{ji}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}^{\dagger}
; the two orientations of an unordered edge are therefore adjoint block couplings. The full local message is

\boldsymbol{G}_{i}(z,\eta)=\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}(z,\eta)-\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}\boldsymbol{G}_{\ell\to i}(z,\eta)\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}^{\dagger}\right]^{-1}\,.

(1316)

These are the same equations used for the non-Hermitian spectral density, but now they must be solved for all points on the boundary of
\mathcal{D}
, or for a discretization of that boundary.

The local determinant also has a Bethe factorization. On a tree, the determinant of the Hermitized block matrix can be written as

\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\det\left[\boldsymbol{G}_{i}(z,\eta)^{-1}\right]\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E}\det\left[\boldsymbol{I}_{2}-\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}(z,\eta)\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}\boldsymbol{G}_{j\to i}(z,\eta)\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{ji}\right]^{-1}\,.

(1317)

This is the matrix-valued analogue of the scalar Bethe determinant identity used for Hermitian sparse matrices. To verify the structure, consider a two-vertex tree. The cavity messages are the inverses of the two local blocks. Multiplying the two full site Schur complements gives two copies of the edge determinant; the edge factor in (
1317
) removes one copy and leaves the exact determinant of the
4\times 4
block matrix.

Taking the logarithm of (
1317
) and substituting into (
1290
), the number of eigenvalues in
\mathcal{D}
becomes a sum of local boundary contributions:

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{i}(\mathcal{D})-\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}\nu_{ij}(\mathcal{D})\,,

(1318)

where

\nu_{i}(\mathcal{D})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\oint_{\Gamma}ds\partial_{n}\log\det\left[\boldsymbol{G}_{i}(z,\eta)^{-1}\right]\,,

(1319)

and

\nu_{ij}(\mathcal{D})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\oint_{\Gamma}ds\partial_{n}\log\det\left[\boldsymbol{I}_{2}-\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}(z,\eta)\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}\boldsymbol{G}_{j\to i}(z,\eta)\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{ji}\right]\,.

(1320)

This is the central local formula for non-Hermitian number statistics on sparse graphs. It says that the two-dimensional eigenvalue count is an extensive observable built from site and edge contributions, each of which is obtained by integrating the normal derivative of a Hermitized local determinant along the boundary of the domain. Note that the logarithms in (
1319
) and (
1320
) must be evaluated with branches, and with the
z
-independent phases in (
1299
), chosen consistently with the global Hermitized determinant in (
1300
). The individual site and edge boundary contributions are convention-dependent, while the Bethe combination in (
1318
) reproduces the integer count.

Example 15.6 : Local number decomposition on a two-site tree

Consider again a two-vertex sparse non-Hermitian matrix

\boldsymbol{A}=\begin{pmatrix}0&J\\
K&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(1321)

The Hermitized determinant factorization on a tree has the structure

\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)=\prod_{i=1}^{2}\det\left[\boldsymbol{G}_{i}(z,\eta)^{-1}\right]\det\left[\boldsymbol{I}_{2}-\boldsymbol{G}_{1\to 2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{12}\boldsymbol{G}_{2\to 1}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{21}\right]^{-1}\,.

(1322)

Taking the logarithm and applying the boundary operator

\frac{1}{4\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\oint_{\Gamma}ds\partial_{n}

(1323)

gives

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})=\nu_{1}(\mathcal{D})+\nu_{2}(\mathcal{D})-\nu_{12}(\mathcal{D})\,.

(1324)

The site terms count the spectral contribution suggested by the two full local Hermitized Green matrices, while the edge term removes the double counting of the interaction between the two vertices. This is the non-Hermitian analogue of the Bethe determinant decompositions used earlier for Hermitian interval counts.

The corresponding tilted ensemble is

P_{s}(\boldsymbol{A})=\frac{P(\boldsymbol{A})\exp\left[s\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})\right]}{\overline{\exp\left[s\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})\right]}}\,.

(1325)

Using (
1318
), the tilt factor becomes

\exp\left[s\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})\right]=\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{s\nu_{i}(\mathcal{D})}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E}e^{-s\nu_{ij}(\mathcal{D})}.

(1326)

Thus the number-statistics problem is a biased graphical model whose messages are not single matrices at one spectral parameter, but functions of the boundary point
z\in\Gamma
. In a discretized implementation, a message is the collection

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{G}}_{i\to j}=\left\{\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}(z_{m}^{\rm out},\eta),\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}(z_{m}^{\rm in},\eta)\right\}_{m=1}^{M}\,.

(1327)

The unbiased update is obtained by applying (
1315
) independently at every boundary point. The tilted update uses the same local maps but samples neighborhoods with weights built from the local contributions (
1319
) and (
1320
), equivalently from the factorization (
1326
). At
s=0
the tilt disappears, and one recovers the ordinary unconditioned Hermitized population dynamics evaluated at the boundary points; the mean count is then obtained by the boundary formula, while the pointwise density is obtained by the usual Wirtinger derivative.

Figure
8
summarizes the non-Hermitian number-statistics construction: the domain count is a winding number, Hermitization turns it into an inner–outer boundary determinant problem, and the sparse cavity formulation promotes each edge message to a boundary-indexed family of
2\times 2
Hermitized Green matrices.

\Gamma

\displaystyle\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})=\log\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)=\begin{pmatrix}i\eta\boldsymbol{I}&z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}\\
z^{*}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger}&i\eta\boldsymbol{I}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})\simeq\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{\Delta s_{m}}{\delta}\Big[\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z_{m}^{\rm out})-\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z_{m}^{\rm in})\Big]

\ell_{1}
\ell_{2}
i
j
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell_{1}\to i}
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell_{2}\to i}
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{G}}_{i\to j}
j

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{G}}_{i\to j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}(z_{m}^{\rm out},\eta)\\[-2.84526pt]
\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}(z_{m}^{\rm in},\eta)\end{array}\right\}_{m=1}^{M}

m=1

\boldsymbol{G}^{\rm out}

\boldsymbol{G}^{\rm in}

m=2

\boldsymbol{G}^{\rm out}

\boldsymbol{G}^{\rm in}

\cdots

m=M

\boldsymbol{G}^{\rm out}

\boldsymbol{G}^{\rm in}

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})=\sum_{i}\nu_{i}(\mathcal{D})-\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}\nu_{ij}(\mathcal{D})

2\times 2

\displaystyle e^{s\mathcal{N}}=\prod_{i}e^{s\nu_{i}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E}e^{-s\nu_{ij}}

Figure 8:
Non-Hermitian number statistics as a boundary and Hermitized-cavity problem. Counting eigenvalues in a complex domain can be written as the winding of
p_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)
along
\Gamma=\partial\mathcal{D}
. Hermitization converts the same count into a normal derivative of the logarithmic determinant
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}
, evaluated by inner–outer boundary displacements. On a sparse graph, the Bethe representation turns this boundary integral into site and edge contributions depending on boundary families of
2\times 2
Hermitized cavity messages.

The typical number in the tilted ensemble is

k_{\mathcal{D}}(s)={}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(s)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\left\langle\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})\right\rangle_{s}\,,

(1328)

where
\langle\cdot\rangle_{s}
denotes the average with respect to (
1325
). The rate function is obtained parametrically as

\Phi_{\mathcal{D}}(k_{\mathcal{D}}(s))=sk_{\mathcal{D}}(s)-{}_{\mathcal{D}}(s)\,.

(1329)

The variance of the count in the original ensemble is

{\rm Var}\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})=N{}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime\prime}(0)+o(N)

(1330)

in the finite-connectivity scaling. Higher derivatives of
D
give higher cumulants.

It is useful to compare this with a direct density integration. The average intensive number of eigenvalues in
\mathcal{D}
is

{}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(0)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\overline{\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})}=\int_{\mathcal{D}}d^{2}z\,\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)}\,.

(1331)

This equation is only the first derivative at
s=0
. It does not determine the variance or the rate function. The full number-statistics problem requires correlations of the spectral density over the domain, or equivalently the tilted boundary-message distribution described above. This is the same lesson as in the Hermitian index problem: integrating the typical density gives the typical count, but not the probability of atypical counts.

Several elementary checks are immediate. If
\mathcal{D}
is empty, then

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})=0\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{}_{\mathcal{D}}(s)=0\,.

(1332)

If
\mathcal{D}
contains the entire spectrum for every realization under consideration, or with probability one at the large-deviation exponential scale, then

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})=N\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{}_{\mathcal{D}}(s)=s\,.

(1333)

If
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
are disjoint and separated by contours that do not cross eigenvalues, then

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{2})=\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D}_{1})+\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D}_{2})\,.

(1334)

At the level of the argument principle this is just additivity of winding numbers. At the level of the Hermitized representation it follows from additivity of the boundary integral over domains.

There is also an important Hermitian limit. Suppose that
\boldsymbol{A}
is Hermitian, so all eigenvalues lie on the real axis, and assume that no eigenvalue lies exactly at
a
or
b
, or that the half-open convention used in Section
12
is adopted. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a thin strip around the interval
I=(a,b]
on the real axis. As the strip thickness goes to zero, the contour integral (
1262
) collapses to the difference of boundary values of the Hermitian resolvent above and below the real axis. One recovers

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}((a,b])=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\int_{a}^{b}d\lambda{\rm Tr}[(\lambda-i\epsilon)\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}]^{-1},

(1335)

which is the interval-count formula used in the Hermitian sections. Thus the non-Hermitian number statistic is a genuine extension of the Hermitian index statistic, not a separate construction.

For dense Ginibre-type matrices, the eigenvalues form a strongly correlated two-dimensional gas, and exact determinantal methods are available in special cases
[
32
,
30
]
. In such ensembles, number statistics in disks or other domains are related to two-dimensional Coulomb-gas fluctuations. The approach described here has a different purpose. It is designed for ensembles where one does not want to start from an eigenvalue joint density, either because the matrix distribution is non-invariant or because the sparse graph structure is the natural disorder variable. The price is that the order parameter is no longer an eigenvalue density alone; it is a distribution of Hermitized cavity messages along the boundary of the counting domain.

The final picture is therefore parallel to the Hermitian large-deviation theory. The Hermitian index below a threshold is the phase jump of a determinant across the real axis. The non-Hermitian number inside a domain is the winding number of the determinant along the boundary of that domain. Hermitization turns this winding problem into a boundary integral of a regularized logarithmic determinant. On a sparse graph, the Hermitized determinant has a Bethe decomposition into local site and edge terms. Consequently, number fluctuations and large deviations are described by a tilted population of boundary-dependent cavity messages. At zero tilt one recovers the ordinary unconditioned Hermitized cavity population; the full number-statistics problem is obtained by keeping the tilt and computing the Bethe free energy of the biased message ensemble.

Exercise 15.10 : Hermitized block determinant.

Exercise 15.11 : Discrete boundary approximation.

For a circular domain of radius

z_{m}=Re^{2\pi im/M}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptm=0,\ldots,M-1\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\Delta s_{m}=\frac{2\pi R}{M}

to derive the finite-difference approximation of the normal derivative used in

\mathcal{N}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\mathcal{D})\simeq\frac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{m}\frac{\Delta s_{m}}{\delta}\left[\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z_{m}^{\rm out})-\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z_{m}^{\rm in})\right]\,.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}=\begin{pmatrix}0&A_{ij}\\

and

.

16
Algorithms, checks, and numerical validation

The previous sections developed the cavity and replica equations for several sparse-matrix ensembles. Those equations are only useful if one can solve them reliably and check that the numerical solution is consistent with the underlying spectral problem. The purpose of this section is therefore practical. We explain how the cavity equations become numerical algorithms, how one reduces their computational cost, which observables should be measured, and which checks should be performed before any numerical result is trusted. Since these notes are meant to be used pedagogically, we work explicitly through the algebra that turns the formal cavity recursions into efficient update rules. The two basic computational viewpoints are the same throughout the subject: one may solve the belief-propagation equations on a single large instance, or one may solve the distributional fixed-point equations directly by population dynamics
[
67
,
38
,
92
,
50
,
79
,
83
]
. The first route is useful for comparison with direct diagonalization of a fixed matrix, while the second route gives the thermodynamic-limit disorder average more directly. Appendix
C
gives the generic population-dynamics construction; the present section focuses on implementation checks and validation diagnostics.

The formulas below deliberately repeat cavity equations derived in earlier sections, but their role is different here. They are no longer being introduced as formal self-consistency relations; they are being reorganized as update rules, cost reductions, diagnostics, and validation checks.

We start with sparse symmetric matrices. Consider

A_{ij}=D_{i}\delta_{ij}+C_{ij}J_{ij}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptA_{ij}=A_{ji}\,,

(1370)

and the spectral parameter

z=\lambda-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon>0\,.

(1371)

The cavity equations on a fixed graph are

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,,

(1372)

and

G_{i}(z)=\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,.

(1373)

At first sight, (
1372
) seems to require, for every directed edge
i\to j
, a sum over all neighbors of
i
except
j
. If one implements this naively, one sweep of updates costs

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j\in\partial i}O(k_{i})=O\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}k_{i}^{2}\right)\,,

(1374)

where
k_{i}=|\partial i|
is the degree of vertex
i
. On graphs with broad degree fluctuations this may be much larger than the number of edges. The first useful derivation is therefore an algebraic reduction of the update cost.

Define the full incoming self-energy at vertex
i
,

\Sigma_{i}(z)=\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)\,.

(1375)

Then (
1373
) becomes

G_{i}(z)=\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\Sigma_{i}(z)}.

(1376)

The cavity sum in (
1372
) is the same quantity with the contribution of neighbor
j
removed:

\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)=\Sigma_{i}(z)-J_{ij}^{2}G_{j\to i}(z)\,.

(1377)

Hence

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\Sigma_{i}(z)+J_{ij}^{2}G_{j\to i}(z)}\,.

(1378)

This formula is the one that should be implemented. In one sweep, one first computes all
\Sigma_{i}(z)
from the incoming messages and then updates all directed-edge messages with (
1378
). Since each undirected edge contributes to exactly two values of
\Sigma_{i}
, the cost of computing all
\Sigma_{i}
is
O(|E|)
, where
|E|
is the number of undirected edges. The cost of the subsequent directed-edge update is also
O(|E|)
. Thus one full sweep costs

O(|E|)\,,

(1379)

not
O(\sum_{i}k_{i}^{2})
. This simple subtraction identity is essential in practice.

Example 16.1 : Why the fast update matters on a high-degree vertex

The subtraction identity behind (
1378
) is not only a notational convenience; it changes the computational scaling of the belief-propagation sweep. Consider a star graph with one central vertex
0
and
K
leaves
1,\ldots,K
. Let all weights be equal to one and all diagonal terms vanish:

J_{0\ell}=1\,,\hskip 22.99988ptD_{i}=0\,.

(1380)

The cavity message from the central vertex to leaf
j
is

G_{0\to j}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1,\,\ell\neq j}^{K}G_{\ell\to 0}(z)}\,.

(1381)

If this is computed naively for all leaves
j=1,\ldots,K
, one recomputes a sum of
K-1
terms for each outgoing message. The cost at the central vertex is therefore
O(K^{2})
.

Instead define the full incoming self-energy at the central vertex,

\Sigma_{0}(z)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}G_{\ell\to 0}(z)\,.

(1382)

Then

\sum_{\ell=1,\,\ell\neq j}^{K}G_{\ell\to 0}(z)=\Sigma_{0}(z)-G_{j\to 0}(z)\,,

(1383)

and hence

G_{0\to j}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\Sigma_{0}(z)+G_{j\to 0}(z)}\,.

(1384)

Now
\Sigma_{0}(z)
is computed once at cost
O(K)
, and all
K
outgoing messages are updated at total cost
O(K)
. Thus the fast update reduces the central-vertex cost from
O(K^{2})
to
O(K)
. On sparse graphs with bounded mean degree this distinction may look mild, but on heterogeneous graphs with hubs it is essential.

A damped synchronous iteration is then

G_{i\to j}^{(t+1)}(z)=(1-\gamma)G_{i\to j}^{(t)}(z)+\gamma\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\Sigma_{i}^{(t)}(z)+J_{ij}^{2}G_{j\to i}^{(t)}(z)}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt0<\gamma\leq 1\,,

(1385)

with

\Sigma_{i}^{(t)}(z)=\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}^{(t)}(z)\,.

(1386)

The damping factor
\gamma
is often needed when
\epsilon
is small or when the graph contains vertices of very large degree. A natural stopping criterion is the mean change per directed edge,

\Delta^{(t)}(z)=\frac{1}{2|E|}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j\in\partial i}\left|G_{i\to j}^{(t+1)}(z)-G_{i\to j}^{(t)}(z)\right|\,.

(1387)

One stops when
\Delta^{(t)}(z)
falls below a prescribed tolerance. Once the fixed point has been reached, one computes the local Green functions from

G_{i}(z)=\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\Sigma_{i}(z)}\,,

(1388)

and the regularized density of the instance from

\rho_{\epsilon}^{\rm BP}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\rm Im}G_{i}(\lambda-i\epsilon).

(1389)

This is the single-instance belief-propagation algorithm for sparse symmetric matrices.

It is useful to understand why this fixed point may fail to converge. Linearizing (
1372
) around a fixed point
G_{i\to j}^{\star}
gives

\delta G_{i\to j}^{(t+1)}=\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}J_{i\ell}^{2}\left(G_{i\to j}^{\star}\right)^{2}\delta G_{\ell\to i}^{(t)}\,.

(1390)

This follows from the elementary derivative

\frac{\partial}{\partial G_{\ell\to i}}\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\sum_{r\in\partial i\setminus j}J_{ir}^{2}G_{r\to i}}=J_{i\ell}^{2}\left(G_{i\to j}\right)^{2}\,.

(1391)

Hence the Jacobian of the update map is explicitly known. If the spectral radius of the linear operator in (
1390
) is smaller than one, the fixed point is locally stable. In numerical work one rarely computes this spectral radius directly, but (
1390
) explains three basic empirical facts: convergence becomes slower when
\epsilon
is small, convergence becomes slower near spectral edges or localization transitions, and damping improves stability by reducing the effective Jacobian.

The ensemble-level alternative to belief propagation is population dynamics. Suppose the graph ensemble has degree distribution
p_{k}
and excess-degree distribution

q_{\ell}=\frac{(\ell+1)p_{\ell+1}}{\sum_{r}rp_{r}}.

(1392)

The distributional cavity equation is

\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q_{\ell}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}J_{r}^{2}G_{r}}\right)\,.

(1393)

To turn (
1393
) into an algorithm, we represent the unknown law by an empirical population

\left\{G^{(1)},G^{(2)},\ldots,G^{(M)}\right\}\,,

(1394)

with large
M
. One update consists of drawing an excess degree
\ell
from
q_{\ell}
, drawing
\ell
indices
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}
uniformly from
\{1,\ldots,M\}
, drawing
D
from
p_{D}
and
J_{1},\ldots,J_{\ell}
from
p_{J}
, and computing

G_{\rm new}=\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}J_{r}^{2}G^{(a_{r})}}\,.

(1395)

A randomly selected population member is then replaced by
G_{\rm new}
. Repeating this procedure many times produces an empirical approximation of the fixed-point distribution. To measure observables, one draws a full degree
k
from
p_{k}
, generates

G_{\rm site}=\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{k}J_{r}^{2}G^{(a_{r})}}\,,

(1396)

and averages

\rho_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\approx\frac{1}{\pi n_{\rm obs}}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\rm obs}}{\rm Im}G_{{\rm site},m}(\lambda-i\epsilon)\,.

(1397)

The statistical error of (
1397
) has the usual Monte Carlo form and decreases as
n_{\rm obs}^{-1/2}
at fixed population, while the error due to the finite population size decreases as
M^{-1/2}
when the fixed point is sufficiently regular. In practice, one must check both sources of error.

The same logic applies to sparse covariance and diluted Wishart matrices, but now the graph is bipartite. We keep the diluted Wishart convention used earlier:
U_{\mu\to i}
is not divided by
d
, and the actual contribution to the variable denominator is
d^{-1}U_{\mu\to i}
. The cavity equations are

U_{\mu\to i}(z)=\frac{(\xi_{i}^{\mu})^{2}}{1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}(\xi_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}(z)}\,,

(1398)

and

G_{i\to\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i\setminus\mu}U_{\nu\to i}(z)}\,.

(1399)

Again, the naïve implementation repeats the same partial sums many times. The efficient form is obtained by introducing

T_{\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j\in\partial\mu}(\xi_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}(z)\,,

(1400)

and

S_{i}(z)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i}U_{\nu\to i}(z)\,.

(1401)

Then

U_{\mu\to i}(z)=\frac{(\xi_{i}^{\mu})^{2}}{1-T_{\mu}(z)+(\xi_{i}^{\mu})^{2}G_{i\to\mu}(z)/d}\,,

(1402)

because the exclusion of the edge
(i,\mu)
is implemented by subtracting the corresponding contribution from
T_{\mu}
. Similarly,

G_{i\to\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z-S_{i}(z)+\frac{1}{d}U_{\mu\to i}(z)}\,,

(1403)

and

G_{i}(z)=\frac{1}{z-S_{i}(z)}\,.

(1404)

Thus one sweep costs
O(|E_{\rm bip}|)
, where
|E_{\rm bip}|
is the number of occupied bipartite edges. The regularized density is

\rho_{\epsilon}^{\rm BP}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\rm Im}G_{i}(\lambda-i\epsilon)\,.

(1405)

The population-dynamics version of the diluted Wishart problem uses two populations, one for variable-to-factor messages and one for factor-to-variable self-energies. Let

\left\{G^{(1)},\ldots,G^{(M)}\right\}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\left\{U^{(1)},\ldots,U^{(M)}\right\}\,,

(1406)

be the two empirical populations. A factor update draws a factor excess degree
k
from the corresponding law, draws
k
incoming variable messages and
k+1
weights, and computes

U_{\rm new}=\frac{\xi^{2}}{1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{r=1}^{k}\xi_{r}^{2}G^{(a_{r})}}\,.

(1407)

A variable update draws a variable excess degree
\ell
and computes

G_{\rm new}=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}U^{(b_{r})}}\,.

(1408)

The corresponding site measurement draws a full variable degree
k_{\rm site}
and computes

G_{\rm site}=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{r=1}^{k_{\rm site}}U^{(b_{r})}}\,.

(1409)

Measurement of the density then averages
{\rm Im}\,G_{\rm site}/\pi
over many such site samples, exactly as in the sparse symmetric case. The important point is that the factor and variable populations must be updated alternately, because the two message types live on different sides of the bipartite graph
[
56
,
79
]
.

For the generalized diluted Wishart ensemble, the same strategy works, but the factor update depends on three partial sums rather than one. If the occupied edge
(i,\mu)
carries the pair of weights
(x_{i}^{\mu},y_{i}^{\mu})
, the factor-to-variable self-energy is

U_{\mu\to i}(z)=\frac{4d\,x_{i}^{\mu}y_{i}^{\mu}+(y_{i}^{\mu})^{2}S_{xx}^{\mu\to i}-2x_{i}^{\mu}y_{i}^{\mu}S_{xy}^{\mu\to i}+(x_{i}^{\mu})^{2}S_{yy}^{\mu\to i}}{\left(2d-S_{xy}^{\mu\to i}\right)^{2}-S_{xx}^{\mu\to i}S_{yy}^{\mu\to i}}\,,

(1410)

with

S_{xx}^{\mu\to i}=\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}(x_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptS_{xy}^{\mu\to i}=\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}x_{j}^{\mu}y_{j}^{\mu}G_{j\to\mu}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptS_{yy}^{\mu\to i}=\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}(y_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}\,.

(1411)

The efficient implementation introduces the full factor sums

T_{xx,\mu}=\sum_{j\in\partial\mu}(x_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptT_{xy,\mu}=\sum_{j\in\partial\mu}x_{j}^{\mu}y_{j}^{\mu}G_{j\to\mu}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptT_{yy,\mu}=\sum_{j\in\partial\mu}(y_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}\,,

(1412)

and then subtracts the contribution of the distinguished variable:

S_{xx}^{\mu\to i}=T_{xx,\mu}-(x_{i}^{\mu})^{2}G_{i\to\mu}\,,\hskip 11.49994ptS_{xy}^{\mu\to i}=T_{xy,\mu}-x_{i}^{\mu}y_{i}^{\mu}G_{i\to\mu}\,,\hskip 11.49994ptS_{yy}^{\mu\to i}=T_{yy,\mu}-(y_{i}^{\mu})^{2}G_{i\to\mu}\,.

(1413)

The variable update remains scalar:

G_{i\to\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\partial i\setminus\mu}U_{\nu\to i}(z)}\,.

(1414)

For the same linear-cost reason as in the ordinary diluted Wishart case, one should also introduce the full variable sum

S_{i}(z)=\sum_{\nu\in\partial i}U_{\nu\to i}(z)\,.

(1415)

Then

G_{i\to\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z-S_{i}(z)+U_{\mu\to i}(z)}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{i}(z)=\frac{1}{z-S_{i}(z)}\,.

(1416)

Thus the generalized ensemble remains computationally manageable because both the rank-two factor sums and the variable sums are updated by subtraction. In practice, the numerical cost differs from ordinary diluted Wishart matrices only by a larger constant factor.

For sparse non-Hermitian matrices the messages are
2\times 2
matrices rather than complex scalars. The cavity recursion is

\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}(z,\eta)=\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}(z,\eta)-\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}\boldsymbol{G}_{\ell\to i}(z,\eta)\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}^{\dagger}\right]^{-1}\,,

(1417)

where

\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}(z,\eta)=\begin{pmatrix}i\eta&z-A_{ii}\\
z^{*}-A_{ii}^{*}&i\eta\end{pmatrix}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}=\begin{pmatrix}0&A_{ij}\\
A_{ji}^{*}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(1418)

The efficient form is again obtained by introducing the full matrix self-energy

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}(z,\eta)=\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}\boldsymbol{G}_{\ell\to i}(z,\eta)\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}^{\dagger}\,.

(1419)

Then

\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}(z,\eta)=\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}(z,\eta)-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}(z,\eta)+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}\boldsymbol{G}_{j\to i}(z,\eta)\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}^{\dagger}\right]^{-1}\,,

(1420)

and

\boldsymbol{G}_{i}(z,\eta)=\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}(z,\eta)-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}(z,\eta)\right]^{-1}\,.

(1421)

The regularized non-Hermitian resolvent field is

g_{\eta}(z,z^{*})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\boldsymbol{G}_{i}(z,\eta)\right]_{21}\,,

(1422)

and the density is

\rho(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\partial_{z^{*}}g_{\eta}(z,z^{*}).

(1423)

Numerically, one evaluates
g_{\eta}
on a grid of points
z=x+iy
and approximates the Wirtinger derivative by finite differences,

\partial_{z^{*}}g=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{x}+i\partial_{y}\right)g\,.

(1424)

Thus, if
\Delta x=\Delta y=h
,

\partial_{z^{*}}g(x,y)\approx\frac{1}{4h}\left[g(x+h,y)-g(x-h,y)\right]+\frac{i}{4h}\left[g(x,y+h)-g(x,y-h)\right]\,.

(1425)

The corresponding population dynamics uses a population of
2\times 2
matrices rather than complex numbers
[
77
,
47
]
. Apart from this, the algorithmic structure is unchanged.

The large-deviation and conditioned-density calculations require enlarged messages rather than a new numerical philosophy. In the Hermitian index problem the message carries the cavity Green functions at the two threshold values

z_{x}^{-}=x-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz_{x}^{+}=x+i\epsilon\,,

(1426)

and, for conditioned densities, also at the probe point
z_{\lambda}=\lambda-i\eta
. Thus the message is

\mathfrak{m}=\left(G^{-},G^{+},G^{0}\right)\,,

(1427)

or the analogous pair of self-energies in a bipartite problem. The local update is the ordinary cavity map applied componentwise, while the tilted problem introduces a local weight
w_{s}(\mathfrak{m},\{\mathfrak{m}_{r}\})
derived from the determinant phase or Bethe free-energy contribution of the observable of interest
[
48
,
70
,
71
,
75
]
. The generic structure of the distributional equation is therefore

\mathcal{P}_{s}(\mathfrak{m})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{C}_{s}}\int\left[\prod_{r}d\mathfrak{m}_{r}\mathcal{P}_{s}(\mathfrak{m}_{r})\right]d\omega\Pi(\omega)w_{s}(\omega,\{\mathfrak{m}_{r}\})\delta\left(\mathfrak{m}-\mathcal{F}(\omega,\{\mathfrak{m}_{r}\})\right)\,,

(1428)

where
\omega
denotes the local disorder variables and
\mathcal{C}_{s}
is the normalization required to keep
\mathcal{P}_{s}
normalized. Equation (
1428
) is the algorithmic heart of the large-deviation and conditioning problems: the map
\mathcal{F}
is the same one used in the unbiased spectral-density calculation, but the empirical population is now reweighted by the local tilting factor.

The validation step is as important as the algorithm itself. The first family of checks consists of exact sum rules. For a symmetric matrix, the density obtained from either belief propagation or population dynamics must satisfy

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\lambda\rho(\lambda)=1\,.

(1429)

The first moment is

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\lambda\lambda\rho(\lambda)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}{\rm Tr}\boldsymbol{A}.

(1430)

For Eq. (
1370
),

\frac{1}{N}{\rm Tr}\boldsymbol{A}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}D_{i}\,,

(1431)

so the ensemble average is

\int d\lambda\lambda\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)}=\int dDp_{D}(D)D\,.

(1432)

The second moment is also explicit:

\frac{1}{N}{\rm Tr}\boldsymbol{A}^{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}D_{i}^{2}+\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}J_{ij}^{2}\,.

(1433)

Hence for a graph of mean degree
c
,

\int d\lambda\lambda^{2}\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)}=\int dDp_{D}(D)D^{2}+c\int dJp_{J}(J)J^{2}\,.

(1434)

These moment checks are elementary but indispensable. Strictly, they are checks on the limiting spectral measure, on direct eigenvalue sums, or on the high-
|z|
expansion of the resolvent. At finite
\epsilon
, Lorentzian regularization has long tails, so moment integrals over a finite grid should be interpreted with the chosen window and regulator kept explicit. When the corresponding controlled checks fail, the numerical density should not be trusted.

Example 16.2 : Moment checks on a weighted three-site chain

Moment checks are among the simplest ways of detecting an implementation error. Consider

\boldsymbol{A}=\begin{pmatrix}D_{1}&J_{12}&0\\
J_{12}&D_{2}&J_{23}\\
0&J_{23}&D_{3}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(1435)

The first spectral moment is

\frac{1}{3}{\rm Tr}\boldsymbol{A}=\frac{D_{1}+D_{2}+D_{3}}{3}\,.

(1436)

The second moment is

\frac{1}{3}{\rm Tr}\boldsymbol{A}^{2}\,.

(1437)

Let us compute it explicitly. The diagonal entries of
\boldsymbol{A}^{2}
are

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{A}^{2})_{11}

\displaystyle=D_{1}^{2}+J_{12}^{2}\,,

(1438)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{A}^{2})_{22}

\displaystyle=D_{2}^{2}+J_{12}^{2}+J_{23}^{2}\,,

(1439)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{A}^{2})_{33}

\displaystyle=D_{3}^{2}+J_{23}^{2}\,.

(1440)

Therefore

\frac{1}{3}{\rm Tr}\boldsymbol{A}^{2}=\frac{1}{3}\left[D_{1}^{2}+D_{2}^{2}+D_{3}^{2}+2J_{12}^{2}+2J_{23}^{2}\right]\,.

(1441)

This is exactly the finite-size version of the general sparse-matrix identity

\frac{1}{N}{\rm Tr}\boldsymbol{A}^{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}D_{i}^{2}+\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}J_{ij}^{2}\,.

(1442)

A numerical density obtained from belief propagation, population dynamics, or direct diagonalization should reproduce these moments after integration over
\lambda
, up to the effects of finite integration range, finite grid spacing, and finite regulator.

For diluted Wishart matrices,

\boldsymbol{W}=\frac{1}{d}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\,,

(1443)

the spectrum must be nonnegative. Therefore

\rho(\lambda)=0\hskip 22.99988pt\text{for }\lambda<0\,,

(1444)

in the limit
\epsilon\downarrow 0
. At finite
\epsilon
the numerical estimator has Lorentzian leakage into
\lambda<0
. Away from the origin this leakage must shrink as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
, but if the limiting measure has a zero atom, the leakage near
\lambda=0
should be treated as a broadening artifact rather than as negative spectral support. The first moment is

\frac{1}{N}{\rm Tr}\boldsymbol{W}=\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}(X_{i}^{\mu})^{2}\,,

(1445)

and in the Poisson diluted ensemble it converges to

\int_{0}^{\infty}d\lambda\lambda\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{W}}(\lambda)}=\frac{\langle\xi^{2}\rangle}{\alpha}.

(1446)

Example 16.3 : A finite Wishart moment and positivity check

Let

\boldsymbol{X}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptd=1\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\boldsymbol{W}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\,.

(1447)

Then

\boldsymbol{W}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&1\\
0&1&1\\
1&1&2\end{pmatrix}\,.

(1448)

The trace is

\frac{1}{3}{\rm Tr}\boldsymbol{W}=\frac{1+1+2}{3}=\frac{4}{3}\,.

(1449)

Using the rectangular matrix directly,

\frac{1}{Nd}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}(X_{i}^{\mu})^{2}=\frac{1}{3}(1+1+1+1)=\frac{4}{3}\,,

(1450)

which agrees with the trace of
\boldsymbol{W}
.

The eigenvalues are

{\rm spec}(\boldsymbol{W})=\{3,1,0\}\,.

(1451)

Hence the spectrum is nonnegative, as it must be for a matrix of the form
\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}
. A numerical density for a Wishart ensemble should therefore pass three elementary checks: nonnegative support in the limit
\epsilon\downarrow 0
, normalization to one, and first moment equal to
N^{-1}{\rm Tr}\boldsymbol{W}
or its ensemble average.

For the generalized diluted Wishart ensemble,

\boldsymbol{F}=\frac{1}{2d}\left(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{Y}^{\rm T}+\boldsymbol{Y}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\right)\,,

(1452)

the first moment becomes

\int d\lambda\lambda\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{F}}(\lambda)}=\frac{m_{11}}{\alpha}\,,

(1453)

with

m_{11}=\int dxdy\varrho(x,y)xy\,.

(1454)

These formulas are immediate consequences of the trace identities and should always be checked numerically.

The second family of checks uses exactly solvable limiting laws. For the adjacency matrix of a random
c
-regular graph, the cavity equations must recover the Kesten–McKay density
[
37
,
45
]
. For sparse symmetric matrices in the large-connectivity scaling, they must reduce to the Wigner self-consistency equation. For diluted Wishart matrices, the dense limit must reproduce the Marčenko–Pastur law
[
43
]
. For dense products of Wishart matrices, the numerical solution must satisfy the polynomial equation derived from the product-Wishart law
[
23
,
17
]
. For sparse non-Hermitian matrices, the large-connectivity limit must approach the circular or elliptic laws
[
32
,
82
]
. These checks are valuable because they test not only the implementation but also the normalization and scaling conventions.

The third family of checks compares cavity results with direct diagonalization of finite instances. For a Hermitian matrix with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N}
, the exact Lorentzian-broadened density is

\rho_{\epsilon,N}^{\rm diag}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\epsilon}{(\lambda-\lambda_{i})^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}\,.

(1455)

Example 16.4 : Why one must compare at the same regulator

Consider a matrix with two eigenvalues,

\lambda_{1}=-1\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\lambda_{2}=1\,.

(1456)

The exact empirical density is

\rho_{N}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\delta(\lambda+1)+\frac{1}{2}\delta(\lambda-1)\,.

(1457)

At finite regulator
\epsilon>0
, the object computed from the resolvent is not (
1457
), but the Lorentzian-broadened density

\rho_{\epsilon,N}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{\epsilon}{(\lambda+1)^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{\epsilon}{(\lambda-1)^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}\,.

(1458)

Thus, if a cavity or population-dynamics calculation is performed at finite
\epsilon
, the correct diagonalization benchmark is (
1458
), not a raw histogram of delta peaks.

This also explains a common numerical pitfall. If
\epsilon
is chosen much smaller than the mean eigenvalue spacing of a finite matrix, then direct diagonalization displays very sharp sample-dependent peaks. A population-dynamics calculation, on the other hand, describes the thermodynamic smoothed density at the same regulator. The correct comparison is therefore

N\to\infty\hskip 11.49994pt\text{at fixed }\epsilon>0\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\text{then}\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon\downarrow 0\,.

(1459)

This is why the regulator is a validation parameter, not just a formal device.

Accordingly, a convenient error measure is

\mathcal{E}_{1}(\epsilon)=\int d\lambda\left|\rho_{\epsilon,N}^{\rm BP}(\lambda)-\rho_{\epsilon,N}^{\rm diag}(\lambda)\right|\,,

(1460)

or the corresponding ensemble-averaged version obtained by averaging both densities over disorder realizations.

For non-Hermitian matrices, direct diagonalization can be compared with the Hermitized cavity estimate only after the regularization and grid have been matched. One convenient diagonalization benchmark is a smoothed empirical density,

\rho_{h,N}^{\rm diag}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi h^{2}N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\exp\left[-\frac{|z-z_{i}|^{2}}{h^{2}}\right]\,,

(1461)

or alternatively a binning procedure on a fine grid in the complex plane. The precise mollifier is less important than consistency: the smoothing scale
h
, the Hermitization regulator
\eta
, the finite-difference grid, and the computational box must all be stated. A practical validation is then obtained by comparing a cavity density
\rho_{\eta,h,N}^{\rm cav}
, evaluated on the same grid and with the stated regulator, with the smoothed diagonalization density:

\mathcal{E}_{2}(\eta,h)=\int_{\mathcal{B}}d^{2}z\left|\rho_{\eta,h,N}^{\rm cav}(z)-\rho_{h,N}^{\rm diag}(z)\right|

(1462)

over a box
\mathcal{B}
that contains the numerical support.

The fourth family of checks concerns the regulator. In the Hermitian problem one solves the cavity equations at
\epsilon>0
, while in the non-Hermitian problem one solves the Hermitized equations at
\eta>0
. The solution should vary smoothly when the regulator is decreased. A robust numerical strategy is continuation. One starts at a value of
\epsilon
or
\eta
for which the fixed-point iteration is strongly contractive, converges the messages, and then uses this fixed point as the initial condition for a smaller regulator. If

\epsilon_{0}>\epsilon_{1}>\cdots>\epsilon_{n}

(1463)

is a decreasing schedule, then the fixed point at
\epsilon_{m-1}
is used to initialize the iteration at
\epsilon_{m}
. This procedure is especially effective near spectral edges and in large-deviation or conditioned calculations, where the enlarged message space can make the map less contractive.

Example 16.5 : Regulator continuation as a numerical protocol

Suppose one wants the spectral density near a point
\lambda
where the cavity iteration is unstable for very small
\epsilon
. Instead of starting directly at the desired regulator, one may use a continuation schedule

\epsilon_{0}>\epsilon_{1}>\cdots>\epsilon_{n}\,.

(1464)

A typical choice is geometric:

\epsilon_{m}=\epsilon_{0}r^{m}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt0<r<1\,.

(1465)

At the first value
\epsilon_{0}
, one initializes the messages by a simple stable guess, for example

G_{i\to j}^{(0)}=\frac{1}{\lambda-i\epsilon_{0}-D_{i}}\,.

(1466)

After convergence at
\epsilon_{0}
, the resulting fixed point is used as the initial condition for
\epsilon_{1}
. Repeating this procedure gives a sequence of fixed points

\{G_{i\to j}^{(\epsilon_{0})}\}\longrightarrow\{G_{i\to j}^{(\epsilon_{1})}\}\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow\{G_{i\to j}^{(\epsilon_{n})}\}\,.

(1467)

The validation check is that observables such as

\rho_{\epsilon_{m}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi N}\sum_{i}{\rm Im}G_{i}(\lambda-i\epsilon_{m})

(1468)

vary smoothly with
\epsilon_{m}
, except where the underlying spectral measure contains atoms or very sharp localized structures. Sudden jumps, loss of the sign condition
{\rm Im}\,G_{i}>0
, or dependence on the initialization indicate that the fixed-point iteration has not been validated.

The fifth family of checks concerns self-averaging. Population dynamics computes the ensemble law directly, whereas belief propagation computes the density of one large instance. For a self-averaging observable, one expects

\rho_{\epsilon,N}^{\rm BP}(\lambda)\approx\overline{\rho_{\epsilon,N}^{\rm diag}(\lambda)}\approx\rho_{\epsilon}^{\rm pop}(\lambda)

(1469)

for large
N
. The first approximation compares one large instance with the disorder average; the second compares the finite-
N
average with the thermodynamic-limit population dynamics. When these comparisons fail badly, there are only a few possible explanations: the system size is too small, the regulator is too small, the observable is not self-averaging in the regime under study, or the numerical fixed point is unstable or inaccurate.

Example 16.6 : A minimal validation workflow

A useful validation workflow for a sparse symmetric ensemble is the following. Choose a benchmark ensemble, for instance random
c
-regular graphs with unweighted adjacency matrices.

First, compute the exact benchmark density. For random
c
-regular graphs this is the Kesten–McKay law,

\rho_{\rm KM}(\lambda)=\frac{c}{2\pi}\frac{\sqrt{4(c-1)-\lambda^{2}}}{c^{2}-\lambda^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{|\lambda|\leq 2\sqrt{c-1}}\,.

(1470)

Second, generate finite random regular graphs and compute the broadened diagonalization density

\rho_{\epsilon,N}^{\rm diag}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi N}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\frac{\epsilon}{(\lambda-\lambda_{\alpha})^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}\,.

(1471)

For a connected
c
-regular graph, this density contains the trivial Perron eigenvalue
c
with weight
1/N
. In a comparison with the limiting Kesten–McKay bulk, one should either remove this contribution from the diagonalization density or add its finite-
N
Lorentzian contribution explicitly to the benchmark.

Third, solve the cavity equation

G_{\rm cav}=\frac{1}{z-(c-1)G_{\rm cav}}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{\rm site}=\frac{1}{z-cG_{\rm cav}}\,,

(1472)

and compute

\rho_{\epsilon}^{\rm cav}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}{\rm Im}G_{\rm site}(\lambda-i\epsilon)\,.

(1473)

Fourth, compare the three objects at the same regulator:

\rho_{\epsilon,N}^{\rm diag}(\lambda)\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\rho_{\epsilon}^{\rm cav}(\lambda)\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\rho_{\rm KM}(\lambda)\text{ broadened at the same }\epsilon\,.

(1474)

Finally, check the moments

\int d\lambda\rho(\lambda)=1\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\int d\lambda\lambda\rho(\lambda)=0\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\int d\lambda\lambda^{2}\rho(\lambda)=c\,.

(1475)

Only after these checks pass should the same implementation be trusted for ensembles without a closed-form benchmark.

The large-deviation and conditioned-density algorithms come with additional exact checks. For the Hermitian index problem,

{}_{x}^{\prime}(0)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}d\lambda\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)}\,,

(1476)

and

{}_{x}^{\prime\prime}(0)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}{\rm Var}\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)\,.

(1477)

These identities can be checked by direct diagonalization on moderate sizes, using

\widehat{\psi}_{x,N}(s)=\frac{1}{N}\log\left[\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}e^{s\mathcal{K}_{m}(x)}\right]\,,

(1478)

where
\mathcal{K}_{m}(x)
is the index of the
m
th sample. Likewise, for conditioned densities one must check

\int_{-\infty}^{x}d\lambda\rho_{x}(\lambda|k)=k\,,

(1479)

and

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\lambda\rho_{x}(\lambda|k)=1\,.

(1480)

For non-Hermitian number statistics in a domain
\mathcal{D}
,

{}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(0)=\int_{\mathcal{D}}d^{2}z\,\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)}

(1481)

which again provides a direct consistency test between the ordinary density and the number-statistics formalism.

The role of direct diagonalization in these checks should be emphasized. For sparse Hermitian matrices of the sizes typically used in validation, full dense diagonalization is often unnecessary; one only needs the spectrum or the spectral measure on a grid. For the lecture-note level discussion, however, the conceptual point is simpler than the implementation detail: whatever exact or numerically exact spectral data are available for finite instances should be compared with the cavity predictions after using the same smoothing and the same observable. Comparing a raw histogram with a finite-
\epsilon
cavity density is not meaningful. Comparing a complex-plane density to a real-axis marginal is not meaningful. One must compare like with like.

Finally, it is useful to record the most common failure modes. If the cavity sweep converges but the density is not normalized, the error is usually a discretization or implementation mistake in the measurement stage, not in the fixed point itself. If the sweep oscillates or diverges as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
, the fixed point may be unstable and damping or continuation is required. If population dynamics and belief propagation disagree while direct diagonalization agrees with one of them, the disagreement usually traces back to insufficient population size, insufficiently large single-instance size, or failure of self-averaging in the chosen regime. If a Wishart density shows substantial weight for
\lambda<0
at very small
\epsilon
, one should first check whether that weight is merely the Lorentzian broadening of a large atom at zero before concluding that the cavity solver is wrong. If the non-Hermitian density does not integrate to one over the computational box, the box is either too small to contain the support or the numerical
\partial_{z^{*}}
derivative is too coarse. Figure
9
summarizes the validation logic advocated in this section: cavity fixed points should be compared with independent information only after the same observable, regulator, smoothing, and computational domain have been fixed.

\rho_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
\rho_{h}(z)

{}_{x}(s)
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{D})

\epsilon

\eta

h

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}(\epsilon)=\int d\lambda\,|\rho_{\epsilon}^{\rm BP}-\rho_{\epsilon}^{\rm diag}|

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}(h)=\int_{\mathcal{B}}d^{2}z\,|\rho_{h}^{\rm cav}-\rho_{h}^{\rm diag}|

\epsilon_{0}>\epsilon_{1}>\cdots>\epsilon_{n}

\eta_{0}>\eta_{1}>\cdots>\eta_{n}

N

Figure 9:
Validation workflow for numerical cavity calculations. A cavity fixed point is trusted only after it is compared with independent information expressed as the same smoothed observable at the same regulator or grid resolution. The validation gates combine normalization and moment checks, exactly solvable limits, matched direct diagonalization, regulator continuation, and consistency between single-instance BP and population dynamics. Failed checks are diagnostic information: they should be traced to smoothing, grids, instability, finite-size effects, population size, or an insufficient computational box before any spectral conclusion is drawn.

The practical moral of this section is simple. The cavity equations are not only formal fixed-point relations; they are the basis of efficient numerical solvers whose cost scales linearly with the number of edges per sweep for sparse instances and linearly with the population size per sweep for ensemble calculations. The necessary algebraic reductions are the subtraction identities that convert cavity sums into full sums plus one correction term. The necessary validation steps are normalization, moment checks, exact solvable limits, comparison with direct diagonalization at fixed smoothing, and regulator continuation. Without these checks one has only a fixed point of an iterative map. With them, one has numerical evidence that the fixed point is the spectral object predicted by the theory.

Exercises for this section

Exercise 16.1 : Fast update for sparse symmetric matrices.

Starting from

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,,

(1482)

define

\Sigma_{i}(z)=\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}J_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)\,.

(1483)

Derive

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\Sigma_{i}(z)+J_{ij}^{2}G_{j\to i}(z)}\,.

(1484)

Explain why this reduces the computational cost of one sweep.

Exercise 16.2 : Cost comparison on a star graph.

For a star graph with
K
leaves, compute the number of additions required to update all messages from the central vertex using the naive formula and using the fast self-energy formula. Verify the
O(K^{2})
versus
O(K)
scaling.

Exercise 16.3 : Damped fixed-point iteration.

Let

G^{(t+1)}=(1-\gamma)G^{(t)}+\gamma\mathcal{F}(G^{(t)})\,.

(1485)

Linearize around a fixed point
G^{\star}=\mathcal{F}(G^{\star})
and derive the effective multiplier

\Lambda=1-\gamma+\gamma\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(G^{\star})\,.

(1486)

Discuss how damping can reduce oscillations.

Exercise 16.4 : Jacobian of the Gaussian BP map.

Starting from the sparse symmetric cavity recursion, derive the linearized perturbation equation

\delta G_{i\to j}^{(t+1)}=\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}J_{i\ell}^{2}\left(G_{i\to j}^{\star}\right)^{2}\delta G_{\ell\to i}^{(t)}\,.

(1487)

What does this tell you about convergence near spectral edges?

Exercise 16.5 : Population-dynamics estimator.

Starting from the distributional equation for
\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G)
, derive the empirical population update

G_{\rm new}=\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}J_{r}^{2}G^{(a_{r})}}\,.

(1488)

Then derive the site estimator

\rho_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\approx\frac{1}{\pi n_{\rm obs}}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\rm obs}}{\rm Im}G_{{\rm site},m}\,.

(1489)

Exercise 16.6 : Wishart fast update.

Starting from the diluted Wishart factor update, define

T_{\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j\in\partial\mu}(X_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}(z)\,.

(1490)

Show that

U_{\mu\to i}(z)=\frac{(X_{i}^{\mu})^{2}}{1-T_{\mu}(z)+(X_{i}^{\mu})^{2}G_{i\to\mu}(z)/d}\,.

(1491)

Exercise 16.7 : Wishart variable fast update.

Define

S_{i}(z)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i}U_{\nu\to i}(z)\,.

(1492)

Derive

G_{i\to\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z-S_{i}(z)+\frac{1}{d}U_{\mu\to i}(z)}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{i}(z)=\frac{1}{z-S_{i}(z)}\,.

(1493)

Exercise 16.8 : Generalized Wishart fast sums.

For the generalized diluted Wishart ensemble, define the full factor sums

T_{xx,\mu}=\sum_{j\in\partial\mu}(x_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptT_{xy,\mu}=\sum_{j\in\partial\mu}x_{j}^{\mu}y_{j}^{\mu}G_{j\to\mu}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptT_{yy,\mu}=\sum_{j\in\partial\mu}(y_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}\,.

(1494)

Derive the corresponding subtracted cavity sums
S_{xx}^{\mu\to i}
,
S_{xy}^{\mu\to i}
, and
S_{yy}^{\mu\to i}
. Then define
S_{i}(z)=\sum_{\nu\in\partial i}U_{\nu\to i}(z)
and derive

G_{i\to\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z-S_{i}(z)+U_{\mu\to i}(z)}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{i}(z)=\frac{1}{z-S_{i}(z)}\,.

(1495)

Explain why the cost remains linear in the number of occupied bipartite edges.

Exercise 16.9 : Non-Hermitian fast update.

Starting from the matrix-valued cavity recursion, define

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}(z,\eta)=\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}\boldsymbol{G}_{\ell\to i}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}^{\dagger}\,.

(1496)

Derive

\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}=\left[\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}\boldsymbol{G}_{j\to i}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}^{\dagger}\right]^{-1}\,.

(1497)

Exercise 16.10 : Wirtinger derivative by finite differences.

Starting from

\partial_{z^{*}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{x}+i\partial_{y}\right)\,,

(1498)

derive the centered finite-difference approximation

\partial_{z^{*}}g(x,y)\approx\frac{g(x+h,y)-g(x-h,y)}{4h}+\frac{i[g(x,y+h)-g(x,y-h)]}{4h}\,.

(1499)

Exercise 16.11 : Normalization check.

Explain why every Hermitian spectral density must satisfy

\int d\lambda\rho(\lambda)=1\,.

(1500)

In a numerical calculation on a finite grid, list at least three reasons why the numerical integral might deviate from one.

Exercise 16.12 : First and second moment checks.

For

(1501)

derive

(1502)

and

\frac{1}{N}{\rm Tr}\boldsymbol{A}^{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i}D_{i}^{2}+\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}J_{ij}^{2}\,.

(1503)

Exercise 16.13 : Wishart first moment.

(1504)

in the Poisson diluted ensemble.

Exercise 16.27 : Programming exercise: failure-mode diagnosis.
