Appendix A
Gaussian integral identities and resolvent conventions

In the main text we repeatedly used a small number of Gaussian identities and resolvent conventions. Since the same formulas appear in the Hermitian, Wishart, and non-Hermitian problems, it is convenient to collect them in one place and derive them explicitly. The purpose of this appendix is therefore not to introduce new ideas, but to make precise the algebra that was used throughout the notes.

We begin with the Hermitian resolvent convention. Let
\boldsymbol{A}
be a real symmetric or Hermitian
N\times N
matrix with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N}
. We define the resolvent and its normalized trace by

\boldsymbol{G}(z)=(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptg(z)=\frac{1}{N}{\rm Tr}\,\boldsymbol{G}(z)\,.

(A.1)

Throughout these notes we use the lower-half-plane convention

z=\lambda-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon>0\,.

(A.2)

This choice is convenient because the Gaussian integrals used later become convergent when
{\rm Im}\,z<0
. To see how the density is recovered, diagonalize
\boldsymbol{A}
as

\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{U}\,{\rm diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N})\,\boldsymbol{U}^{-1}\,.

(A.3)

Then

g(z)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{z-\lambda_{i}}\,.

(A.4)

Substituting
z=\lambda-i\epsilon
gives

\frac{1}{\lambda-i\epsilon-\lambda_{i}}=\frac{\lambda-\lambda_{i}+i\epsilon}{(\lambda-\lambda_{i})^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}\,,

(A.5)

and therefore

{\rm Im}\frac{1}{\lambda-i\epsilon-\lambda_{i}}=\frac{\epsilon}{(\lambda-\lambda_{i})^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}\,.

(A.6)

Since

\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{\epsilon}{x^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}=\delta(x)

(A.7)

in the sense of distributions, we obtain

\rho(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}g(\lambda-i\epsilon)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta(\lambda-\lambda_{i})\,.

(A.8)

This is the convention used everywhere in the main text. If one chooses instead the upper-half-plane convention
z=\lambda+i\epsilon
, then the sign in front of the imaginary part changes. The two conventions are equivalent as long as one is consistent.

The same computation gives the local density of states. Let
u_{i}^{(j)}
be the
i
th component of the normalized eigenvector associated with
\lambda_{j}
. Then

G_{ii}(z)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{|u_{i}^{(j)}|^{2}}{z-\lambda_{j}}\,,

(A.9)

so that

\rho_{i}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}G_{ii}(\lambda-i\epsilon)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}|u_{i}^{(j)}|^{2}\delta(\lambda-\lambda_{j})\,.

(A.10)

Averaging over
i
gives the global density:

\rho(\lambda)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho_{i}(\lambda)\,.

(A.11)

We next derive the Gaussian integral identity that underlies the Edwards–Jones representation. We start in one dimension. If
a\in\mathbb{C}
has positive real part,
{\rm Re}\,a>0
, then

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{du}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}au^{2}}=a^{-1/2}\,,

(A.12)

where the square root is taken on the principal branch. The condition
{\rm Re}\,a>0
guarantees convergence. For a complex symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
with positive-definite real part, the multidimensional version is

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{du_{i}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{u}\right]=\det(\boldsymbol{M})^{-1/2}\,.

(A.13)

To prove (
A.13
) in the real symmetric positive-definite case, diagonalize

\boldsymbol{M}=\boldsymbol{O}^{\rm T}{\rm diag}(m_{1},\ldots,m_{N})\boldsymbol{O}\,,

(A.14)

with
\boldsymbol{O}
orthogonal and
m_{i}>0
. Since
d\boldsymbol{u}=d\boldsymbol{v}
under the rotation
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{O}\boldsymbol{u}
, the integral factorizes:

\int\left[\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{dv_{i}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}v_{i}^{2}}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}^{-1/2}=\det(\boldsymbol{M})^{-1/2}\,.

(A.15)

The complex Gaussian identities used in the Edwards–Jones representation are then obtained by analytic continuation to complex symmetric matrices whose real part is positive definite.

Now set

\boldsymbol{M}=i(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz=\lambda-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon>0\,.

(A.16)

If
\boldsymbol{A}
is real symmetric, the eigenvalues of
i(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})
are

i(\lambda-\lambda_{i})+\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pti=1,\ldots,N\,,

(A.17)

whose real part is
\epsilon>0
. Thus the Gaussian integral converges and gives

Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{du_{i}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm T}(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})\boldsymbol{u}\right]=\det\left[i(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})\right]^{-1/2}\,.

(A.18)

This is the precise real-Gaussian form of the Edwards–Jones representation used throughout the lecture notes for real symmetric sparse matrices.

The connection with the resolvent follows by differentiating the logarithm. Since

\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=-\frac{1}{2}\log\det\left[i(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})\right]\,,

(A.19)

we obtain

\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=-\frac{1}{2}{\rm Tr}(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}\,,

(A.20)

because for any invertible matrix
\boldsymbol{M}(z)
,

\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\log\det\boldsymbol{M}(z)={\rm Tr}\left[\boldsymbol{M}(z)^{-1}\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{M}(z)}{\partial z}\right]\,.

(A.21)

Applying (
A.20
) to
z=\lambda-i\epsilon
gives

g(\lambda-i\epsilon)=-\frac{2}{N}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda-i\epsilon)\,,

(A.22)

and therefore

\rho(\lambda)=-\frac{2}{\pi N}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda-i\epsilon)\,.

(A.23)

This is the Edwards–Jones representation in the conventions of the main text.

A second identity used repeatedly is the Schur-complement formula. Let

\boldsymbol{M}=\begin{pmatrix}a&\boldsymbol{b}^{\rm T}\\
\boldsymbol{b}&\boldsymbol{B}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(A.24)

where
a
is a scalar,
\boldsymbol{b}
is a column vector, and
\boldsymbol{B}
is an invertible
(N-1)\times(N-1)
matrix. Then

(\boldsymbol{M}^{-1})_{11}=\left(a-\boldsymbol{b}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}\boldsymbol{b}\right)^{-1}\,.

(A.25)

To see this, write the inverse in block form,

\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}\\
\boldsymbol{\gamma}&\boldsymbol{\Delta}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(A.26)

and impose
\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{I}
. The
(1,1)
block gives

a\alpha+\boldsymbol{b}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{\gamma}=1\,,

(A.27)

while the lower-left block gives

\boldsymbol{b}\,\alpha+\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\boldsymbol{0}\,.

(A.28)

Solving (
A.28
) for
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
,

\boldsymbol{\gamma}=-\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}\boldsymbol{b}\,\alpha\,,

(A.29)

and substituting into (
A.27
), we get

\left(a-\boldsymbol{b}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}\boldsymbol{b}\right)\alpha=1\,,

(A.30)

which proves (
A.25
).

The same identity is recovered directly from the Gaussian integral by completing the square. Consider

I(u_{1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N-1}}\left[\prod_{i=2}^{N}\frac{du_{i}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
\boldsymbol{u}\end{pmatrix}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{M}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}\\
\boldsymbol{u}\end{pmatrix}\right]\,,

(A.31)

where
\boldsymbol{u}=(u_{2},\ldots,u_{N})^{\rm T}
. Expanding the exponent,

-\frac{1}{2}\left[au_{1}^{2}+2u_{1}\boldsymbol{b}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{u}\right]\,.

(A.32)

Now complete the square:

\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{u}+2u_{1}\boldsymbol{b}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{u}=\left(\boldsymbol{u}+u_{1}\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}\boldsymbol{b}\right)^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{B}\left(\boldsymbol{u}+u_{1}\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}\boldsymbol{b}\right)-u_{1}^{2}\boldsymbol{b}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}\boldsymbol{b}\,.

(A.33)

Therefore

I(u_{1})\propto\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\left(a-\boldsymbol{b}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}\boldsymbol{b}\right)u_{1}^{2}\right]\,.

(A.34)

Thus the effective inverse variance of
u_{1}
is exactly the Schur complement. This is the elementary mechanism behind the cavity recursions in the main text.

Two further Gaussian identities are especially useful for bipartite and generalized diluted Wishart ensembles. The first is the matrix determinant lemma. If
\boldsymbol{D}
is invertible and
\boldsymbol{U}
and
\boldsymbol{V}
are matrices of size
N\times r
, then

\det\left(\boldsymbol{D}+\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{V}^{\rm T}\right)=\det(\boldsymbol{D})\det\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{r}+\boldsymbol{V}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{D}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}\right)\,.

(A.35)

The proof is a one-line application of

\det(\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{B})=\det(\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{A})

(A.36)

to

\det\left(\boldsymbol{D}+\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{V}^{\rm T}\right)=\det(\boldsymbol{D})\det\left(\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{D}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{V}^{\rm T}\right)\,.

(A.37)

For the diluted Wishart factor node we need only the rank-one case. Let

\boldsymbol{D}={\rm diag}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})^{\rm T}\,,

(A.38)

and consider

I_{\rm rank\mbox{-}1}=\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}\frac{du_{r}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{r=1}^{k}a_{r}u_{r}^{2}+\frac{c}{2}\left(\sum_{r=1}^{k}\xi_{r}u_{r}\right)^{2}\right]\,.

(A.39)

In vector notation,

I_{\rm rank\mbox{-}1}=\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}\frac{du_{r}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm T}\left(\boldsymbol{D}-c\,\boldsymbol{\xi}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\rm T}\right)\boldsymbol{u}\right]\,.

(A.40)

Applying (
A.13
) and then (
A.35
),

\displaystyle I_{\rm rank\mbox{-}1}

\displaystyle=\det\left(\boldsymbol{D}-c\,\boldsymbol{\xi}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\rm T}\right)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=\det(\boldsymbol{D})^{-1/2}\left(1-c\,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{D}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{r=1}^{k}a_{r}^{-1/2}\right)\left(1-c\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{\xi_{r}^{2}}{a_{r}}\right)^{-1/2}\,.

(A.41)

This is exactly the identity used in the diluted Wishart factor update.

The generalized diluted Wishart ensemble requires the rank-two version. Let

\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})^{\rm T}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})^{\rm T}\,,

(A.42)

and consider

I_{\rm rank\mbox{-}2}=\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}\frac{du_{r}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm T}\left(\boldsymbol{D}-\frac{1}{2d}(\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{y}^{\rm T}+\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{x}^{\rm T})\right)\boldsymbol{u}\right]\,.

(A.43)

Write

\boldsymbol{U}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{x}&\boldsymbol{y}\end{pmatrix},\hskip 22.99988pt\boldsymbol{V}=\frac{1}{2d}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{y}&\boldsymbol{x}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(A.44)

so that

\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{V}^{\rm T}=\frac{1}{2d}(\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{y}^{\rm T}+\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{x}^{\rm T})\,.

(A.45)

Then

\det\left(\boldsymbol{D}-\frac{1}{2d}(\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{y}^{\rm T}+\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{x}^{\rm T})\right)=\det(\boldsymbol{D})\det\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{2}-\boldsymbol{V}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{D}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}\right)\,.

(A.46)

Define

S_{xx}=\boldsymbol{x}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{D}^{-1}\boldsymbol{x}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptS_{xy}=\boldsymbol{x}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{D}^{-1}\boldsymbol{y}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptS_{yy}=\boldsymbol{y}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{D}^{-1}\boldsymbol{y}\,.

(A.47)

Then

\boldsymbol{V}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{D}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}=\frac{1}{2d}\begin{pmatrix}S_{xy}&S_{yy}\\
S_{xx}&S_{xy}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(A.48)

and therefore

\displaystyle\det\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{2}-\boldsymbol{V}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{D}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{S_{xy}}{2d}\right)^{2}-\frac{S_{xx}S_{yy}}{4d^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2d-S_{xy})^{2}-S_{xx}S_{yy}}{4d^{2}}\,.

(A.49)

Hence

I_{\rm rank\mbox{-}2}=\det(\boldsymbol{D})^{-1/2}\left[\frac{4d^{2}}{(2d-S_{xy})^{2}-S_{xx}S_{yy}}\right]^{1/2}\,.

(A.50)

This is the finite-rank Gaussian identity behind the generalized diluted Wishart factor contribution.

We now collect the non-Hermitian conventions. Write

z=x+iy\,,\hskip 22.99988ptd^{2}z=dx\,dy\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\delta^{(2)}(z-z_{0})=\delta(x-x_{0})\delta(y-y_{0})\,.

(A.51)

We use the Wirtinger derivatives

\partial_{z}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{x}-i\partial_{y}\right)\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\partial_{z^{*}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{x}+i\partial_{y}\right)\,,

(A.52)

so that

\Delta=\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{y}^{2}=4\partial_{z}\partial_{z^{*}}\,.

(A.53)

The basic distributional identity is

\partial_{z^{*}}\frac{1}{z-z_{0}}=\pi\delta^{(2)}(z-z_{0})\,.

(A.54)

One way to understand (
A.54
) is to test it against a smooth compactly supported function
\varphi
and integrate by parts:

\int d^{2}z\frac{\partial_{z^{*}}\varphi(z,z^{*})}{z-z_{0}}=-\pi\varphi(z_{0},z_{0}^{*})\,,

(A.55)

which is the Cauchy–Pompeiu formula in distributional form.

For a fixed finite matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
, the trace resolvent satisfies the meromorphic identity

g(z)=\frac{1}{N}{\rm Tr}(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\frac{1}{z-z_{\alpha}}\,,

(A.56)

where the eigenvalues are counted with algebraic multiplicity. Therefore, at finite
N
in the distributional sense,

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\partial_{z^{*}}g(z)\,.

(A.57)

For non-normal random matrices, however, this identity is not a stable starting point for large-
N
spectral-density calculations. Near the spectrum, the relevant regularization is controlled by the small singular values of
z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}
, and the limiting non-holomorphic field is obtained by Hermitization.

A convenient Hermitized block matrix is

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)=\begin{pmatrix}\eta\boldsymbol{I}&i(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})\\
i(z^{*}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger})&\eta\boldsymbol{I}\end{pmatrix}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\eta>0\,.

(A.58)

At the level of determinants, this convention is equivalent, up to a
z
-independent phase, to the form with diagonal blocks
i\eta\boldsymbol{I}
and off-diagonal blocks
z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}
and
z^{*}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger}
used in the non-Hermitian sections. Such
z
-independent factors do not affect the logarithmic-potential formulas for the density.
Let

\boldsymbol{C}=z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}\,.

(A.59)

Then

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)=\begin{pmatrix}\eta\boldsymbol{I}&i\boldsymbol{C}\\
i\boldsymbol{C}^{\dagger}&\eta\boldsymbol{I}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(A.60)

Using the block determinant formula with the upper-left block
\eta\boldsymbol{I}
,

\displaystyle\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)

\displaystyle=\det(\eta\boldsymbol{I})\det\left(\eta\boldsymbol{I}-i\boldsymbol{C}^{\dagger}(\eta\boldsymbol{I})^{-1}i\boldsymbol{C}\right)

\displaystyle=\eta^{N}\det\left(\eta\boldsymbol{I}+\eta^{-1}\boldsymbol{C}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{C}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\eta^{2}\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{C}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{C}\right)\,.

(A.61)

Since
\boldsymbol{C}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{C}
and
\boldsymbol{C}\boldsymbol{C}^{\dagger}
have the same nonzero eigenvalues,

\det\left(\eta^{2}\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{C}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{C}\right)=\det\left(\eta^{2}\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{C}\boldsymbol{C}^{\dagger}\right)\,,

(A.62)

and therefore

\det\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z,\eta)=\det\left[(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})(z^{*}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger})+\eta^{2}\boldsymbol{I}\right]\,.

(A.63)

This is the determinant identity used throughout the non-Hermitian sections.

The regularized logarithmic potential is then

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})=\frac{1}{N}\log\det\left[(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})(z^{*}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A}^{\dagger})+\eta^{2}\boldsymbol{I}\right]\,.

(A.64)

The non-Hermitian spectral density is recovered through

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\partial_{z^{*}}\partial_{z}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})\,,

(A.65)

or, equivalently,

\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\Delta\Phi_{\boldsymbol{A},\eta}(z,z^{*})\,.

(A.66)

Equations (
A.58
)–(
A.66
) fix the non-Hermitian determinant and density conventions used in the main text.

To summarize, the identities collected in this appendix are the minimal algebraic toolbox behind the whole lecture-note construction. Equations (
A.8
) and (
A.23
) explain how Hermitian spectral densities are obtained from resolvents and Gaussian partition functions. Equations (
A.25
) and (
A.34
) explain why local Gaussian marginals close under cavity recursion. Equations (
A.41
) and (
A.50
) are the finite-rank Gaussian formulas needed in diluted Wishart and generalized diluted Wishart ensembles. Finally, equations (
A.54
) and (
A.63
)–(
A.66
) fix the non-Hermitian conventions used in the Hermitization approach. Once these identities are in place, all the cavity and replica constructions in the main text follow from them by repeated application on sparse graphical structures.

Appendix B
Dense-limit reductions

In the main text we used the sparse cavity equations as the primary description of the spectrum, and we repeatedly stated that the classical dense laws are recovered when the mean connectivity becomes large. The purpose of this appendix is to show this explicitly. The derivations are simple once one identifies the correct scaling. The general mechanism is always the same: each cavity sum contains many terms of size
O(c^{-1})
or
O(d^{-1})
, the fluctuations of the sum are therefore of order
c^{-1/2}
or
d^{-1/2}
, and the sums entering the denominators self-average. In homogeneous cases this collapse is literally a collapse of the message law to a deterministic fixed point; with diagonal disorder or distinguished edge weights, the remaining local variables are averaged only after the dense self-energy has been formed. In that limit the full distributional cavity equation reduces to an ordinary deterministic self-consistency equation.

The order of limits matters. Throughout this appendix one should understand that the thermodynamic limit is taken first, and only afterwards the mean connectivity is sent to infinity. If one mixes the two limits carelessly, one may confuse a genuinely dense law with a finite-connectivity law evaluated at large but finite degree.

We begin with sparse symmetric matrices. Consider

A_{ij}=D_{i}\delta_{ij}+C_{ij}J_{ij}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptA_{ij}=A_{ji}\,,

(B.1)

on a sparse graph of mean degree
c
, and impose the dense scaling

J_{ij}=\frac{\widetilde{J}_{ij}}{\sqrt{c}}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\int d\widetilde{J}p_{\widetilde{J}}(\widetilde{J})\widetilde{J}=0\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\int d\widetilde{J}p_{\widetilde{J}}(\widetilde{J})\widetilde{J}^{2}=\sigma^{2}\,.

(B.2)

The cavity equation is

G_{i\to j}(z)=\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\widetilde{J}_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)}\,.

(B.3)

We now analyze the sum in the denominator. Since the degree of a typical vertex is
k_{i}=c+O(\sqrt{c})
, the number of terms is
O(c)
. Each term is
O(c^{-1})
, because
\widetilde{J}_{i\ell}^{2}=O(1)
and
G_{\ell\to i}(z)=O(1)
away from poles. Hence the whole sum is
O(1)
. More precisely, if the message distribution has a finite first moment, then by the law of large numbers

\frac{1}{c}\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\widetilde{J}_{i\ell}^{2}G_{\ell\to i}(z)\longrightarrow\sigma^{2}g(z)\,,

(B.4)

where

g(z)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}{\rm Tr}(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}

(B.5)

is the deterministic limiting Stieltjes transform. The exclusion of one neighbor becomes irrelevant in the dense limit, because removing one term changes the sum by
O(c^{-1})
. Therefore the cavity and full Green functions coincide at leading order:

G_{i\to j}(z)=G_{i}(z)+O(c^{-1})\,.

(B.6)

Substituting (
B.4
) into (
B.3
) gives

G_{i}(z)\longrightarrow\frac{1}{z-D_{i}-\sigma^{2}g(z)}\,.

(B.7)

Averaging over the diagonal disorder
D_{i}
with law
p_{D}(D)
, we find

g(z)=\int dDp_{D}(D)\frac{1}{z-D-\sigma^{2}g(z)}\,.

(B.8)

Equation (
B.8
) is the Pastur self-consistency equation for a Wigner-type ensemble with diagonal disorder
[
66
]
. It is the dense reduction of the sparse distributional cavity equation.

The most familiar special case is
D_{i}=0
. Then (
B.8
) becomes

g(z)=\frac{1}{z-\sigma^{2}g(z)}\,.

(B.9)

Multiplying both sides by the denominator gives

\sigma^{2}g(z)^{2}-zg(z)+1=0\,.

(B.10)

Solving the quadratic,

g(z)=\frac{z\pm\sqrt{z^{2}-4\sigma^{2}}}{2\sigma^{2}}\,.

(B.11)

To select the correct branch, we impose two conditions. First, for large
|z|
one must have

g(z)\sim\frac{1}{z}\,,

(B.12)

because

(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})^{-1}=\frac{1}{z}\boldsymbol{I}+O(z^{-2})\,.

(B.13)

Second, with the convention
z=\lambda-i\epsilon
,
\epsilon>0
, one must have
{\rm Im}\,g(z)>0
. These two conditions give

g(z)=\frac{z-\sqrt{z^{2}-4\sigma^{2}}}{2\sigma^{2}}\,,

(B.14)

where the square root is chosen so that
\sqrt{z^{2}-4\sigma^{2}}\sim z
for large
|z|
. For
\lambda\in[-2\sigma,2\sigma]
and
z=\lambda-i0^{+}
,

\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-4\sigma^{2}}=-i\sqrt{4\sigma^{2}-\lambda^{2}}\,,

(B.15)

and therefore

{\rm Im}g(\lambda-i0^{+})=\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{4\sigma^{2}-\lambda^{2}}\,.

(B.16)

Using the convention

\rho(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}{\rm Im}g(\lambda-i0^{+})\,,

(B.17)

we obtain the semicircle law

\rho_{\rm sc}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{4\sigma^{2}-\lambda^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{|\lambda|\leq 2\sigma}\,.

(B.18)

Thus the sparse symmetric cavity equations reduce, in the large-connectivity scaling, to the classical Wigner law
[
89
,
46
]
.

The same idea applies to diluted Wishart matrices. Recall that

\boldsymbol{W}=\frac{1}{d}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptX_{i}^{\mu}=B_{i}^{\mu}\xi_{i}^{\mu}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Prob}(B_{i}^{\mu}=1)=\frac{d}{N}\,,

(B.19)

with

P=\frac{N}{\alpha}\,.

(B.20)

The cavity equations are

U_{\mu\to i}(z)=\frac{(\xi_{i}^{\mu})^{2}}{1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}(\xi_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}(z)}\,,

(B.21)

and

G_{i\to\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i\setminus\mu}U_{\nu\to i}(z)}\,.

(B.22)

We assume

\int d\xi p_{\xi}(\xi)\xi^{2}=1

(B.23)

for convenience. The factor degree is
d+O(\sqrt{d})
, and the variable degree is
d/\alpha+O(\sqrt{d})
. As before, the cavity/full distinction disappears at leading order:

G_{i\to\mu}(z)=G_{i}(z)+O(d^{-1})\,,\hskip 22.99988ptU_{\mu\to i}(z)=U_{\mu\to i}^{\rm full}(z)+O(d^{-1})\,.

(B.24)

Now consider the denominator of (
B.21
). It contains
O(d)
terms, each of order
d^{-1}
, so by the law of large numbers

\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}(\xi_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}(z)\longrightarrow G(z)\,,

(B.25)

where
G(z)
is now the deterministic limiting diagonal Green function on the variable side. Substituting (
B.25
) into (
B.21
) gives

U_{\mu\to i}(z)\longrightarrow\frac{(\xi_{i}^{\mu})^{2}}{1-G(z)}\,.

(B.26)

The variable node receives
d/\alpha+O(\sqrt{d})
such terms, but each enters the denominator through the contribution
d^{-1}U_{\nu\to i}
. Averaging over
\xi_{i}^{\mu}
and summing, we find the deterministic self-energy

\Sigma(z)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\nu\in\partial i}U_{\nu\to i}(z)\longrightarrow\frac{1}{\alpha[1-G(z)]}\,.

(B.27)

Hence

G(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha[1-G(z)]}}\,.

(B.28)

This is the dense-limit equation in the normalization and message convention used in the main text, namely
\boldsymbol{W}=d^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}
with
U_{\mu\to i}
not divided by
d
.

To compare with the conventional Marčenko–Pastur law, it is better to rescale the matrix as

\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}=\alpha\boldsymbol{W}\,.

(B.29)

If
\widehat{g}(z)
denotes the Stieltjes transform of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}
, then

G(z)=\alpha\widehat{g}(\alpha z)\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\widehat{g}(z)=\frac{1}{\alpha}G\!\left(\frac{z}{\alpha}\right)\,.

(B.30)

Substituting (
B.30
) into (
B.28
), with
z
replaced by
z/\alpha
, gives

\widehat{g}(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\alpha\widehat{g}(z)}}\,.

(B.31)

Multiplying both sides by the denominator yields

\alpha z\widehat{g}(z)^{2}+(1-\alpha-z)\widehat{g}(z)+1=0\,.

(B.32)

Solving the quadratic,

\widehat{g}(z)=\frac{z+\alpha-1-\sqrt{(z-\lambda_{-})(z-\lambda_{+})}}{2\alpha z}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\lambda_{\pm}=(1\pm\sqrt{\alpha})^{2}\,,

(B.33)

where the branch is fixed by
\widehat{g}(z)\sim z^{-1}
for large
|z|
. Therefore

\rho_{\rm MP}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha\lambda}\sqrt{(\lambda_{+}-\lambda)(\lambda-\lambda_{-})}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda_{-}\leq\lambda\leq\lambda_{+}}

(B.34)

for the rescaled matrix
\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}
, together with an atom of weight
1-1/\alpha
at the origin when
\alpha>1

[
43
,
7
]
. Returning to the normalization used in the main text, the absolutely continuous support is divided by
\alpha
, and the zero atom, when present, keeps the same weight:

\lambda_{\pm}^{(W)}=\frac{(1\pm\sqrt{\alpha})^{2}}{\alpha}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\rho_{W}(\lambda)=\alpha\rho_{\rm MP}(\alpha\lambda)\,.

(B.35)

Thus the diluted Wishart cavity equations reduce to the Marčenko–Pastur law in the dense limit.

The same reduction can be carried out for the generalized diluted Wishart ensemble. Recall that

\boldsymbol{F}=\frac{1}{2d}\left(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{Y}^{\rm T}+\boldsymbol{Y}\boldsymbol{X}^{\rm T}\right)\,,

(B.36)

with nonzero edge weights
(x_{i}^{\mu},y_{i}^{\mu})
drawn from the joint law
\varrho(x,y)
. Define the moments

m_{20}=\int dxdy\varrho(x,y)x^{2}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptm_{11}=\int dxdy\varrho(x,y)xy\,,\hskip 22.99988ptm_{02}=\int dxdy\varrho(x,y)y^{2}\,,

(B.37)

and

\Delta=m_{20}m_{02}-m_{11}^{2}\,.

(B.38)

In the finite-connectivity cavity equation, the factor-to-variable self-energy depends on the three sums

S_{xx}^{\mu\to i}=\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}(x_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptS_{xy}^{\mu\to i}=\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}x_{j}^{\mu}y_{j}^{\mu}G_{j\to\mu}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptS_{yy}^{\mu\to i}=\sum_{j\in\partial\mu\setminus i}(y_{j}^{\mu})^{2}G_{j\to\mu}\,.

(B.39)

When
d\to\infty
, these sums self-average:

\frac{S_{xx}^{\mu\to i}}{d}\to m_{20}G(z)\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\frac{S_{xy}^{\mu\to i}}{d}\to m_{11}G(z)\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\frac{S_{yy}^{\mu\to i}}{d}\to m_{02}G(z)\,.

(B.40)

Substituting into the finite-connectivity factor formula, one finds that a single factor message is of order
d^{-1}
:

U_{\mu\to i}(z)\longrightarrow\frac{1}{d}\frac{4xy+y^{2}m_{20}G(z)-2xym_{11}G(z)+x^{2}m_{02}G(z)}{\left(2-m_{11}G(z)\right)^{2}-m_{20}m_{02}G(z)^{2}}\,.

(B.41)

A variable vertex receives
d/\alpha
such contributions. Averaging over
(x,y)
and summing, the self-energy becomes

\Sigma(z)=\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{4m_{11}+2\Delta G(z)}{4-4m_{11}G(z)-\Delta G(z)^{2}}\,.

(B.42)

Hence the dense-limit Green function satisfies

G(z)=\frac{1}{z-\Sigma(z)}=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{4m_{11}+2\Delta G(z)}{4-4m_{11}G(z)-\Delta G(z)^{2}}}\,.

(B.43)

Multiplying through gives the cubic equation

\alpha\Delta zG(z)^{3}+\left[4\alpha m_{11}z+(2-\alpha)\Delta\right]G(z)^{2}+4\left[m_{11}(1-\alpha)-\alpha z\right]G(z)+4\alpha=0\,.

(B.44)

This is the dense reduction of the generalized diluted Wishart cavity equation. Two checks are immediate. If
x=y
, then

m_{20}=m_{11}=m_{02}=1\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\Delta=0\,,

(B.45)

and (
B.43
) reduces to

G(z)=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha[1-G(z)]}}\,,

(B.46)

which is exactly the dense diluted-Wishart equation. If
y=-x
, then
m_{11}=-1
and
\Delta=0
, so one obtains the same equation with the opposite sign in the self-energy, corresponding to a negative Wishart matrix. Thus the dense limit of the generalized ensemble interpolates between positive and negative covariance-type laws, as it should
[
72
]
.

Finally, we consider the dense limit of sparse non-Hermitian matrices. For simplicity we take real entries, zero diagonal, and the paired-support convention used in the sparse non-Hermitian ensemble: for each unordered pair
{i,j}
, the support variable satisfies
C_{ij}=C_{ji}
, the support has mean degree
c\to\infty
, and

A_{ij}=\frac{C_{ij}}{\sqrt{c}}J_{ij}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptA_{ji}=\frac{C_{ij}}{\sqrt{c}}J_{ji}\,.

(B.47)

The pair
(J_{ij},J_{ji})
has moments

\mathbb{E}[J_{ij}]=0\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\mathbb{E}[J_{ij}^{2}]=\sigma^{2}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\mathbb{E}[J_{ij}J_{ji}]=\tau\sigma^{2}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt-1\leq\tau\leq 1\,.

(B.48)

The parameter
\tau
measures the reciprocal correlation between opposite edges. The Hermitized cavity recursion is

\boldsymbol{G}_{i\to j}(z,\eta)=\left[\boldsymbol{Z}(z,\eta)-\sum_{\ell\in\partial i\setminus j}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}\boldsymbol{G}_{\ell\to i}(z,\eta)\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}^{\dagger}\right]^{-1}\,,

(B.49)

where

\boldsymbol{Z}(z,\eta)=\begin{pmatrix}i\eta&z\\
z^{*}&i\eta\end{pmatrix}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{c}}\begin{pmatrix}0&J_{i\ell}\\
J_{\ell i}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(B.50)

Assume, as before, that the message distribution collapses onto a deterministic matrix

\boldsymbol{G}(z,\eta)=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\,.

(B.51)

We now compute the average contribution of one edge explicitly:

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}\boldsymbol{G}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}^{\dagger}=\frac{1}{c}\begin{pmatrix}J_{i\ell}^{2}d&J_{i\ell}J_{\ell i}c\\
J_{i\ell}J_{\ell i}b&J_{\ell i}^{2}a\end{pmatrix}\,.

(B.52)

Averaging over the pair
(J_{i\ell},J_{\ell i})
and summing over
c
neighbors, we obtain the deterministic self-energy

\sum_{\ell\in\partial i}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}\boldsymbol{G}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{i\ell}^{\dagger}\longrightarrow\sigma^{2}\begin{pmatrix}d&\tau c\\
\tau b&a\end{pmatrix}\,.

(B.53)

Thus the dense Hermitized fixed-point equation is

\boldsymbol{G}(z,\eta)=\left[\begin{pmatrix}i\eta&z\\
z^{*}&i\eta\end{pmatrix}-\sigma^{2}\begin{pmatrix}d&\tau c\\
\tau b&a\end{pmatrix}\right]^{-1}\,.

(B.54)

Outside the spectral support the solution is holomorphic. In that regime the diagonal components vanish as
\eta\downarrow 0
, so we set

a=d=0\,,\hskip 22.99988ptc=g(z)\,,\hskip 22.99988ptb=g(z)^{*}\,.

(B.55)

Substituting into (
B.54
) and setting
\eta=0
, we must invert

\begin{pmatrix}0&z-\tau\sigma^{2}g(z)\\
z^{*}-\tau\sigma^{2}g(z)^{*}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(B.56)

The inverse of

\begin{pmatrix}0&B\\
C&0\end{pmatrix}

(B.57)

is

\begin{pmatrix}0&C^{-1}\\
B^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(B.58)

so we obtain

g(z)=\frac{1}{z-\tau\sigma^{2}g(z)}\,.

(B.59)

This is the dense non-Hermitian resolvent equation outside the support. For
\tau=0
it reduces to
g(z)=1/z
, the holomorphic branch of the circular law. For
\tau=1
it becomes the Hermitian Wigner equation.

To locate the spectral boundary we linearize around the holomorphic branch. Let
a
and
d
be infinitesimal. Write

\boldsymbol{M}=\begin{pmatrix}-\sigma^{2}d&z-\tau\sigma^{2}g\\
z^{*}-\tau\sigma^{2}g^{*}&-\sigma^{2}a\end{pmatrix}\,,

(B.60)

so that

\boldsymbol{G}=\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}\,.

(B.61)

The determinant of
\boldsymbol{M}
is

\Delta=\sigma^{4}ad-|z-\tau\sigma^{2}g|^{2}\,.

(B.62)

Using the formula for the inverse of a
2\times 2
matrix,

a=\frac{-\sigma^{2}a}{\Delta}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptd=\frac{-\sigma^{2}d}{\Delta}\,.

(B.63)

A nonzero infinitesimal solution requires

\Delta=-\sigma^{2}\,.

(B.64)

On the holomorphic branch
a=d=0
, so (
B.62
) reduces to

\Delta=-|z-\tau\sigma^{2}g|^{2}\,.

(B.65)

Hence the boundary of the spectrum is determined by

|z-\tau\sigma^{2}g(z)|^{2}=\sigma^{2}\,.

(B.66)

Using (
B.59
), we have

z-\tau\sigma^{2}g(z)=\frac{1}{g(z)}\,,

(B.67)

so (
B.66
) becomes

|g(z)|=\frac{1}{\sigma}\,.

(B.68)

We may therefore parametrize

g(z)=\frac{e^{-i\theta}}{\sigma}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt0\leq\theta<2\pi\,.

(B.69)

Substituting into

z=\frac{1}{g(z)}+\tau\sigma^{2}g(z)\,,

(B.70)

we find

z(\theta)=\sigma e^{i\theta}+\tau\sigma e^{-i\theta}\,.

(B.71)

Separating real and imaginary parts,

x(\theta)=\sigma(1+\tau)\cos\theta\,,\hskip 22.99988pty(\theta)=\sigma(1-\tau)\sin\theta\,.

(B.72)

Thus the dense limit of the sparse non-Hermitian cavity equations recovers the support of the elliptic law
[
82
]
. The non-holomorphic branch gives the corresponding bulk density; the calculation above fixes the boundary. The special case
\tau=0
gives the circle

x(\theta)=\sigma\cos\theta\,,\hskip 22.99988pty(\theta)=\sigma\sin\theta\,,

(B.73)

that is, the circular law
[
32
,
33
,
84
]
. The special case
\tau=1
gives the interval

x(\theta)=2\sigma\cos\theta\,,\hskip 22.99988pty(\theta)=0\,,

(B.74)

which is exactly the support of the Hermitian semicircle law.

The general lesson is now clear. In every ensemble treated in the main text, the sparse cavity equations contain a sum of many weak contributions. When the connectivity becomes large and the edge weights are scaled appropriately, those sums self-average. In homogeneous cases the message distribution collapses to a delta function; with residual diagonal or edge-weight randomness, the dense reduction is instead a deterministic self-consistency equation for the averaged Green function or self-energy. For sparse symmetric matrices this gives the Pastur equation and the semicircle law. For diluted Wishart matrices it gives the Marčenko–Pastur equation. For the generalized diluted Wishart ensemble it gives a deterministic cubic equation interpolating between covariance and cross-correlation limits. For sparse non-Hermitian matrices it gives the circular and elliptic dense limits. These reductions provide one of the most important checks on the finite-connectivity formalism developed in the main text.

Appendix C
Population-dynamics algorithms

Population dynamics is the numerical realization of a distributional self-consistency equation. Since this algorithm is used repeatedly in these notes, it is worth deriving it carefully once and for all. We will do this in a way that makes the logic transparent. First we derive the generic algorithm from a fixed-point equation for a probability law. Then we specialize the construction to the scalar messages of sparse symmetric matrices, the two-population structure of diluted Wishart ensembles, the finite-rank factor updates of generalized diluted Wishart matrices, the matrix-valued messages of sparse non-Hermitian ensembles, and finally the tilted populations that appear in large-deviation and conditioned-density problems. The algorithm itself is standard in the statistical mechanics of disordered systems
[
51
,
52
,
50
]
; what matters here is how it arises from the specific cavity equations of sparse random matrices
[
56
,
79
,
77
,
83
,
47
]
.

We begin with a generic distributional fixed-point equation. Let
h
denote a cavity message. Depending on the problem,
h
may be a complex number, a tuple of complex numbers, or a small matrix. Suppose the cavity law
\mathcal{P}(h)
satisfies

\mathcal{P}(h)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q_{\ell}\int d\omega\Pi_{\ell}(\omega)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}dh_{r}\mathcal{P}(h_{r})\right]\delta\left(h-\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(\omega;h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell})\right)\,,

(C.1)

where
q_{\ell}
is the cavity branching law,
\omega
denotes the local disorder variables,
\Pi_{\ell}(\omega)
is their distribution for a neighborhood with
\ell
incoming messages, and
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
is the local cavity map. This equation says the following: to generate one sample from
\mathcal{P}
, one first draws the number
\ell
of incoming neighbors, then draws the local disorder, then draws
\ell
incoming messages independently from
\mathcal{P}
, and finally applies the deterministic map
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
.

The idea of population dynamics is to replace the unknown law
\mathcal{P}
by an empirical measure supported on a large population of messages. Let

\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{M}(h)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{M}\delta\left(h-h^{(\alpha)}\right)\,,

(C.2)

where

\left\{h^{(1)},h^{(2)},\ldots,h^{(M)}\right\}

(C.3)

is the current population. To understand the algorithm, we do not start from a recipe but from the action of the fixed-point map on test functions. Let
\varphi(h)
be a bounded test function. Integrating (
C.1
) against
\varphi
gives

\displaystyle\int dh\varphi(h)\mathcal{P}(h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q_{\ell}\int d\omega\Pi_{\ell}(\omega)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}dh_{r}\mathcal{P}(h_{r})\right]\varphi\left(\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(\omega;h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell})\right)\,.

(C.4)

Now replace
\mathcal{P}
by the empirical measure (
C.2
). Then

\displaystyle\int dh\varphi(h)\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{M}(h)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{M}\varphi\left(h^{(\alpha)}\right)\,,

(C.5)

and the right-hand side of (
C.4
) becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q_{\ell}\int d\omega\Pi_{\ell}(\omega)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}dh_{r}\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{M}(h_{r})\right]\varphi\left(\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(\omega;h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q_{\ell}\int d\omega\,\Pi_{\ell}(\omega)\frac{1}{M^{\ell}}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell}=1}^{M}\varphi\left(\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(\omega;h^{(\alpha_{1})},\ldots,h^{(\alpha_{\ell})})\right)\,.

(C.6)

Equation (
C.6
) is simply an expectation over random draws. It says that if one draws
\ell
, then draws
\omega
, then draws
\ell
indices
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell}
independently and uniformly from
\{1,\ldots,M\}
, and finally defines

h_{\rm new}=\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(\omega;h^{(\alpha_{1})},\ldots,h^{(\alpha_{\ell})})\,,

(C.7)

then

\mathbb{E}\left[\varphi(h_{\rm new})\middle|\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{M}\right]=\int dh\varphi(h)\mathcal{T}[\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{M}](h)\,,

(C.8)

where
\mathcal{T}
is the fixed-point map defined by the right-hand side of (
C.1
). This is the derivation of the elementary population update.

There are two standard ways to use (
C.7
). In the synchronous, or batch, version one generates a whole new population

h_{\rm new}^{(1)},\ldots,h_{\rm new}^{(M)}

(C.9)

from the current one and then replaces the entire population at once. In the asynchronous version one picks a random index
\beta\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
and performs the replacement

h^{(\beta)}\leftarrow h_{\rm new}\,.

(C.10)

The asynchronous version is the one most often used in practice. Let us derive its mean action on test functions. Define

\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{M}^{\prime}(h)=\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{M}(h)+\frac{1}{M}\left[\delta(h-h_{\rm new})-\delta(h-h^{(\beta)})\right]\,.

(C.11)

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int dh\varphi(h)\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{M}^{\prime}(h)\middle|\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{M}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{1}{M}\right)\int dh\varphi(h)\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{M}(h)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.49994pt+\frac{1}{M}\int dh\varphi(h)\mathcal{T}[\widehat{\mathcal{P}}_{M}](h)\,.

(C.12)

Thus one asynchronous replacement is a stochastic Euler step toward the fixed-point map, and one sweep of
M
such replacements approximates one application of
\mathcal{T}
to the population. This is the mathematical content of population dynamics.

The next step is to explain how observables are measured once the cavity law has been approximated. Suppose the corresponding site law is

\mathcal{P}_{\rm site}(g)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}p_{k}\int d\omega\Pi^{\rm site}_{k}(\omega)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}dh_{r}\mathcal{P}(h_{r})\right]\delta\left(g-\mathcal{G}_{k}(\omega;h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\right)\,,

(C.13)

where
p_{k}
is the full degree distribution and
\mathcal{G}_{k}
is the site map. If
\mathcal{O}(g)
is a site observable, then

\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle_{\rm site}=\int dg\mathcal{P}_{\rm site}(g)\mathcal{O}(g)\,.

(C.14)

The empirical estimator is obtained by drawing
n_{\rm obs}
independent site samples from (
C.13
) with the current population and setting

\widehat{\mathcal{O}}=\frac{1}{n_{\rm obs}}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\rm obs}}\mathcal{O}(g_{m})\,.

(C.15)

Thus the population itself approximates the cavity law, while the site observable is measured by a second Monte Carlo average on top of the population.

We now specialize the generic construction to the main ensembles of these notes. For sparse symmetric matrices the message is a complex scalar Green function
G
, and the fixed-point equation is

\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q_{\ell}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}dG_{r}\mathcal{P}_{\rm cav}(G_{r})\,dJ_{r}\,p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}J_{r}^{2}G_{r}}\right)\,,

(C.16)

with

z=\lambda-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon>0\,.

(C.17)

The elementary population update follows directly from (
C.7
):

G_{\rm new}=\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}J_{r}^{2}G^{(\alpha_{r})}}\,.

(C.18)

The corresponding site sample is

G_{\rm site}=\frac{1}{z-D-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{k}J_{r}^{2}G^{(\alpha_{r})}}\,,

(C.19)

where now
k
is drawn from
p_{k}
rather than
q_{\ell}
. The regularized spectral density is therefore estimated by

\widehat{\rho}_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi n_{\rm obs}}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\rm obs}}{\rm Im}G_{{\rm site},m}\,.

(C.20)

There are three elementary consistency checks for the scalar population. First, all messages should satisfy

{\rm Im}G^{(\alpha)}>0

(C.21)

with the convention
z=\lambda-i\epsilon
,
\epsilon>0
. Second, the density estimator (
C.20
) should be nonnegative. Third, numerical integration over
\lambda
should give approximately one when the density is measured over a sufficiently large interval and the regularization is small.

The diluted Wishart ensemble requires two message laws. The variable-to-factor message is a complex scalar
G
, while the factor-to-variable message is a self-energy
U
. We use the convention of the main text:
U
is not divided by
d
, and the actual contribution to the variable denominator is
d^{-1}U
. The distributional equations are

\mathcal{Q}(U)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}q_{k}^{(\rm f)}\int d\xi p_{\xi}(\xi)\prod_{r=1}^{k}\left[dG_{r}\mathcal{P}(G_{r})d\xi_{r}p_{\xi}(\xi_{r})\right]\delta\left(U-\frac{\xi^{2}}{1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{r=1}^{k}\xi_{r}^{2}G_{r}}\right)\,,

(C.22)

and

\mathcal{P}(G)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q_{\ell}^{(\rm v)}\int\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}\left[dU_{r}\mathcal{Q}(U_{r})\right]\delta\left(G-\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}U_{r}}\right)\,.

(C.23)

The derivation of the algorithm is now a direct application of the generic one, but with two populations,

\left\{G^{(1)},\ldots,G^{(M_{G})}\right\}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\left\{U^{(1)},\ldots,U^{(M_{U})}\right\}\,.

(C.24)

A factor update is

U_{\rm new}=\frac{\xi^{2}}{1-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{r=1}^{k}\xi_{r}^{2}G^{(\alpha_{r})}}\,,

(C.25)

where
k\sim q_{k}^{(\rm f)}
, while a variable update is

G_{\rm new}=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}U^{(\beta_{r})}}\,,

(C.26)

where
\ell\sim q_{\ell}^{(\rm v)}
. The site sample is

G_{\rm site}=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{r=1}^{k}U^{(\beta_{r})}}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptk\sim p_{k}^{(\rm v)}\,,

(C.27)

and the density estimator is again

\widehat{\rho}_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi n_{\rm obs}}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\rm obs}}{\rm Im}G_{{\rm site},m}\,.

(C.28)

The positivity of the Wishart spectrum implies a further check away from the origin:

\widehat{\rho}_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\approx 0\hskip 22.99988pt\text{for }\lambda<0

(C.29)

for negative
\lambda
on scales not touching a possible zero atom, up to the Lorentzian broadening produced by finite
\epsilon
.

The generalized diluted Wishart and sparse cross-correlation ensemble is only slightly more complicated at the algorithmic level, because the factor contribution remains finite rank. The self-energy distribution is determined by the map

U=\frac{4dxy+y^{2}S_{xx}-2xyS_{xy}+x^{2}S_{yy}}{(2d-S_{xy})^{2}-S_{xx}S_{yy}}\,,

(C.30)

where

S_{xx}=\sum_{r=1}^{k}x_{r}^{2}G_{r}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptS_{xy}=\sum_{r=1}^{k}x_{r}y_{r}G_{r}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptS_{yy}=\sum_{r=1}^{k}y_{r}^{2}G_{r}\,.

(C.31)

Thus a factor update consists of drawing
k\sim q_{k}^{(\rm f)}
, drawing
k
incoming Green functions from the
G
population, drawing the corresponding pairs
(x_{r},y_{r})
from the local weight law
\varrho(x,y)
, drawing an additional distinguished pair
(x,y)
, forming the sums (
C.31
), and substituting them into (
C.30
). In the generalized diluted Wishart convention used here,
U
is already the full contribution to the variable denominator, so the variable update is

G_{\rm new}=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}U^{(\beta_{r})}}\,,

(C.32)

where
\ell
is drawn from the variable excess-degree law
q_{\ell}^{(\rm v)}
. The corresponding site sample is

G_{\rm site}=\frac{1}{z-\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{k_{\rm site}}U^{(\beta_{r})}}\,,

(C.33)

where
k_{\rm site}
is drawn from the full variable-degree law
p_{k}^{(\rm v)}
. The density estimator is

\widehat{\rho}_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi n_{\rm obs}}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\rm obs}}{\rm Im}G_{{\rm site},m}\,.

(C.34)

Thus the only extra numerical cost is the evaluation of the three sums and the rational map (
C.30
). This is one of the reasons why generalized diluted Wishart ensembles remain computationally accessible despite their richer algebraic structure
[
72
]
.

For sparse non-Hermitian matrices the message becomes a
2\times 2
matrix. Denote one such message by

\boldsymbol{G}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\,.

(C.35)

The distributional fixed-point equation is

\mathcal{P}(\boldsymbol{G})=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q_{\ell}\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}d\boldsymbol{G}_{r}\mathcal{P}(\boldsymbol{G}_{r})dJ_{r}dK_{r}p_{J_{1},J_{2}}(J_{r},K_{r})\right]\delta\left(\boldsymbol{G}-\left[\boldsymbol{Z}-\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}(J_{r},K_{r})\,\boldsymbol{G}_{r}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}(J_{r},K_{r})^{\dagger}\right]^{-1}\right)\,,

(C.36)

with

\boldsymbol{Z}=\begin{pmatrix}i\eta&z\\
z^{*}&i\eta\end{pmatrix},\hskip 22.99988pt\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}(J,K)=\begin{pmatrix}0&J\\
K^{*}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(C.37)

The empirical population is now a set of matrices

\left\{\boldsymbol{G}^{(1)},\ldots,\boldsymbol{G}^{(M)}\right\},

(C.38)

and the elementary update is simply

\boldsymbol{G}_{\rm new}=\left[\boldsymbol{Z}-\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}(J_{r},K_{r})\boldsymbol{G}^{(\alpha_{r})}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}(J_{r},K_{r})^{\dagger}\right]^{-1}\,.

(C.39)

The site sample is generated by the same formula with the full degree law
p_{k}
. The regularized non-Hermitian resolvent field is estimated by

\widehat{g}_{\eta}(z,z^{*})=\frac{1}{n_{\rm obs}}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\rm obs}}\left[\boldsymbol{G}_{{\rm site},m}\right]_{21}\,,

(C.40)

and the regularized density by

\widehat{\rho}_{\eta}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\partial_{z^{*}}\widehat{g}_{\eta}(z,z^{*})\,.

(C.41)

In practice, one either approximates the Wirtinger derivative by finite differences on a grid in the complex plane or introduces companion populations for the response of the messages with respect to
z^{*}
. The finite-difference route is simpler and is usually sufficient for plotting the density.

The large-deviation and conditioned-density problems require one more ingredient, namely weighted populations. The formal structure is again completely transparent. Suppose the tilted cavity law satisfies

\mathcal{P}_{s}(h)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{C}_{s}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q_{\ell}\int d\omega\Pi_{\ell}(\omega)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}dh_{r}\mathcal{P}_{s}(h_{r})\right]w_{s}(\omega;h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell})\delta\left(h-\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(\omega;h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell})\right)\,,

(C.42)

where
\mathcal{C}_{s}
is the normalization

\mathcal{C}_{s}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q_{\ell}\int d\omega\Pi_{\ell}(\omega)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{\ell}dh_{r}\mathcal{P}_{s}(h_{r})\right]w_{s}(\omega;h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell})\,.

(C.43)

This is the generic form of the tilted distributions that appear in the index problem, in conditioned spectral densities, and in the non-Hermitian number-statistics problem. The local map
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
is the same cavity map as before. The new ingredient is only the weight
w_{s}
, which is built from the local contribution of the observable used in the tilt
[
48
,
70
,
71
,
75
]
. The normalization
\mathcal{C}_{s}
in (
C.43
) is the normalization of a tilted message update. In concrete spectral-count calculations, the scaled cumulant-generating function is obtained from the corresponding Bethe combination of local normalizations, with the appropriate site, factor, and edge terms for the ensemble under consideration.

To derive the weighted algorithm, integrate (
C.42
) against a test function
\varphi
:

\displaystyle\int dh\varphi(h)\mathcal{P}_{s}(h)=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[w_{s}\varphi(h_{\rm new})\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[w_{s}\right]}\,,

(C.44)

where the expectation is taken with respect to the sampling of
\ell
,
\omega
, and the incoming messages from the current population, and where
h_{\rm new}
is still defined by (
C.7
). Equation (
C.44
) immediately suggests the batch algorithm. One generates
M
candidates

\widetilde{h}^{(1)},\ldots,\widetilde{h}^{(M)}

(C.45)

and their associated weights

w^{(1)},\ldots,w^{(M)}.

(C.46)

When the weights are nonnegative, the new population is obtained by resampling the candidates with probabilities

\pi_{m}=\frac{w^{(m)}}{\sum_{n=1}^{M}w^{(n)}}\,.

(C.47)

This is importance sampling in message space. If a complex or sign-changing replica representation is used directly, one must keep explicit signed or complex weights rather than interpreting (
C.47
) as probabilities. The empirical average of
\varphi
in the resampled population converges to the right-hand side of (
C.44
) whenever the probability-resampling interpretation is valid. In lecture-note language, the real-tilt large-deviation algorithms used here are ordinary population dynamics plus importance-resampling.

For the Hermitian index problem, the message is not one Green function but a tuple,

h=\left(G^{-},G^{+}\right)

(C.48)

or, for conditioned densities,

h=\left(G^{-},G^{+},G^{0}\right)\,,

(C.49)

with

G^{-}=G(x-i\epsilon),,\hskip 22.99988ptG^{+}=G(x+i\epsilon)\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG^{0}=G(\lambda-i\eta)\,.

(C.50)

The map
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
is simply the scalar cavity map applied to each component, while the weight
w_{s}
is built from the relevant Bethe phase contribution to the determinant. For sparse symmetric matrices these are site and edge terms; for diluted Wishart matrices they are variable, factor, and edge terms, and the message tuple contains both variable and factor components. Thus the formal structure of the weighted algorithm does not change from one problem to another.

We conclude with a few remarks on convergence and numerical practice. Since the appendix is about algorithms, it is useful to state explicitly what one should monitor. First, the observables of interest should stabilize as the population size
M
is increased. Second, at fixed
M
, the running average of the measured observable should become stationary after a burn-in time. Third, when the messages are complex Green functions with the lower-half-plane convention
z=\lambda-i\epsilon
, one should monitor that

{\rm Im}G^{(\alpha)}>0

(C.51)

for all population members, up to numerical roundoff. Fourth, independent runs with different random seeds should agree within Monte Carlo errors. Finally, the observables obtained from population dynamics should agree with the corresponding sum rules and dense-limit checks derived elsewhere in the notes.

The main lesson of this appendix is therefore straightforward. Population dynamics is not an ad hoc numerical recipe. It is the Monte Carlo implementation of a distributional fixed-point equation. The empirical population (
C.2
) approximates the unknown cavity law, the local map (
C.7
) generates one draw from the fixed-point operator, and the weighted extension (
C.42
)–(
C.47
) implements the large-deviation and conditioning problems. Once this generic structure is understood, all the specific algorithms of these lecture notes are just concrete realizations of the same idea.

Appendix D
Replica-symmetric saddle-point derivations

In the main text we emphasized that the cavity equations and the replica equations are two descriptions of the same finite-connectivity mean-field structure. The purpose of this appendix is to show this explicitly at the level of the saddle point. We will derive the replica-symmetric saddle-point equations for the simplest sparse symmetric ensemble, and then show how the same derivation extends to the replicated generating functions used for spectral-count large deviations and conditioned spectral densities. The derivation follows the general strategy introduced in the early replica treatments of sparse random matrices
[
26
,
76
,
15
]
, but is written here in the notation of these lecture notes and in a way that makes the connection with the cavity distributions transparent
[
39
,
83
]
.

To keep the algebra readable, we first consider a Poissonian sparse symmetric ensemble. Let

A_{ij}=D_{i}\delta_{ij}+C_{ij}J_{ij}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptA_{ij}=A_{ji}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptC_{ii}=0\,,

(D.1)

where, for
i<j
,

{\rm Prob}(C_{ij}=1)=\frac{c}{N}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Prob}(C_{ij}=0)=1-\frac{c}{N}\,,

(D.2)

the edge weights
J_{ij}
are independent with distribution
p_{J}(J)
, and the diagonal terms
D_{i}
are independent with distribution
p_{D}(D)
. The spectral density is obtained from the Edwards–Jones partition function

Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)=\int\left[\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{du_{i}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm T}(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})\boldsymbol{u}\right]\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz=\lambda-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon>0\,,

(D.3)

through

\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)}=-\frac{2}{\pi N}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\overline{\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda-i\epsilon)}\,.

(D.4)

The quenched logarithm is handled formally through the thermodynamic replica relation

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\overline{\log Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)}=\lim_{n\to 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{nN}\log\overline{[Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)]^{n}}\,.

(D.5)

We therefore begin with integer
n\geq 1
and compute
\overline{[Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)]^{n}}
. Introducing replicated variables

\underline{u}_{i}=(u_{i}^{1},\ldots,u_{i}^{n})\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\underline{u}_{i}^{\,2}=\sum_{a=1}^{n}(u_{i}^{a})^{2}\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\underline{u}_{i}\cdot\underline{u}_{j}=\sum_{a=1}^{n}u_{i}^{a}u_{j}^{a}\,,

(D.6)

we may write

\displaystyle[Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)]^{n}

\displaystyle=\int\left[\prod_{i=1}^{N}\prod_{a=1}^{n}\frac{du_{i}^{a}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\exp\Bigg[-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(z-D_{i})\underline{u}_{i}^{2}+i\sum_{i<j}C_{ij}J_{ij}\underline{u}_{i}\cdot\underline{u}_{j}\Bigg]\,.

(D.7)

The disorder average over the graph and the weights factorizes over pairs
i<j
. Since

\overline{e^{iC_{ij}J_{ij}\underline{u}_{i}\cdot\underline{u}_{j}}}=1-\frac{c}{N}+\frac{c}{N}\int dJp_{J}(J)e^{iJ\underline{u}_{i}\cdot\underline{u}_{j}}\,,

(D.8)

we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{[Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)]^{n}}

\displaystyle=\int\left[\prod_{i=1}^{N}\prod_{a=1}^{n}\frac{du_{i}^{a}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\prod_{i=1}^{N}\left[\int dD_{i}p_{D}(D_{i})e^{-\frac{i}{2}(z-D_{i})\underline{u}_{i}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\prod_{i<j}\left[1-\frac{c}{N}+\frac{c}{N}\int dJp_{J}(J)e^{iJ\underline{u}_{i}\cdot\underline{u}_{j}}\right]\,.

(D.9)

The product over pairs is of the standard sparse form
1+O(N^{-1})
. Writing

F(\underline{u}_{i},\underline{u}_{j})=\int dJp_{J}(J)e^{iJ\underline{u}_{i}\cdot\underline{u}_{j}}\,,

(D.10)

we have

\prod_{i<j}\left[1+\frac{c}{N}\left(F(\underline{u}_{i},\underline{u}_{j})-1\right)\right]=\exp\left[\frac{c}{2N}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\left(F(\underline{u}_{i},\underline{u}_{j})-1\right)+O(1)\right]\,.

(D.11)

The
O(1)
term is negligible in the large-
N
saddle-point analysis, since the dominant contribution to
\log\overline{Z^{n}}
is of order
N
.

At this point the interaction between different sites enters only through the empirical distribution of replicated vectors. We therefore introduce the order parameter

\varrho(\underline{u})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta(\underline{u}-\underline{u}_{i})\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\int d\underline{u}\varrho(\underline{u})=1\,,

(D.12)

where

d\underline{u}=\prod_{a=1}^{n}du^{a}\,.

(D.13)

To enforce (
D.12
), we insert the functional identity

1=\int\mathcal{D}\varrho\mathcal{D}\hat{\varrho}\exp\left[-iN\int d\underline{u}\hat{\varrho}(\underline{u})\varrho(\underline{u})+i\sum_{i=1}^{N}\hat{\varrho}(\underline{u}_{i})\right]\,.

(D.14)

Substituting (
D.14
) into (
D.9
), the integrals over the replicated site variables factorize. After straightforward rearrangement, we obtain

\overline{[Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z)]^{n}}=\int\mathcal{D}\varrho\mathcal{D}\hat{\varrho}\exp\left[NS_{n}[\varrho,\hat{\varrho}]\right]\,,

(D.15)

with action

\displaystyle S_{n}[\varrho,\hat{\varrho}]

\displaystyle=-i\int d\underline{u}\hat{\varrho}(\underline{u})\varrho(\underline{u})+\frac{c}{2}\int d\underline{u}d\underline{v}\varrho(\underline{u})\varrho(\underline{v})\left[F(\underline{u},\underline{v})-1\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 11.49994pt+\log\int dDp_{D}(D)\int d\underline{u}\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}(z-D)\underline{u}^{2}+i\hat{\varrho}(\underline{u})\right]\,.

(D.16)

The large-
N
limit is now obtained by a saddle point. Varying with respect to
\hat{\varrho}(\underline{u})
gives

\varrho(\underline{u})=\frac{\int dDp_{D}(D)\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}(z-D)\underline{u}^{2}+i\hat{\varrho}(\underline{u})\right]}{\int dDp_{D}(D)\int d\underline{w}\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}(z-D)\underline{w}^{2}+i\hat{\varrho}(\underline{w})\right]}\,.

(D.17)

Varying with respect to
\varrho(\underline{u})
gives

i\hat{\varrho}(\underline{u})=c\int d\underline{v}\varrho(\underline{v})\left[F(\underline{u},\underline{v})-1\right]\,.

(D.18)

Substituting (
D.18
) into (
D.17
), we obtain a single functional saddle-point equation:

\varrho(\underline{u})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}_{n}}\int dDp_{D}(D)\exp\left[-\frac{i}{2}(z-D)\underline{u}^{2}+c\int d\underline{v}\varrho(\underline{v})\left(F(\underline{u},\underline{v})-1\right)\right]\,,

(D.19)

where
\mathcal{N}_{n}
is the normalization that enforces
\int d\underline{u}\,\varrho(\underline{u})=1
.

Equation (
D.19
) is exact at the replica-symmetric saddle-point level, but it is still an equation for a function of an
n
-component replica vector. The simplification comes from the Gaussian structure of the original problem. Since the replicated variables entered through a Gaussian integral, and since the cavity solution of the main text uses Gaussian messages, we look for a replica-symmetric superposition of identical Gaussians in replica space. We therefore introduce the normalized Gaussian

\phi(\underline{u}|\omega)=\prod_{a=1}^{n}\left(\frac{\omega}{2\pi}\right)^{1/2}\exp\left[-\frac{\omega}{2}(u^{a})^{2}\right]\,,\hskip 22.99988pt{\rm Re}\omega>0\,,

(D.20)

and make the ansatz

\varrho(\underline{u})=\int d\omega\pi_{n}(\omega)\phi(\underline{u}|\omega)\,,

(D.21)

with

\int d\omega\,\pi_{n}(\omega)=1\,.

(D.22)

Replica symmetry means that all replicas are statistically equivalent, so the Gaussian factor (
D.20
) depends only on the same inverse variance
\omega
in each replica direction.

We now evaluate the interaction term in (
D.19
). Using (
D.10
) and (
D.21
),

\displaystyle\int d\underline{v}\varrho(\underline{v})F(\underline{u},\underline{v})

\displaystyle=\int dJp_{J}(J)\int d\omega\pi_{n}(\omega)\int d\underline{v}\phi(\underline{v}|\omega)e^{iJ\underline{u}\cdot\underline{v}}\,.

(D.23)

The integral over
\underline{v}
factorizes over replicas. For one replica,

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dv\left(\frac{\omega}{2\pi}\right)^{1/2}\exp\left[-\frac{\omega}{2}v^{2}+iJuv\right]=\exp\left[-\frac{J^{2}}{2\omega}u^{2}\right]\,.

(D.24)

Hence

\int d\underline{v}\phi(\underline{v}|\omega)e^{iJ\underline{u}\cdot\underline{v}}=\exp\left[-\frac{J^{2}}{2\omega}\underline{u}^{2}\right]\,,

(D.25)

and therefore

\int d\underline{v}\varrho(\underline{v})\left[F(\underline{u},\underline{v})-1\right]=\int d\omega\pi_{n}(\omega)\int dJp_{J}(J)\left[e^{-J^{2}\underline{u}^{2}/(2\omega)}-1\right]\,.

(D.26)

Substituting (
D.26
) into (
D.19
), we obtain

\displaystyle\varrho(\underline{u})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}_{n}}\int dDp_{D}(D)\exp\Bigg[-\frac{i}{2}(z-D)\underline{u}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 48.36958pt+c\int d\omega\pi_{n}(\omega)\int dJp_{J}(J)\left(e^{-J^{2}\underline{u}^{\,2}/(2\omega)}-1\right)\Bigg]\,.

(D.27)

At first sight (
D.27
) does not look Gaussian. The key step is to expand the exponential in Poisson form:

\displaystyle\exp\left[c\int d\omega\pi_{n}(\omega)\int dJp_{J}(J)\left(e^{-J^{2}\underline{u}^{2}/(2\omega)}-1\right)\right]

\displaystyle=e^{-c}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\left[\int d\omega\pi_{n}(\omega)\int dJp_{J}(J)e^{-J^{2}\underline{u}^{\,2}/(2\omega)}\right]^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-c}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\int\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}d\omega_{r}\pi_{n}(\omega_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\underline{u}^{\,2}\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{J_{r}^{2}}{\omega_{r}}\right]\,.

(D.28)

Substituting (
D.28
) into (
D.27
), we find

\displaystyle\varrho(\underline{u})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-c}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}d\omega_{r}\pi_{n}(\omega_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\exp\left[-\frac{\Omega}{2}\underline{u}^{\,2}\right]\,,

(D.29)

where

\Omega=i(z-D)+\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{J_{r}^{2}}{\omega_{r}}\,.

(D.30)

Equation (
D.29
) is the crucial step. The order parameter is again a superposition of Gaussians, so the Gaussian-mixture ansatz is self-consistent.

To read off the induced distribution of
\Omega
, note that

e^{-\frac{\Omega}{2}\underline{u}^{2}}=\left(\frac{2\pi}{\Omega}\right)^{n/2}\phi(\underline{u}|\Omega)\,.

(D.31)

Hence (
D.29
) has the same form as (
D.21
), with the induced mixture

\displaystyle\pi_{n}(\Omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-c}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}d\omega_{r}\pi_{n}(\omega_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\left(\frac{2\pi}{\Omega}\right)^{n/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\delta\left(\Omega-i(z-D)-\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{J_{r}^{2}}{\omega_{r}}\right)\,,

(D.32)

where
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
normalizes
\pi_{n}
. In the replica limit
n\to 0
,

\left(\frac{2\pi}{\Omega}\right)^{n/2}=1+O(n)\,,

(D.33)

so the saddle-point equation for the cavity inverse-variance distribution becomes

\displaystyle\pi(\Omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-c}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}d\omega_{r}\pi(\omega_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\!\left(\Omega-i(z-D)-\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{J_{r}^{2}}{\omega_{r}}\right)\,.

(D.34)

This is exactly the distributional cavity equation written in the inverse-variance variable

\omega=iG^{-1}\,.

(D.35)

Indeed, since the local Gaussian covariance of one replica is
\omega^{-1}
, while the Gaussian partition function (
D.3
) has inverse covariance
i(z\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{A})
, we have

\langle u_{i}^{2}\rangle=\frac{1}{\omega_{i}}=-iG_{ii}(z)\,,\hskip 22.99988ptG_{ii}(z)=\frac{i}{\omega_{i}}\,.

(D.36)

For a Poisson ensemble the cavity and site degree laws coincide, so the full-site inverse-variance distribution is the same as the cavity one. More generally, if
p_{k}
is the full degree law and
q_{k}
the excess-degree law, the message distribution
\pi
is generated by the analogue of (
D.34
) with
e^{-c}c^{k}/k!
replaced by
q_{k}
, while the site distribution uses the full degree law:

\displaystyle\pi_{\rm site}(\Omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}p_{k}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}d\omega_{r}\pi(\omega_{r})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]\delta\left(\Omega-i(z-D)-\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{J_{r}^{2}}{\omega_{r}}\right)\,.

(D.37)

Therefore the spectral density is

\overline{\rho_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\lambda)}=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\int d\Omega\pi_{\rm site}(\Omega)\frac{i}{\Omega}\,.

(D.38)

Equation (
D.38
) is exactly the cavity formula derived in the main text. This completes the explicit replica-symmetric derivation of the standard sparse-symmetric cavity equations.

It is useful to make the logic of the derivation explicit. The replica trick turned the quenched logarithm into an annealed replicated partition function. The functional order parameter (
D.12
) encoded the empirical law of replicated local variables. The Gaussian mixture (
D.21
) was the replica-symmetric ansatz appropriate to a Gaussian problem. The Poisson expansion (
D.28
) restored Gaussianity after disorder averaging. Finally, the inverse-variance distribution (
D.34
) coincided with the cavity-message distribution. This is why the replica and cavity methods agree in this setting.

The same derivation extends to tilted problems. We first treat the large deviations of the number of eigenvalues below a threshold
x
. Define

z_{-}=x-i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988ptz_{+}=x+i\epsilon\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\epsilon>0\,,

(D.39)

and the replicated generating function

\mathcal{Q}_{N}(n_{-},n_{+})=\overline{[Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{-})]^{n_{-}}[Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{+})]^{n_{+}}}\,.

(D.40)

For integer
n_{-},n_{+}\geq 1
, introduce two replica sectors

\underline{u}_{i}^{-}=(u_{i}^{-,1},\ldots,u_{i}^{-,n_{-}})\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\underline{u}_{i}^{+}=(u_{i}^{+,1},\ldots,u_{i}^{+,n_{+}})\,,

(D.41)

and define the joint order parameter

\varrho(\underline{u}^{-},\underline{u}^{+})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta(\underline{u}^{-}-\underline{u}_{i}^{-})\delta(\underline{u}^{+}-\underline{u}_{i}^{+})\,.

(D.42)

Repeating the same steps that led to (
D.16
), we obtain the two-sector saddle-point equation

\displaystyle\varrho(\underline{u}^{-},\underline{u}^{+})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}_{n_{-},n_{+}}}\int dDp_{D}(D)\exp\Bigg[-\frac{i}{2}(z_{-}-D)(\underline{u}^{-})^{2}-\frac{i}{2}(z_{+}-D)(\underline{u}^{+})^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+c\int d\underline{v}^{-}d\underline{v}^{+}\varrho(\underline{v}^{-},\underline{v}^{+})\left(e^{iJ(\underline{u}^{-}\cdot\underline{v}^{-}+\underline{u}^{+}\cdot\underline{v}^{+})}-1\right)_{J\text{-avg}}\Bigg]\,,

(D.43)

where

\left(e^{iJ(\underline{u}^{-}\cdot\underline{v}^{-}+\underline{u}^{+}\cdot\underline{v}^{+})}\right)_{J\text{-avg}}\equiv\int dJp_{J}(J)e^{iJ(\underline{u}^{-}\cdot\underline{v}^{-}+\underline{u}^{+}\cdot\underline{v}^{+})}\,.

(D.44)

Replica symmetry now means permutation symmetry within each sector separately. The Gaussian factors in the
z_{+}
sector are understood by analytic continuation of the same formulas, since the Edwards–Jones integral is directly convergent only for the lower-half-plane boundary value. We therefore use the product-Gaussian ansatz

\varrho(\underline{u}^{-},\underline{u}^{+})=\int d\omega^{-}d\omega^{+}\pi_{n_{-},n_{+}}(\omega^{-},\omega^{+})\phi(\underline{u}^{-}|\omega^{-})\phi(\underline{u}^{+}|\omega^{+})\,.

(D.45)

The Gaussian integral (
D.24
) factorizes independently in the two sectors, so

\displaystyle\int d\underline{v}^{-}d\underline{v}^{+}\phi(\underline{v}^{-}|\omega^{-})\phi(\underline{v}^{+}|\omega^{+})e^{iJ(\underline{u}^{-}\cdot\underline{v}^{-}+\underline{u}^{+}\cdot\underline{v}^{+})}

\displaystyle=\exp\left[-\frac{J^{2}}{2\omega^{-}}(\underline{u}^{-})^{2}-\frac{J^{2}}{2\omega^{+}}(\underline{u}^{+})^{2}\right]\,.

(D.46)

The same Poisson expansion as before then yields a Gaussian mixture again. Keeping the finite replica numbers
n_{-}
and
n_{+}
, the joint recursion is

\displaystyle\pi_{n_{-},n_{+}}({}^{-},{}^{+})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{n_{-},n_{+}}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-c}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}d\omega_{r}^{-}d\omega_{r}^{+}\pi_{n_{-},n_{+}}(\omega_{r}^{-},\omega_{r}^{+})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\left(\frac{2\pi}{{}^{-}}\right)^{n_{-}/2}\left(\frac{2\pi}{{}^{+}}\right)^{n_{+}/2}\delta\left({}^{-}-i(z_{-}-D)-\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{J_{r}^{2}}{\omega_{r}^{-}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\delta\left({}^{+}-i(z_{+}-D)-\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{J_{r}^{2}}{\omega_{r}^{+}}\right)\,.

(D.47)

In the strict
n_{-}\to 0
,
n_{+}\to 0
limit, the prefactor in (
D.47
) tends to one and the recursion reduces to the unweighted joint cavity law for the two boundary values
z_{-}
and
z_{+}
. For the finite-
s
index problem, however, the same prefactor is retained after the analytic continuation (
D.49
); it is the replica origin of the tilted or weighted population laws used in the main text.
This equation deserves emphasis. The two components are not independent even though the map factorizes componentwise. They are correlated because the same local disorder variables
D
and
J_{r}
appear in both delta constraints. This is exactly what is needed in the large-deviation problem: the distribution of the two resolvents at
z_{-}
and
z_{+}
is coupled through the same local graph environment.

The corresponding site law is

\displaystyle\pi_{{\rm site},n_{-},n_{+}}({}^{-},{}^{+})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}^{\rm site}_{n_{-},n_{+}}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}p_{k}\int dDp_{D}(D)\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}d\omega_{r}^{-}d\omega_{r}^{+}\pi_{n_{-},n_{+}}(\omega_{r}^{-},\omega_{r}^{+})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\left(\frac{2\pi}{{}^{-}}\right)^{n_{-}/2}\left(\frac{2\pi}{{}^{+}}\right)^{n_{+}/2}\delta\left({}^{-}-i(z_{-}-D)-\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{J_{r}^{2}}{\omega_{r}^{-}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\delta\left({}^{+}-i(z_{+}-D)-\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{J_{r}^{2}}{\omega_{r}^{+}}\right)\,.

(D.48)

After the analytic continuation

n_{-}=-\frac{s}{\pi i}\,,\hskip 22.99988ptn_{+}=\frac{s}{\pi i}\,,

(D.49)

the large-deviation theory of the index is recovered from the corresponding replica free energy, with the additive constant in the determinant-phase identity for
\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(x)
contributing the separate term
s
to the scaled cumulant-generating function. At the level of the saddle-point equation, however, the essential content is already visible in (
D.47
): the scalar replica-symmetric order parameter becomes a
joint
distribution of two inverse variances.

The conditioned spectral density requires one more sector. Introduce the probe spectral parameter

z_{0}=\lambda-i\eta\,,\hskip 22.99988pt\eta>0\,,

(D.50)

and consider

\mathcal{Q}_{N}(n_{-},n_{+},n_{0})=\overline{[Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{-})]^{n_{-}}[Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{+})]^{n_{+}}[Z_{\boldsymbol{A}}(z_{0})]^{n_{0}}}\,.

(D.51)

The order parameter is now

\varrho(\underline{u}^{-},\underline{u}^{+},\underline{u}^{0})\,,

(D.52)

and the replica-symmetric ansatz is

\varrho(\underline{u}^{-},\underline{u}^{+},\underline{u}^{0})=\int d\omega^{-}d\omega^{+}d\omega^{0}\pi_{n_{-},n_{+},n_{0}}(\omega^{-},\omega^{+},\omega^{0})\phi(\underline{u}^{-}|\omega^{-})\phi(\underline{u}^{+}|\omega^{+})\phi(\underline{u}^{0}|\omega^{0})\,.

(D.53)

Carrying through exactly the same steps, one obtains

\displaystyle\pi_{n_{-},n_{+},n_{0}}({}^{-},{}^{+},{}^{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{n_{-},n_{+},n_{0}}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-c}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\int dDp_{D}(D)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\left[\prod_{r=1}^{k}d\omega_{r}^{-}d\omega_{r}^{+}d\omega_{r}^{0}\pi_{n_{-},n_{+},n_{0}}(\omega_{r}^{-},\omega_{r}^{+},\omega_{r}^{0})dJ_{r}p_{J}(J_{r})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\left(\frac{2\pi}{{}^{-}}\right)^{n_{-}/2}\left(\frac{2\pi}{{}^{+}}\right)^{n_{+}/2}\left(\frac{2\pi}{{}^{0}}\right)^{n_{0}/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\delta\left({}^{-}-i(z_{-}-D)-\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{J_{r}^{2}}{\omega_{r}^{-}}\right)\delta\left({}^{+}-i(z_{+}-D)-\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{J_{r}^{2}}{\omega_{r}^{+}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\delta\left({}^{0}-i(z_{0}-D)-\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{J_{r}^{2}}{\omega_{r}^{0}}\right)\,.

(D.54)

In the conditioned-density calculation one eventually takes
n_{0}\to 0
, so the probe sector measures the local Green function without changing the tilted ensemble. The two threshold sectors, however, retain the finite analytic continuation associated with the conditioning field. The conditioned density is then obtained from the site law associated with (
D.54
) by taking the imaginary part of the probe component:

\rho_{x}(\lambda|k(s))=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}{\rm Im}\int d{}^{-}d{}^{+}d{}^{0}\pi_{\rm site}^{(s)}({}^{-},{}^{+},{}^{0})\frac{i}{{}^{0}}\,.

(D.55)

Once again, the structure matches the cavity picture exactly. The replica-symmetric saddle point is a joint law of the inverse variances at all spectral parameters involved in the observable.

Two final remarks are worth making. First, the derivation above was presented for the Poissonian sparse symmetric ensemble because it makes the algebra as transparent as possible. For arbitrary degree distributions or ensembles with topological constraints, one introduces degree- or type-conditioned order parameters. The Gaussian replica-symmetric ansatz then yields the corresponding degree- or type-conditioned cavity laws discussed in the main text. Second, for bipartite ensembles such as diluted Wishart matrices, one needs two replicated order parameters, one on the variable side and one on the factor side, but the same logic applies: the Gaussian replica-symmetric ansatz turns the replicated saddle-point problem into the same recursive distributional equations that follow from the cavity method.

The main point of this appendix can therefore be summarized succinctly. The replica-symmetric saddle-point derivation does not produce a different theory from the cavity method. It produces the same message variables and distributional structure, but from the replicated disorder average rather than from local tree recursions. In the simple spectral-density problem one obtains the single distribution (
D.34
); in the index problem one obtains the two-sector law (
D.47
); and in the conditioned-density problem one obtains the three-sector law (
D.54
). The finite-replica normalization factors are the replica origin of the local tilted weights used in the large-deviation and conditioned-density algorithms; the corresponding scaled cumulant-generating functions are obtained from the associated Bethe free-energy combinations. This is the precise technical sense in which the replica and cavity approaches coincide in the finite-connectivity problems studied in these notes.
